One Inclosure. 4 | No. 1. 
Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston —(Received January 20.) 
(No. 1.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 3, 1851. 
—— ) A DESPATCH from Mr. Bennett, Acting-Consul at Varna, informs me, 
under date of the 25th ultimo, that the number of remaining refugees, removed 
4 from Shumla to Varna, and embarked at the latter place for Copeanyneplss 
| under the superintendence of an Ottoman Commissioner, amounts to 296 
! individuals, of whom 230 are Poles, and 66 Hungarians. There is still a small 
amount of the refugees at Varna, about 30 Hungarians and a few Poles, who, 
having previously received a gratuity from the Porte, are considered to have no 
further title to assistance from the same quarter. I believe, however, that these 
‘ndividuals are able to obtain a livelihood in the country, 
= T have, &c.. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING. 
| ; E 
F 4 
No.2. 
| Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—(Received January 20.) 
i] (No. 2.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 3, 1851. 
THE Committee of Armenian Protestants, desirous of expressing their 
cordial thanks for the firman which, as your Lordship is aware, has been lately 
| procured for them by this Embassy, have sent me an address, which | take the 
a | present opportunity of forwarding in translation herewith. 1 venture to hope 
iF that your Lordship wil see in the glowing expression af their acknowledgments 
A to me, a natural effect of the sentiments which they are bound by every tie of 
ton sympathy and gratitude to entertain not only towards their own Sovereign, but 
= | more particularly towards Her aout the Queen and that Government of 
ee ia aS be pba? is so distinguish be member, 
; e original address is written in the Armenian language, and deposited in 
a the archives of the Embassy. am 
Me | 4 I have, &e. 
ms 3 ) (Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Inclosure in No. 2. 
Address from the Armeman Protestants to Sir Stratford Canning. 


(Translation.) 
Sir, 

WE, the Evangelical Christian subjects of the Sublime Ottoman Porte, 
through the infinite and unspeakable mercy of God, and by the special solicitude 
and activity of your Excellency, having been acknowledged by our compassionate 
and benevolent Sovereign as faithful and obedient subjects, and having obtained 
the favour of enjoying the individual and social privileges of good citizens under 
his protecting care, are constrained at the present time to make known to your 
Excellency with unfeigned Christian love and respect the deep and never-failing 
eratitude of our hearts. 

We cannot conceal from you the strong and lasting emotions excited in all 
our hearts, when, in a full assembly of our community, one of our reverend 
presbyters related to us your Excellency’s very kind reception of the thirteen 
individuals who waited upon you; and especially when we heard those deeply 
affecting and soul-stirring words of counsel and exhortation, designed for our 
whole community, which flowed from your Excellency’s lips. 

The many and widely known and appreciated acts of kindness of which 
your Excellency, as the Representative of Her Royal Majesty, has been the 
author, have spread your renown through the four quarters of the globe, and 
have justly made it conspicuous on the pages of civil history- But your sincere 
Christian love, and benevolent and unwearied efforts—worthy of everlasting 
remembrance—in behalf of freedom of conscience, have immortalized your 
illustrious naine, Yes, and it will not only be written indelibly in the church 
histories of the nineteenth century, but also engraven in imperishable characters 
on the hearts of ourselves and of our children. | 

Is it possible that we can ever for a moment forget the multiform acts of 
kindness you have so constantly shown to us in our humiliation during several 
years past, without justly exposing ourselves to the opprobrium of ingratitude ? 
When, in consequence of our adherence to the simple doctrines of the gospel, 
we fell under the power of civil and spiritual oppression, being driven from our 
houses and shops, and subjected to temporal punishments and persecutions, ant 
vexed on every side, and they strenuously demanded of us that, against our own 
consciences, we should obey the false and erruneous doctrines of human 
tradition; and, not content with this, they even ventured to invent new 
confessions of faith, unknown to the ancient fathers, to which they commanded 
us to subscribe; in one word, when, being in this miserable condition, we could 
by no means find protection for our rights in any quarter, and to whichever side 
we turned our eyes, and to whomsoever we looked for help, we were repelled as 
turbulent sectarians and rebels—then your Excellency kindly interposed for our 
relief, And if, through the good and wise Providence of God, you had not then 
extended to us your love and sympathy, and had not made known to the Porte 
the oppressions and persecutions we were unjustly suffering, and had not shown 
an interest in our case, humanly speaking our very names must long ere this 
have been blotted from the earth, But, instead of this, behold! we are 
recognised as an Evangelical church and people, and enjoy all the liberties and

Sir, Foreign Office, January 93, 1851. 
WITH reference to your despatch No. 2 of the 3rd instant, inclosing an 
address from the Protestant Armenians, I have to state to your Excellency that 
it is very pleasing to Her Majesty’s Government, and must be very gratifying to 
you, to receive from these poor people such fervent expressions of their deep 
thankfulness for the arrangement which, through your Exeellency’s exertions, 
the Porte has made in their favour ; and it is satisfactory to see, that in making 
their acknowledgments towards their Christian protectors, they have not 
forgotten their loyalty to their own Sovereign. 
Iam, &e.

Le fond de la mission du Pacha consiste 4 vérifier l'exécution effective et 
d'une maniére conforme aux intentions pleines d’équité de Sa Majesté le Sultan, 
et aux principes consacrées par le Tanzimati Khairivé, de chaque chose dans les 
endroits qu'il visitera; & réprimer tout ce qui est contraire a ces principes ; a 
donner les ordres nécessaires pour la suppression des abus; et a prendre des 
renseignements sur toutes choses, A cet. effet, et pourqu’il ait 4 se conformer 4 
toutes les dispositions qu’ils renferment, on lui a donné des eopies des réglements 
établis sous la régne actuel comme une marque visible de la justice et de l'équité 
qui le caractérisent, afin qu’au besoin il puisse y recourir. 

En partant d'ici le Pacha réglera son itinéraire sur In neécessité et Vimpor- 
tance des affaires des endroits qu'il aura h visiter; et arrivé dans un district, 
dans un village, il s'enquérira de l'état de toute chose et de la conduite des auto- 
rités—c’ested-dire, il s'informera au juste si, contraire au Tanzimat, on y commet 
deg assassinate, des actes de brigandage sur les grands chemins, on commet des 
pillages, on emploie la torture, on fait travailler des gens 4 la corvée, on sé laisse 
corrompre par des présents, on prend de |'argent 4 titre d’amende, on donne la 
bastonnade, ou l'on se permet d'autres choses semblables qui sont illégales et 
reprouvées; si l'on répartit les impdts de maniere de prendre plus d’argent que 
ce qui est exigible d’aprés les impdts réguliérement éta lis; si dans la perception 
des dimes et des droits, et sur d'autres points, il se commet des injustices; si les 
juges ont soin de bien administrer la justice dans l'application des lois saintes, et 
s‘ils se comportent bien, ‘Tels sont les points sur lesquels le Pacha aura soin de 
se procurer les renseignements les plus exacts. 

Lorsque le Pacha sera arrivé A une province qui est le centre des affaires, ou 
dans un district, il y convoquera une assemblée géntrale, composée d'hommes 
dont la présence est nécessaire ; 1h il sera fuit lecture du firman qui lui contére la 
mission dont il est chargé; ct apres que les points compris dans sa mission auront 
été publiés, il fera cesser tout ce qui sera trouve etre contraire i la volonté 
souveraine ; et ceux qui se trouveront avoir eu la hardiesse de commettre des 
choses contraires & la volonté souveraine, qui qu’ils puissent étre, une fois les 
fuits constatés, soit par des renseignements pris sur la route, soit par des infor- 
mations obtenues dans le chef lieu de la province ou du district, seront jugés 
dans le Conseil Municipal. et ils auront, eux aussi, 4 apposer leurs cachets sur 
les Mazbatas qui seront rédigés constatant leurs crimes. 5i les hommes de cette 
catégorie sont des gens du commerce, ils seront mis en prison. Si ce sont des 
Mudirs de districts, ou des membres du Conseil, et, par conséquent, comme tels, 
des employes inféricurs, ils seront: immédiatement révoqués et remplacés par 
Wautres personnes convenables, ad inferim, au choix du Conseil, et seront, 
suivant leur qualité, ou emprisonnés ou détenus; les Mazbatas qui les coneernent 
seront envoyés ici, pour que l’on procéde Ales punir suivant le Code Pénal, Et, 
si de pareilles choses sont arrivées dans un endroit, est-ce par la négligence et 
Vinsouciance du Gouverneur de cet endroit, quia manqué de faire exécuter effec- 
tivement les rdglements existants et les ordres donnés, ou bien prennent-elles leurs 
source dans quelque autre cause; qui sont parmi les autoriles, les hommes qui 
administrent bien le pays, et ceux qui, au contraire, 86 comportent mal? C'est 
ce que le Pacha a ordre de vérifier aussi, ouvertement et sous main, et de la 
faire savoir A la Sublime Porte séparément. Tl aura, en méme temps, 4 prendre 
telles mesures qu'il jugera nécessaires pour laccomplissement des principes justes 
et équitables qui ont dicté le Tanzimati Khairiyé, et pour la prospérité a laquelle 
on vise de l'Empire et de la nation, en adoptant, pour obtenir ces résultats, les 
mesures que chaque endroit réclame, d’aprés la position et l'état dans lequel il se 
trouve actucllement. On dit du mal de la plupart des Medjlis (Conseils Mum- 
cipaux); il faut aussi en modifier et réformer l'organisation. Les points sus- 
énoncés ont été recommandés aussi et laissés d la droiture et & ln sagacité qui 
distinguent Ismet Pacha, 

Le paiement convenable des dépenses dau Gouvernement Ottoman dépend 
de la régularité de l'euvoi des revenus de Empire. Or, cette régularité est 
un devoir des autorités dang les provinces, qui ne doivent épargner ni efforts

des fonds du Gouvernement, Ainsi le Pacha a des ordres de prendre des infor- 
mations, et d’agir, relativement aux déniers publics, de la_maniére suivante. Il 
fora examiner dans les Conseils les comptes de l'administrateur des fonds du 
trésor, et du Mudir du Caza (district), afin que l'on voie combien d'argent prove- 
nant des revenus élablis a été recouvré, et combien il en reste @ recouyrer; et 
s'il y a des becaya (c'est-d-dire, des arrérages) pourquoi ils n'ont pas été 
recouvrés. Parmi les sommes arriérées combien sont-elles comme perdues et 
combien sont solides et recouvrables; qui sont ceux qui retiennent les deniers 
du Gouvernement, et s’en servent pour faire le commerce ; les sommes dargent 
nouvellement dies, et les sommes arriérées qu'on veut faire considérer comme 
non recouvrées ; qui les a, sont-ce les habitants, les notables, ou d'autres indi- 
yidus? voilA ce qu'il faut que le Pacha découvre; et ceux qui se trouvent avoir 
entre leurs mains de V'argent qui revient au Tyésor Tmpérial, qui qu’tls putssent 
atre, s'ils ont de l’argent comptant on prendra tout de snite en argent comptant 
ce qui revient au Gouvernement; s‘ils n'en ont pas, on ie prendra, d'aprés la 
régle établie, de leurs garants, et si cela n'est pas faisable, on prendra Vargent du 
Gouvernement du produit de leurs biens que Von fera vendre a leur juste valeur, 
et on le fera verser sans aneun délai dans la caisse du Trésor Imperial. 57 
conste que ceux qui font le commerce avec argent du ‘Trésor, ou qui sont devenus

Jusqu’é présent nous avons tant et tant de fois envoyé les ordres nécessaires et

Le réglement qui concerne emploi des soldats de police dans les pays hors 
de Constantinople, et qui a été fait dans le but salutaire de maintenir la tran- 
quillité de la population et la sécurité publique, nest pas observe convenable- 
ment; et c'est pour cela que des désordves de nature i compromettre la tran-

la Porte a été attaquée plus d'une ig A Ismid, 2 Aidin, et dans d'autres endroits 5

énergiques 4 cet gard. 

La Porte, il est vrai, est apres A compléter les réglements touchant la police 
dans les Provinces ; mais pour parvenir au but que l'on désire obtenir, il faut 
qu'au préalable nous eachions certaines choses sur lesquelles il faut. prendre des 
informations dans les Provinces, 

En effet, d’apres ce qu’on entend dire, les autorités hors de la capitale se 
permettent certains abus et certaines fraudes, en ce qui concerne le service de la 
police. Par exemple, tout le nombre établi des soldats de police dans une 
province, dans un andjak, ou dans un district, n’est pas employé, dans beau- 
coup d’endroits. Indépendamment de cela, parmi les hommes que les Medjlis, 
les en eroire, ont employés comme soldats de police, ily ena qui font partie 
des gens attaches au service particulier du Gouverneur de lieu, et qui retirent, 
sans aucun profit pour le public, ln paye de soldats de police. Il ya aussi des 
hommes qui passent pour hommes de police, et qui ne recoivent toutefois 
nucune paye. C'est pour cela que lorsque la Sublime Porte donne quelquetois 
des ordres pour le maintien de la stireté qui est & désirer, on lui répond que 
le nombre des soldats établi n’est pas suffisant, et que, par consequent, il faut 
'augimenter. 

En conséquence de tout ce qui a été dit plus haut, il est tout naturel que la 
Sublime Porte preune des renseignements positifs sur la vérite des fuits, avant

d'un état qui fair voir le nombre de soldats de chaque endroit,

No. 10. 


Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—(Received February 7.) 
(No. 28.) 

My Lord, Constantinople, January 22, 1851. 

I HAVE communicated to the Grand Vizier, as well as to Aali Pasha, the 
contents of your Lordship’s instruction No, 319, relative to the late deplorable 
outrages committed in Bulgaria and Syria, by a part of the Mussulman popu- 
lation, at the expense of their Christian fellow-subj ects. I made the communi- 
cation at a personal interview with each of these Ministers, and while I readily 
udmitted that much had been done to restrain and to punish the offenders at 
Aleppo, I repeated my former representations with respect to the Porte’s remiss- 
ness elsewhere, and expressed a hope that justice would still be dealt out in full 
measure both to the guilty and to their surviving victims. An extract of your 
Lordship’s despatch has since been placed in Aali Pasha’s hands, and 1 trust 
that it will find its way to the Sultan. 

The answer which 1 received from the two Ministers was in substance 
nearly the same. I was assured that the Porte intended sincerely to redeem its 
pled#e; that the pending investigations would be carried birdneh to a practical 
and efficient result; that the leading criminals would be punished severely on 
conviction ; and that the deficiency of restored property would be made up by 
a fair compensation, 

The Minister for Foreign Affairs informed me that the proceedings of the 
Porte’s Commissioner at Widdin, however tardy they might appear, were highly 
satisfactory, and ultimately calculated to secure the ends of justice. His Excel- 
lency added, that those among the detected offenders at Aleppo who were 
possessed of effects, would. be compelled to answer for the pillaged property not 
restored, and that any remaining deficiency would be supplied, more or less, by 
the Government. 1 was tot sorry to perceive a reluctance in his mind to make 
the whole city responsible for the losses sustained by the Christian part of its 
population; but upon my observing that it might have a salutary effect tor the - 
future to declare the principle of a gencral responsibility, and to admit an 
exception in favour of those who were known to have stood by the Sultan's 
authority, and to have assisted in recovering the pillaged property, he appeared 
to be pleased with the idea, and 1am inclined to think that the matter will be 
regulated accordingly. 

I may take this opportunity to add, on the authority of a confidential 
communication from the same quarter, that orders have been sent to Salonica, 
calculated to bring home justice to the doors of those functionaries who, appear 
to have abetted the horrible atrocities lately perpetrated by a band of robbers 
and assassins, on the borders of the Province which bears that name. 

The latest accounts which | have received from Mr. Werry describe the 
city of Aleppo as continuing in a state of almost unbroken tranquillity, and the 
Turkish local authorities, with Mehemet Pasha at their head, as strenuously 
eiployed in arresting the disaffected, and collecting the plundered property, 
though with far better success as to the criminals than as to their plunder. 

Upon the whole, my Lord, though much remains to be done for example 
and compensation, and though there is some apprehension among the Christians 
of future vengeance, [ venture to hope that whether we look to the maintenance 
of good order, to the progress of improvement, or to the protection of the Rayah 
population, the late atrocities will not leave a fruitless or uninstructive page in 
the records of this country. 

1 have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Kho. T1. 
Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—( Recetved March 3.) 


(No. 43.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, February 7, 1851. 

I OBSERVE with concern that, notwithstanding the Porte’s assurances, 
but little progress is made in the proceedings instituted at Widdin, Aleppo, and 
Damascus, for the professed purpose of bringing to justice the principal actors in 
the late atrocious massacres, and of indemnifying the Christian sufferers. 

At Aleppo indeed a signal punishment has been inflicted on the rebellious 
quarter of that town by the Sultan’s forces ; and the principal instigators of the 
revolt have been either sent into exile, or brought up to the capital, and there 
detained or punished. ‘The inquiries preparatory to indemnification appear, 
however, to meet with much obstruction, caused, no doubt, in part, by the diffi- 
culty of finding the scattered plunder, and by the exaggerated pretensions of the 
claimants, but also attributable to the sympathies of Islamism, and the unwilling- 
ness of the Turkish authorities to admit the fall amount of liability. 

The Special Commission at Widdin, far from sending the Mussulman male- 
factors to punishment, bas hitherto, as far as 1 know, confined its decisions to 
the condemnation of some Christians charged with safe originated the ¢lis- 
orders in Bulgaria, and to the imposition of a fine on the district to which they 
belong. This is not an auspicious beginning, though it may prove in the eud to 
be a prelude to that more substantial justice which the Porte has pledged itself 
to give, and which Christendom is entitled to expect. The reserve and manage- 
ment practised by the Turkish Ministers throughout the course af this long- 
pending investigation render its final issue uncertain. The present appearances 
are by no means satisfactory. 

With respect to Maloula, Emin Pasha maintains that the Christiana were 
in fault, and that the conduct of the Turkish soldiery was irreproachuble. Your 
Lordship need not be told that the Porte accepts his version of the matler with 
unlimited confidence. On the other hand, Mr. Wood, since his return to 
Damascus, corroborates the charge of wanton pillage and massacre as made 
originally by the Christian population of Maloula, and supported by Mr, Calvert 
and Colonel Rose in their respective correspondence. 

More has been done on the side of Salonica, where the crimes committed, 
however atrocious in their character, were of little extent, and traceable to ban- 
ditti, important only as appearing to be connected with some of the local author 
ties. But it is worthy of remark that even there only four of the criminals have 
been hitherto arrested, and none of them, | believe, have as yet been brought to 
condign punishment. 

Jeanwhile, newspaper writers in the pay of Government trumpet forth the 
praises of Ottoman justice, and the pee of France and Belgium ring with the 
echoes of their flourishes. It would seem that at Brussels, where the Porte’s 
diplomacy is conducted by a Belgian, the hoax has been carried to the point of 
striking a medal in honour of the Sultan’s triumph at Aleppo. 

Exaggerated eulogies, if they spring from any independent quarter, and 
have any real meaning but that of flattery, are, after all, poor compliments to 
the Porte’s capacity for realizing its pretensions to the confidence of Europe 
and the affections of its Christian subjects. They are mischievous also in their 
effect. They blind the Sultan to the evils of misgovernment; they encourage 
the indolence, if not the more culpable vices, of authority; and they mislead 
that pubis opinion which is the best security for improvement, 

Neither the Sultan's Christian subjects, nor the partisans of that power to 
whieh they look, are duped by such illusions. The former are more an ever 
alive to the vexations which they are still called upon to endure, and the latter 
are always on the watch for acts of injustice favourable to the extension ofa 
dangerous influence. I may fairly illustrate these remarks by bringing to your 
Lordship's notice two documents which have reached me within the lust few 
days. One of them isa petition to the Grand Vizier from the spiritual heads 
of the several Christian communities at Aleppo. The other is a despatch 
addressed to me by Her Majesty’s Vice-Consul at Scutari, A copy of the latter 
and translation of the former are inclosed herewith, Goth of them announce 


15 


the approach of Rayah deputations, empowered to supplicate for relief which it 
has been found impossible to obtain from the local authorities. 

In addition to these circumstances I am assured that a strong feeling of 
discontent prevails in Bulgaria, and that if the existing disappointment were to 
be confirmed by the final decisions of the Commission at Widdin, there would 
be danger of a popular movement extending throughout the province, and 


perhaps even affecting the Frente of Servia. 
5 


1 need not observe to your Lordship that the whole of this subject deserves 
an attentive consideration from its connection with the prospects of real improve- 
ment in this country, and with the general interests of Europe. 

[ have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING. 


Inclosure | in No. 11. 
Memorial of the Aleppo Christians to the Grand Vizier. 


THE late occurrences at Aleppo having awakened His Gracious Majesty's 
compassion towards his faithful subjects, he was pleased to ordain that the 
aggressors should be punished, and that the plundered property should be 
restored in full to the owners; and his Excellency Mehemed Pasha gave 
stringent orders to that effect on his arrival. A vizirial letter was subsequently 
received reiterating those orders, and we offered up our prayers, &e. 

If the benevolent intentions of His Majesty had been carried into effect, 
comfort and security would have been restored to his faithful subjects. That, 
however, has not been the case. Besides our churches and our metropolitan 
residence burnt down in the space of a few hours, only 74 per cent. of the 
27,000 purses’ worth of plundered property has been recovered. We have, 
therefore, decided on repairing ourselves to the capital, in order to throw 
ourselves at the foot of the Throne, and submit our wretched condition in 
person to His Imperial Majesty, and we beg that your Highness will extend 
towards us your charitable protection and support. 


Inclogure 2 in No, 11. 


Extract of a Report from the British Vice-Consul at Scutari, dated 
January 14, 1851, 


A FEW days ago the Pasha summoned the Chiefs of the Christian popula- 
tion, to inform them that a firman had arrived enjoining the Rayahs and their 
Spiritual Chief to make lists of all the Christians of Scutari who were to pay 
taxes, 

They accordingly drew up the lists and presented them, but it turned out 
that the frinan directed the payment of 110,000 piastres, to be divided into two 
classes among the Rayahs, they having expected that the firman would exempt 
them from baratch. Pate 

It was then that the Christians, alarmed at the equivocation, declared, in a 
public meeting, that they were not willingly prepared to pay an additional sum ; 
that the number of Christians did not suffice to make up the deficiency. They 
were aware the Sultan’s intentions were to lessen, and not to inerease, the 
burdens of the Rayahs. If it was intended to extort: the sum by force, they 
could no longer oppose, provided the Pasha gave a formal receipt for the 
amount. 

The Rayahs can hardly believe it to be the Sultan's intention that 3,000 
adult Christians. including poor, of one city, should pay 110,000 piastres ; being 
a tax far more onerous than the haratch, which, till last year, was only 30 piastres 
for each person, though even that amount was a local abuse; the teskerés of 
exemption at the capital only costing 15 piastres. 

n spite of the Pasha’s threats, the Chiefs of the Christians announced their 
resolution to send a deputation to Constantinople, After some resistance the

No. 12. 
Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—( Received March 10.) 


(No, 44.) | 
My Lord, Constantinople, February 7, 1851. 

MANY of my despatches within the last ten months bear witness to the 
dissatisfaction with which I have been compelled to view the state of affairs in 
this country. The few good measures which have been successfully carried 
during that period are far from sufficing to form a solid foundation for the 
prosperity and independence of the Empire. They have, moreover, been accom- 
panied with several disastrous events, afflicting to humanity, and tending still 
farther, in some respects, to impair what yet remains of Ottoman power. The 
Sultan’s arms have, indeed, succeeded in repressing rebellion, and extending the 
application of the new administrative principles ; but the triumph has cost dear 
in more ways than one. It has operated as an additional drain on the military 
population, already fast decreasing, and it has left a deficit, not easy to be filled 
up, in the resources of the Empire. These drawbacks on success were, perhaps, 
unavoidable, but still they are evils requiring a remedy. 

On the whole, we have reached a point at which it is more than ever 
manifest that the existing course of reform requires itself to be reformed ; and 
that, without the adoption of a better and more effective system of improve- 
ment, even the ordinary march of Government is likely to fall into confusion, 
and the cloud, which has long gathered over the destinies of Turkey, to thicken 
into hopeless gloom. | 

It is painful, but necessary, to add that, whatever the cause may be, the 
exertions of Reshid Pasha, sustained and stimulated by British counsels, are 
unequal to the task of either producing unanimity in the Cabinet, or of removing 
the scruples of the Seraglio. In proportion as disease takes root, repugnance 
to the means of cure would seem to strengthen also, The test has been fairly 
applied. A further pressure would, in all probability, under present circum 
stances, either deprive me of the Sultan’s goodwill, or send the Grand Vizier 
into a long retirement. | 1 

Such being the case, I need hardly offer any excuse for determining to 
refrain awhile from any direct efforts to overcome the existing difficulty. Shoult 
further reflection, increasing embarrassment, or change of Court favour, bring 
out a more complete acceptance of my suggestions, nothing will be lost by the 

yause. Should things continue pretty much as they are, which is more probable, 
may turn the time to account by communicating fully with your Lordship, and 
ascertaining the opinion of Her Majesty's Government as to what course of 
proceeding will be most advisable hereafter, S 

With this view I proceed to offer a few remarks, which may possibly contri- 
bute in some degree to a clearer apprehension of the case, or serve at least 
to reeall to mind what has been urged before, in other stages of the same 
«juestion. 7 PA ey Fe 

It is needless to tell your Lordship that the master mischief in this country 
is the dominant religion,—such, at least, as human passions, political interests, 
and ethnical manners have practically made it, That is the real f leviathan,” 
which, “floating many a rood,” overluys the prostrate energies ol Purkey. 
Though altogether effete as a principle of national strength and reviving power, 
the spirit of Islamism, thus perverted, lives in the supremacy of the conquering 
race, und in the prejudices engendered by a long Pins domination, — It 
may not be too much to say, that the progress of the Empire towards a firm 
reestablishment of its prosperity and independence, is to be measured by the 
degree of its emancipation from that source of injustice anil weakness, With 


7 


Islamism regulating the faith and moral conduct of the individuals who profess 
it, we have nothing to do. With Islamism monopolizing all the powers and 
functions of the State, haying one measure of justice for the Mussuliman, another 
for the Rayah, and sheltering the malefactor of its own creed while it oppresses 
and degrades the most deserving of other classes, there can be no lasting concur- 
renee or cordial sympathy on the part of Christendom. Such Powers, if any 
there be, as look to the dismemberment of Turkey, will see in these fatal distine- 
tions the strongest encouragement of their hopes; while those who are anxious 
to make the Sultan’s Empire, in its full integrity, a part of the European system 
of balance and civilization, must be proportionately disheartened and alienated 
by the continuance of so great an evil. ‘The reform of abuses, as deeply seated 
as they are intolerable in the eye of justice and according to every sound view 
of policy, is, no doubt, a work of time, but, to yield the intended result, it must 
also be a work of progression. Allow it to be stopped, and all stops with it, 
except the process of decay, which works best in stagnation. 

The unrelaxing continuance of that progression is not only recommended 
by principles of humanity, and the maxims of a sound European policy; the 
national interests, both of the Ottoman Empire and of those nations which trade 
with it, are closely attached to the same course of action. The Sultan himself 
has everything to gain by its success. 

As to the dangers of reform, all great advances over rooted prejudices and 
long-established habits involve more or less of sacrifice or of risk. ‘The danger 
in this case may be either of weakness resulting from the diminished uscendancy 
of the Turks, or of their resistance to further innovations on the part of the 
Government. But neither of these objections will be found to offer any just 
enuse of apprehension. ‘The former has long existed of itself as a necessary 
consequence of governing on fulse principles, and a state of transition, which is 
clearly one of weakness, has naturally ensued. ‘The danger of partial resistance 
has been already encountered with signal success, and it has never yet threatened 
to take the shape of a general or spreading insurrection, Be it, moreover, 
remembered that, peril for peril, there is more in the end to be dreaded from a 
continuance of the present abuses, vexations, and embarrassments, than from an 
earnest and effective determination to correct them. 

The more prominent of these evils are the corruption of persons in office, 
financial disorder, judicial partiality, degrading distinctions, fanatical crime, and 
& monopolizing jealousy of foreigners. The baneful consequences of their 
existence are acknowledged by the Sultan and his leading Ministers. Nor is 
there any real want of power or of means to surmount the obstacles to their 
removal. ‘The army is perfectly loyal, and submission to supreme authority is 
all but an article of faith with the great majority of Mahometans. The inquiries 
already instituted, and the corrections adopted in part, with respect. to criminal 
jurisdiction and the collection of the haratch, may be used as facilities to further 
and more efficient progress. 

There is another class of impediments more difficult to deal with, but it is 
reasonable to hope that they will gradually, though slowly, give way of them. 
selves, in proportion as the stream of improvement is let in upon them by the 
removal of more palpable obstructions. Such, for instance, are the mutual 
antipathies of race, the backwardness of the Turks in knowledge of eve 
description, their general unfitness for competition with their Christian fellow- 
subjects, the consequent fewness of those who are properly qualified for office, 
and the scarcity of carriageable roads and of internal water communications. 
To these may he added, the extravagance of domestic establishments, the 
Sepanagnee of men in power on a corrupt class of money makers, the enervating 
influences of the liarem, and the limited commund of numbers for military 
SUT De, 

Here is a formidable array of difficulties, it must be admitted, but not an 
insurmountable one, if 1am right in asserting that power enough exists in the 
Government to remove the foremost of them, and to open the way for time and 
circumstance to dispose of the rest, Such is the conviction which may fairly be 
entertained as a rational probability, not indeed susceptible in all respects of 
logical proof, but nevertheless recommended asa basis of conduct by many 
forcible considerations, and by the most urgent of all, necessity and seli-preser., 
vation. 


The real causes of this Empire's weakness, as 1 have already intimated, are 
r 


18 


the remains of those false exclusive principles of government which, equally in 
a social, an administrative, and an international point of view, accelerated the 
exhaustion of its original strength, and prevented it from conforming in time to 
the requirements of-an altered relative position by introducing needful improve- 
ments and developing its natural resources. What little was done in those 
respects before the overthrow of the Janissaries enabled the Porte, with occasional 
assistance from abroad, to prolong a discreditable and existence in spite of its 
disastrous wars and still more disastrous internal commotions. Greece, Adri- 
anople, and Egypt, were the stepping-stones to that change of poliey which, 
notwithstanding the numerous motives for its adoption, had previously extended 
but little, if'at all, with the exception of quarantine, beyond the army, ‘To the 
principles proclaimed at Gulhané, however imperlectly carried out, the Porte is, 
no doubt, indebted for a certain degree of advancement, by means of which its 
subjects have been relieved, in part, from the old system of oppression, and the 
Porte itself has acquired a stronger hold on the sympathies of Christendom, and 
a better direction towards the revival of its prosperity. 

But this stage of improvement being reached under circumstances of 
financial embarrassment, of administrative corruption, and very defective know- 
ledge, added to much that remains of religious prejudice and habitual tyranny, 
there is room to fear that the hopes and exertions which | have encouraged, will 
evaporate in palliatives and partial expedients, affording, indeed, some lecal or 
temporary relic, but wholly meffectual for the accomplishment of any great or 
permanent result. The united wants and opportunities of the present juncture 
ought, in reason. to have the effect of overpowering all objection and to impose, 
asa necessity, that efficient and comprehensive system of reform which few, 
except Reshid Pasha and his immediate partizans, have any serious inclination 
to necept. Unfortunately, the lever which is most needed is exactly the one 
which it ig the most difficult to command. | have looked in vain to the Sultan, 
and even to his Minister, for that degree of resolution and firmness which ts 
indispensable for confronting the opposition of interested parties, and carrying 
a new system of policy into practical effect, It would seem that the fear of 
laying himself open to cen-ure by any striking innovation, operates as power- 
fully on the Sultan’s mind as a certain timidity of character and natural 
delicacy towards his Sovereign operate on that of Reshid. The intluence and 
advice of this Embassy, which haye not been spared in combating these weak- 
nesses, have had more than one predisposing circumstance in their favour; but, 
unhappily, no proportional success has hitherto accrued. This is the more 
surprising, a8 the uecessities, on which I oviginally founded my suggestions, are 
becoming daily more apparent. ‘The financial part of them las even received a 
simnal testimony from the Portes own bankers, whose interests have long been 
engaged in maintaining the present system, 

I may still, without inconvenience, recommend any separate measure of 
good promise to the Porte, and submit such further information as may come in 
aid of my previous advice; but the main and ultimate object of our solicitude 
can hardly be realised without a recourse to stronger and more Imposing means 
than any which I have hitherto employed. 

If any such means be within our reach, it is fitter for your Lordship than 
for me to estimate the expediency or opportuneness of employing them. My 
very imperfect reflections on the subject have led me to an alternative which I 
rather put forward at present as an clement of deliberation than asa choice of 
means designed for immediate application. Of the two eventual modes. of 
yroceeding which it comprises, one would be in the exclusive competency of 
er Majesty's Government; the other, fo ensure success, might require a 
eoneutrence of two or more of the leading Powers of Europe, In neither case 
would there necessarily be any departure trom the most friendly tone. 

Her Majesty's Government empowering me to repeat and enforce the 
aubstance of my recent counsels to the Sultan in the form of a conlidential note 
addressed to the Porte, might further instruct me to seck an audienee by the 
Queen's command, for the purpose of expressing satisfaction at the gracious 
reception given to me on a later occasion, und ai carnest hope on Her Mujesty’s 
part that the Ottoman Ministers would be commanded to take my note into 
their enlightened and effective deliberation. Considering the probable results of 
such a step, [ should say that at best it would be more likely to yield a partial 
than a complete success, 


19 


In the latter hypothesis it would perhaps be necessary to give a more 
decided character, as well as a collective form, to the proposed communication. 
The Representatives of the concurring parties might be authorized to declare the 
anxious desire of’ their respective Governments that Turkey should continue to 
advance in all those improvements which are best calculated to associate her 
with the general interests and sympathies of Europe, and to call forth her 
productive energies ; that measures should be taken to perpetuate the happy 
result of the Sultan's successful military operations in those parts of his domi- 
nions where a rebellious spirit had been lately manifested ; and that the tranquillity 
of his Empire should be consolidated, and the loyal services of all classes of his 
subjects he secured to his throne, by extending impartial justice and an equality 
of civil rights to every religious community alike. 

This recommendation might be grounded on the marked increase of actual 
and prospective intercourse between the Sultan's dominions and other commer- 
cial countries: on the submissive conduct and enlarged acquirements of the 
Porte’s Christian subjects since the establishment of Hellenic independence ; and 
on the title to a friendly interference for this object of justice and benevolence 
derived by the Powers of Christendom from their late spontaneous engagement 
to respect the integrity of the Sultan’s Empire. How far the returning tran- 
quillity of Europe, or the relations of the several leading Powers with each other, 
may be likely to prove eventually fayourable to an experiment of this kind, it is 
not for me to determine. A conjecture, however, may be hazarded, that if it 
were once adopted by the Western Powers, it would hardly be left to: their 
separate direction by the Northern. . 

Reeurring for a moment to the first alternative, | would venture to observe 
that its success might be considerably promoted if the suggestions of Her 
Majesty's Government were to be accompanied with some distinct assurance to 
the Sultan of support in cases of emergency from without, and a clearer opening 
for that eventual reform of the Capitulations at which I have hinted in former 
parts of my correspondence, and on which the Porte’s attention is naturally and 
justly fixed with increasing interest. An engagement of this kind would, in the 
former respect, be a mere embodiment of obligations virtually existing in the 
very nature of our relations with Turkey, and, in the latter, little more than an 
admission of that approaching necessity which social progress and mutual 
convenience have begun already to indicate, 

The growing importance of this country in all that relates to the highest 
interests of society, taken in its simple or in its most complex signification, may, 
I trust, excuse me for having been carried into greater length than I had antici- 
ped when entering on a subject so frequently treated before. To my appre- 

iension the undeniable importance of the question is greatly increased by the 
circumstanees in which this Empire is placed. These cireumstances concur, 
upon the whole, to require or to promote the establishment of a real effective 
system of reform, but their concurrence is. rather fortuitous than durable. 
Whatever personally regards the Sovereign and his Ministers, whatever more 
generally relates to passing occurrences and fluctuating interests, carries with it 
everywhere a certain degree of instability, Here emphatically such considera- 
tions constitute the springs of action and regulating influences of the State. 

In writing to your Lordship it would be worse than idle for me to enter 
more deeply into particulars. 1 have rather to apologise for the veal Which has 
led me so fur already ; yet knowing how constantly your Lordship’s attention is 
directed to all that concerns tle public service, the interests of lumanity, and 
the welfare of Europe, Lam confident that neither excess nor deficiency on my 
side will prevent the whole of the question from receiving a deliberate und 
impartial consideration on yours. Whenever the moment for a final determina- 
tion shall be thought to have arrived, the wisdom of Her Majesty's Government 
wili, no doubt, decide for the best; and in the meanwhile it will be my duty to 
convey to your Lordship such further light as incidents and observation may 
throw upon the subject. 

L have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING,

No. 18. 
Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston. —(Received July 7.) 


(No 201.) 
My Lord, Therapia, June 18, 1851. 

I AVAIL myself of the present opportunity to transmit to your Lordship 
in copy an official note which { lately presented to the Porte, in the hope of 
obtaining some remedy against those remains of religious persecution which fall 
occasionally under my notice, and for which I find it so diffieult to obtain 
adequate redress, though I believe the time to be now arrived when the Sultan’s 
Ministers might complete without danger or serious inconvenience the more 
enlightened system of policy adopted some years ago by their predecessors at the 
requisition of Her Majesty's Government in concert with that of France. It may 
nohappily be presumed that for one instance which comes to ny knowledge of 
sufferings occasioned by religious fanaticiam, there are several which never 
transpire beyond the precinets ofthe remote town or secluded village where they 
oveur, If the Porte could be induced to adopt more stringent measures of 
repression on the spot, and to displace those bigoted magistrates and members of 
Provineial Councils who counteract and often control the Sultan’s immediate 
representative, there would be less taste for displaying superstitious zeal at the 
expense of humanity. Eire | 

It is not to be expected that the Porte will give any formal reply to my 
note, But the serious manner in which I have drawn attention to the existing 
cases of religious fanaticism will doubtless produce a tacit effect, and strengthen 
the hands of those members of the Government who, together with the Sultan, 
are sincerely averse to every indulgence of fanatical animosity.

(Signed) RATFORD CANNING.

Inclosure in No. 18. 


Sir Stratford Canning to Aali Pasha. 


Therapia, June 6, 1851. 
THE Undersigned has the honour to recommend what follows to the 

peculiar attention of his Excellency the Ottoman Minister for Foreign Affairs. 
Several years have elapsed since His Imperial Majesty the reigning Sultan, 
moved as much by his own generous disposition as by a regard for friendly 
representations, abolished capital punishment on account of religion throughout 
his Empire, and pledged his sovereign word, with reference to the same cause of 
cution, that even those of his subjects who were not of the Mussulman 
faith should be treated universally with justice and benevolence. Agreeably to 
that solemn engagement no apostate has lately suffered death under the sanction 
of authority, and some practical improvements have been introduced into the 
treatment of Christians summoned on religious grounds before the Turkish 
magistrate, Proceedings, however, which it is dilficult, if not impossible, to 
reconcile with the laws of justice and humanity, continue to be allowed, and 
sacrifices unworthy of an enlightened Government are still unhappily made to 


ao 


fanaticism and its attendant spirit of persecution. Persons accused of having 
changed their religious principles are thrown into prison, or transported to the 
capital from remote provinees at much cost and hazard. Children incapable of 
discerning right from wrong are not unirequently trepanned into Islamism by 
artful intrigues, and restrained by magisteria coercion from abiding in the fait 
of their parents. Parents themselves are sometimes deprived of their children, 
and sometimes empowered to retain them, in flagrant violation of the rights of 
ronseience. Cases of this and of other kinds, more or less bearing e character 
of bigotry and persecution, and clearly militating against the Sultan's promises, 
have been occasionally brought by this Embassy to the knowledge of the Porte. 
A certain degree of attention has assuredly not been withheld from such repre- 
sentations, and it may perhaps be stated with truth, that in the plurality of 
instances relief, if not redress, has been given. 

But there is, nevertheless, much need of further improvement. Not only 
do the evils already pointed out continue to operate in the provinces, but the 
Government itself does not carry out its own principle of forbearance with suffi- 
cient vigour and consistency. The consequences of this remissness, besides 
being injurious to the victims of a misguided zeal, encourage among the people 
those very dispositions which it is naturally the object of a reforming Govern- 
ment to correct, Mutual animosities still prevail among the classes of different 
creeds, and those who most need protection look for it in the sympathies of some 
foreign nation or of some encroaching Power. 

The time ig come when the Porte may fairly be expected to pursue a more 
decided course in the direction of its declared policy. ‘There is no longer any 
suificient motive for temporizing. Reason and Humanity have shot their beans 
‘nto the strongholds of fanaticism. ‘The Sultan's authority has overpowered the 
force of antique prejudice, even at Aleppo or Damascus. Measures calculated 
to remove what remains of the old exclusive system may now be taken with 

erfect safety. Attempts at persecution may be put down with ease on the spot. 
he imprisonment, or transfer to Constantinople, of persons accused of irreligion 
or apostacy, is become a wanton and needless act of cruelty. The Mushir of a 


province, or Mutesellim of a city, has only to be duly instructed and supported 
by the Supreme Government, in order to protect the rights of conscience in all 
that is essential to their exercise. ‘Too much is at present left to the discretion 
or pleasure of the individual in office, who tie seeks to guard his own 
responsibility by referring to the Medjlis or to the Porte. 

There is at this moment the case of an Armenian woman, who, having 
apostatized to Islamism, pretends to oblige her two children, both of tender age, 
to follow her example, notwithstandin the unwillingness of their Christian 
father to renounce his claim. The whole family has been brought up from 
Sivas to Constantinople on this account. 

Another and very painful case is pending at Damascus. The mother, a 
Greek, having renounced her Christian faith for Islamism, has attempted to take 
away her children from their father, whom she had deserted. The father was 
east into prison—the children were torn by violence from his protection. 
Though the eldest son has been since given up, the youngest, a boy of seven 
years old, was handed over to the mother to be educated as a Mussulman, in 
spite of his tears and resistance, An extract of the Consul’s report is annexed 

ercto. 

Such out against nature and morality discredit the religion which 
eanctions and the Government which allows their commission. The state of 
society in which they can take place with impunity is altogether unsound and 
dangerous. ‘The Porte will do well to amend it without any unnecessary delay. 


‘Vue Undersigned, &c. | 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

No. 19. : 
Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—(Received July 7.) 
(No. 202.) 
My Lord, Therapia, June 18, 1851. 


IF the information conveyed to Mr. Vico-Consul Clapperton be correct, @ 
conspiracy has lately been formed at Adana for a massacre of the Christian 
population at that place or in its neighbourhood. Immediately on the receipt 
of his despatches | made known their contents to Aali Pasha, who submitted 
them next day to the Council, and obtained a decision anep ung my suggestion 
that reinforcements should be forthwith sent to the scene of danger under the 
command of a superior officer instructed to arrest the conspirators, to protect 
the Christians, and, in ease of any overt act, to punish the offenders. 

Scarcely had this decision been taken, when the Government, as Aali Pasha 
informed me, received an official report from Adana, attributing the alleged plan 
of massacre to the credulity of the French Vice-Consul, and utterly denying the 
correctness of the charge. The Porte, in consequence, altered its plan of 
proceeding, and determined on sending down an officer of superior authority and 
character, without any additional troops, but with powers to act according to 
circumstances in the sense of the intended instructions. 

All things considered, I am not disinclined to acquiesce in this arrangement, 
as one that will probably be found sufficient to meet the supposed danger. What 
I regret is, that several days have been allowed to elapse without dispatching the 
officer in question, or even appointing him ; and it is to no purpose that T have 
endeavoured to obtain a more prompe execution of a measure adopted as 
necessary by the Porte itself, 

Meanwhile | have written to inform Mr. Clapperton of what has occurred 
hetween me and the Turkish Mimsters. My despatch will pass, under flyin 
seal, through Mr, Suter’s hands; and I hear from Aleppo that Her Majesty's 
ship “ Frolic” was about to appear off the coast of Tarsous. 


1 have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Sir, Constantinople, April 30, 1851. 

1 SEND inclosed, herewith, for your information. a lithographed copy 
and English translation of an Imperial firman which was issued at my 
suggestion some months ago for the purpose of establishing the Sultan's 
Protestant subjects us a separate community, invested with the same privileges 
as those enjoyed by the more anciet. Rayah communities in this Empire. ‘The 
object of this firman was not only to secure to the Protestant Rayahs their right 
af conscience and freedom from religious persecution, but alzo to put an en 
to those conllicting pretensions which oceasionally broke out into a breach af the 
peace, and gave much trouble to the Ottoman authorities. 

It ig to be hoped that the Sultan’s pleasure, a8 now irrevocably declared, 
will recive a fulland general execution wherever there is a congregation ot 
Protestants, more or less numerous, throughout His Majesty's dominions, and 
that the intercourse necessary for that purpose will be established on a proper 
footing between the Protestant agent and the principal functionary of the place, 
The Protestants, enabled to enjoy the free exercise of their worship with those 
essential adjuncts for which the frman has provided, will, no doubt, be careful 
to avoid all unnecessary collision with the Churel from which they have seceded, 
and to manifest a grateful and affectionate loyalty towards the Savereign who 
has so generously vindicated their rights, ‘The interest whieh | cannat but feel 
for their coutinued tranquillity and welfare, wil be so readily partaken by you 
that it is almost unnecessary for me to recommend them to. your observation 
and friendly offices. In employing the latter on any occasion which may require 
them you will remember that any lind of interference in such matters om your 
part is Hable to excite jealousy in a greater or jess degree, and that it can seldom 
be efficiently exercised without opportuneness and discretion. 

lam, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Inclosure 2 in No, 20. 


Firman. 
(‘Translation.)} 

MOST honoured Vizier, Illustrious Counsellor, Maintainer of the good erder 
of the world, Director of public affairs with wisdom and judgment, Accomplisher 
of the important transactions of mankind with intelligence and good sense, 
Consolidator of the edifice of Empire and of glory, endowed by the Most High 
with abundant gifts, and Mushir at this time of my gate of felicity, my Vizier, 
Mehmed Pasha, may God be pleased to preserve him long in exalted dignity ! 

Let it be known on receipt of this my noble rescript that— 

Whereas those of my Christian subjects who have embraced the Protestant 
faith have suffered inconveniences and difficulties in consequence of their not 
having been hitherto placed under a separate and spectal jurisdiction, and in 
consequence of the patriarchs and primates of their old creeds which they have 
abandoned not being naturally able to administer their affairs. 

Whereas in necessary accordance with my Imperial solicitude and benevo- 
lence towards all classes of my subjects, it is contrary to my Imperial pleasure 
that any class of them should be exposed to trouble, and whereas by reason of 
their faith the aforesaid Protestants for a separate community, it is in conse- 
quences my Royal pleasure that measures bo taken for the sole purpose of 
facilitating the administration of their affairs, so that they may live in peace, 
quiet, and security, let then a respectable and trustworthy person, chosen by 
themselves from among their own number, be appointed, with the tile of "A rent 
of the Protestants,” to be attached to the Department of the Minster af Police. 
It chall be the duty of the Agent to take charge of the register of the members 
of the community, and which is to be kept at the Police Department. The 
Agent is to register therein all births and deaths, All applications for passporty 


und marriage licenses, and on those special affairs of the community which are 
fl 


26 


to come before the Sublime Porte, or any other department, are to be made 
under the official seal of the Agent. The present Royal and August Ediet has 
been especially ranted and issued rom my Imperial Chancery for carryiig my 
pleasure into execution ; hence thou, the above indicated Mushir, shall curry the 
preceding Ordinance into scrupulous execution, conformably with the explana- 
tions given. As. however, the nsacssment of taxes and the delivery of passports 
ure subject to specitic regulations, thou shalt not permit anything to be done in 
contravention thereto; thou shalt not suffer any tax or haratch to he required 
of the Protestants for marriage licences, or for registration ; thou shelt be careful 
that, like unto the other communities of the Empire, every facility and required 
acsistance be afforded to them in all their affairs, and in all matters concerning 
their cemeteries and places of worship ; thou shalt not permit any interference 
whatsoever on the part of any other community with their rites or with their 
religious concerns, Nor, in short, with any of their affairs, either secular or 
religious. in any manner whatsoever, in order that they may be enabled to 
exercise the usages of their faith m security; thou shalt not suffer them to be 
molested one iota in these or in any other matters ; und thou shalt be careful 
and attentive to maintain them in the desired quict and security, ‘They are to 
be permitted to make those representations to the Sublime Porte which it may 
be necessary to make concerning their affairs, through their Agent. 

After thou hast taken due cognizance of these matters, thou shalt cause the 
present noble rescript to be registered in the proper quarter, and shalt cause it to 
be confirmed in the possession of the aforesaid subjects, and thou shalt be careful 
that the high provisions thereof be always carried into due execution. ‘Thus be 
‘t known unto thee, giving full credence to the Imperial Signet. 

Done in the second decade of the sacred month of Moharrem, in the year of 
the Hegira 1267, at Constantinople the well-guarded,

Sir Stratford Canning to Viscount Palmerston.—(Received August 5.) 


(No. 220.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 15, 1851. 

IN one of your recent instructions [to Mes vane Lordship directs iy 
attention to the expediency of rather recommending separate measures of 
improvement to the Turkish Ministers. than any combined plan or compre 
hensive system of reform. Such is, in fact, the course I have practically 
yursued since Reshid Pasha avowed his inability to obtain the adoption of a 
oan, which formed the basis of my last. year’s suggestions to the Sultan, and 
since His Majesty himself, under the influence of corrupt advisers, manifested 
a decided repugnance to that part of the proposed scheme. | cannot indeed 
say that I have seen any reason to change my opinions in that respect. The 
necessity of embracing 0 complete system of measures, and carrying: i reste 
lutely, though gradually, out, by means of borrowed capital in the outset, 
becomes, on the contrary, Mere evident with every day's experience. W haat 
jus really decided my conduct is, want of power to remove the tveak scruples 
prevailing 1 high quarters, and a eonvietion that time und circumstance can 
alone, as they probably will in due season, do justice to the soundness of my 
counsels. 

The separate measures of superior practical importance which | have more 


25 


particularly endeavoured to press forward of late, are, the extension of the new 
principles of criminal procedure to all parts of the Empire, the formation ofa 
national bank on principles of fair dealing and pablie confidence, the intro- 
duction of a more humane system of prison discipline and accommodation, the 
promotion of a searching inquiry into the adininistrative abuses in the Provinces 
by means of Lmperial Commissioners, and an improvement of the works which 
defend the approaches to Constantinople. 

Referring to these five measures in the order wherein they are here placed, 
L have to state that, although the Porte's assurances with respect to eriminal 
procedure will, probably, be realized at no distant period, [ have hitherto 
looked in vain for any ostensible step, on its part, to warrant that expectation. 

More progress has been made towards the establishment of an etheient 
bank, and I have reason to believe that the greater part of my amendments 
have been admitted, Some serious difficulties remain, nevertheless, to be sur- 
mounted, and it is the opinion of practical men that the project, as it now 
stands. will not be found equal to the proposed objects without a considerable 
increase of capital and better security for an honest administration of the estab- 
lishment. 

The question of prison improvement has not yet advanced beyond a 
favourable reeeption of my suggestions and the papers which accompanicd 
them. TaN ain 

The Imperial Commissioners are still pursuing their inquiries in Albania, 
Bulgaria, and Asia Minor. ‘Those inquiries, properly direeted, may be eventually 
productive of much beneht to the Government, and much relief to its subjects ; 
but whether they will be carried through with a sufficient degree of mtegrity, 
veal, and resolution, isa question which nothing but the result can determine. 

With respect to the fortifications of the Bosphorus, | beg to refer your 
Lordship to the accompanying papers, communicated to me in confidence by 
the Grand Vizier, ‘They do not say much for the foresight or activity of those 
who are charged with the defence of the capital, nor have | concealed from 
Reshid Pasha the feclings of disappointment, and, in some respects, of Surprise, 
which their respective contents, especially when compared with cach other, are 
calculated to mwaken.

No, 26. 


Viscount Palmerston to Sir Stratford Canning. 
(No, 227.) 
Sir, Foreign Office, September 5, 1851, 

MR. CONSUL KERR has transmitted to me copies of his despatches 
Nos. 3] and 33, respecting the conduct pursued by the authorities in the Island 
of Cyprus, towards persons apostatizing from Islamism ; and I have to instruct 
your Excellency to state to the Turkish Government that Her Majesty's 
Government feel confident that the Porte will deem it necessary to put an end 
to proceedings which, if allowed to continue, would cast a stain upon the good 
faith of the Turkish Government ; and you will point out that if the Turkish 
officers who are now in authority at Rhodes and in Cyprus do not choose to act 
in conformity with the humane intentions of the Sulton, the Turkish Govern- 
ment will no doubt have recourse to the obvious remedy, namely, the dismissal 
of those disobedient officers, and the appointment of others who may better 
know how to perform their duties. 

Her Majesty’s Government are convinced that the Turkish Government 
must be aware that the time is gone by when Christian Europe could see with 
indiflerence the persecutions of Christians on account of their religious opinions ; 
and os the Porte thinks it essential for the interests and safety of the Turkish 
Empire that Turkey should be deemed one of the European pan and that 


al 


it should be an element in the general balance of power in Europe, the nations 
of Christendom have a right to expect that fanatical persecution of Christians 
shall cease in Turkey, and that no persons shall in Turkey be punished or 
molested in consequence of their religious opinions, whether those opinions may 
have been originally imbibed or may have been adopted upon conversion, 


Tam, &e. 
(Signed) PALMERSTON.

Inclosure 2 in No, 27. 
Sir Stratford Canning to Mr. Ff. Pisant. 


Monsicur, Therapia, le 10 Seplembre, 1851. 

UNE année a peu pres s'est écoulée depuis insurrection des Musulmans & 
Alep. Cet acte de rébellion avait sa source dans le désir d’empécher la mise en 
exécution de la Loi de Reerutement, Onadésobéi au Sultan pour ne pas fournir 
son contingent de soldat d’aprés le nouveau réglement, Les fanatiques, excités 
par la soif du pillage et par leur haine des Chrétiens, se sont empresses de profiter 
de occasion. Le sang des Rayahs a coulé; leurs maisons, leurs églises, ont éte 
incendiées; leurs propriétés de toute espéce ont eté pillées, Aussitit que la 
nouvelle de ces forfaits est arrivée 4 la capitale, la Porte, agissant d’apres la 
volonté du Sultan, a pris ses mesures pour le rétablissement de Vordre, pour la 

unition des coupables, et pour la mise en exécution de la Loi de Recrutement. 

le a aussi donné les promesses les plus positives que les effets pillés seraient 
autant que possible restitués, et que les Rayahs et d'autres victimes de la fureur 
populaire seraient indemnisées pour la perte de ceux quil serait impossible dle 
retrouver. 

Les mesures prises par la Porte pour l'accomplissement des objets de la 
premiére catégorie ont eu leur plein effet. Mais les promesses qui accom- 
pagnaient l’adoption de ces mesures restent encore fort loin d’étre remplies. Les 
rapports et les mémoires parvenus ici d’Alep font foi de cette différence entre ce 
qu'on a fait pour les intéréts de l'autorité et ce qu’on a promis de faire pour ceux 
des individus. La restitution des effets pillés n'a été que trés-partiale; lindem- 
nité est nulle, Ce qui empire la position des malheurenx réclamants est 
encouragement que donne & leurs oppresseurs la tiédeur avec laquelle les auto- 
rités ont agi A cet égard. 

Il est bien connu, pourtant, que la prospérité d'un Etat n'est ala longue 
autre que la somme des prospérités individuelles, que la justice fait la yéritable 
force d'un Gouvernement, et que la protection des lois, ussurée ¢galement & tous 
les sujets, est surtout ce que ln justice exige. 

Jintérét sineére que je porte au bien-étre de cet Empire suffirait seul pour 
garantir ces remarques contre tout reproche d'une ingérence mal placée dans les 
affaires de 'intérieur Cependant, j'ai un titre de plus au droit de les proférer

sans critique. Le Consul de Sa Majesté & Alep m’a fai ir q ji

Sir Stratford Canning lo Viscount Puimerston —(Receiwed December 10.) 


(No, 355.) 
My Lord, Therapia, November 4, 1851. 
JA‘ a small village of mixed population, situated on the Asiatic shore of the 
Bospliorus, there cecurred, some few days ago, an incident which merits attention 
as exemplifying the spirit of fanaticism which still prevails among all classes of 
Mahometans not enlightened by frequent association with Christian foreigners. 
The circumstances to which I allude are stated in the accompanying instruction, 
addressed to Mr. Stephen Pisani, and in hastening to bring them under the 
notice of the Turkish Ministers, I felt the importance of taking the lead in urging 
the Porte to vindicate the rights ofits Christian subjects in. so flagrant a case. 
Your Lordship will learn from Mr. Pisani’s report, which 15 also inclosed 
herewith im copy, that the Grand Vizier manifested a very becoming sense of 
duty on the occasion, and that he appears to have been prepared of his own 
accord to adopt those measures of justice which the outrage imperatively 
requircs. 

Having visited the scene of violence I can bear witness to the extent of 
injury inflicted on the unfortunate proprietor whose buildings were destroyed, 
and to the truly painful impression which so.cruel a violation of private. nights 
had produced on the Greek inhabitants - re place, i 


have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Inclosure | in No. al, 


Sir Stratford Canning to Mr. 8. Pisant. 


Sir, Therapic, November 21, 1851. 
IMMEDIATELY on the receipt of this instruction you will wait wpon the 
Grand Vizier, and inform his Highness that | have heard with deep concern of 
the scandalous outrage which took place the day before yesterday ut the village 
8 


—— 


—— 


See ae 


<0 = 


34 


of Beikos, situated, as you know, on the Asiatic side of the Bosphorus, and 
nearly opposite the house in which I am residing. The Greek inhabitants of 
that place, amounting, as 1am told, to between §00 and 400 souls, have been 
long destitute of any building, however small, appropriated to religious worship, 
The Mussulmans have their mosques; the Armenians have a church in their 
quarter; the Greeks alone were deprived of the means of assembling for the 
purpose of worshipping the common Father of all. A priest of their persuasion 
purchased a small piece of ground, obtained @ finuan of permission, surrounded 
the space with a wall, and raised within it a building of moderate dimensions, 
partly for his own residence, and partly, as it would seem, for a place of devotion. 
A question being raised between him and the Moslem inhabitants of Beikos 
respecting this tenement, reference was made to legal authority; a trial took 
place, and sentence was given to his disadvantage. The knowledge of this 
incident appears to have excited the Mahometan population, and two days ago 
a large and riotous assemblage proceeded to the ground in question, broke within 
the inclosure, and destroyed the buildings there. This outrage was not 
accidental. It was provoked by certain ringleaders, whose names are inclosed 
herewith. A violent spirit of fanaticism was roused, the lice was intimidated, 
personal injury was inflicted upon several individuals, a Rayah girl is reported 
to have had a limb broken in the tumult. Altogether it was a most scandalous 
and cruel outraze, perpetrated in contempt of the Sultan's authority, and in 
cowardly reliance on superior numbers. 

You will read this statement to Reshid Pasha, and you will inform him 
that my present object in making it is only to let him know, and through him 
the Government, that the circumstances comprised in it are known to the 
Representatives of Christendom residing here. I cannot for a moment doubt his 
readiness to do justice in so flagrant a case of fanatical violence, or his 
perception of the measures, whether of reparation or of punishment, which 
justice imperatively requires. [| am thoroughly persuaded that the gracious 
assurances which Elis Imperial Majesty condescended to give me with his own 
lips in favour of his Christian subjects, and their protection against religious 

ersecution, will not be allowed to remain a dead letter on the present occasion. 

t will, of course, be necessary for me to report the circumstances to Her 
Majesty’s Government, and it will be a great consolation for me in doing 80 to 
be able to convey an account of the measures of redress adopted by the Sultan’s 
Government. 

You will give in the accompanying list of names, and you are at liberty to 
leave a copy of this instruction with the Grand Vizier, should his Highness 
express a wish to that effect, 

You will subsequently apprise Aali Pasha of the proceeding, and afford 
him any explanation which may be required, 


am, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD CANNING.

Sir, Pera, November 22, 1851, 

IMMEDIATELY on the receipt of your Excellency’s instruction of 
yesterday's date, respecting the scandalous outrage which took plate at Beikos 
two days ago, I lost no time in waiting on the Grand Vizier, and reading and 
explaining to him the whole of its contents, 

His Highoess informed me that the Seraskier Pasha having been apprized 
of the occurrence soon after it took place, he instantly transmitted orders to the 
head of the police in the Bosphorus to repair to Beikos, apprehend the perpe- 
trators, and send them to the prison of the central police. But having taken 
good care to abscond, their arrest could not be effected until this morning, when 
the Seraskier Pasha sent one of his aides-de-camp with peremptory orders not 
te come back until he had got hold of them. As soon as they are found, they 
are to appear before the Supreme Council, where the affair is to be inquired 
intsuent the authors of the outrage punished in accordance with the Penal Code 
in forces.

Meanwhile the priest is to be ‘ndemnified for the losses he sustained on 
this occasion, and permission is to be granted to him for carrying on his 
building. 

This matter has been submitted to the consideration of the Sultan, and as 
soon as His Majesty's Iradé is issued, the priest will be furnished with the 
necessary means to carry into effect his object without hindrance on the part 
of any one. 

is Highness having expressed a wish to be allowed to haye a copy of

Sa 4 Aali Pasha of the proceeding in questi 

Having subsequently apprise i Pasha of the proceeding in quesucn,

and of the Porte’s intentions in favour of the priest.

No. 33. 


Colonel Rose to the Earl of Malmesbury.—(Recewed December 2.) 
(No. 136.) 
My Lord, Therapia, November 16, 1852. 

1 HAVE much satisfaction in informing your Lordship that I have 
succeeded in obtaining a very important concession from the Porte in favour 
of Turkish Protestants. 

Numerous complaints have been made to me by the American missionaries, 
and these Protestants, as to the oppreasion they have suffered for years through- 
out Turkey ; the continuation of it they attribute mainly to the circumstance 
that the firman in their favour has never been sent from Constantinople 
throughout the provinces. 

The Armenians, who dislike the Protestants because they seceded from 
them, only too often induce, by means of their wealth and influence, venal 
authorities and tribunals to decide against them; and it is the Armenians who 
are the main eause that the Protestant firman has never issued from the archives 
of the Porte. ‘They know well how much the Protestants would gain in strength 
and unity by the publication of this charter of their rights. 

The feeling both of Aah Pasha and Fuad Effendi as to the Protestants was 
rather unfavourable. They seemed bored by their continued complaints, and 
apprehensive lest ‘Turkish Protestantism, and the not always prudent attempts 
at Protestant conversion, should involve the Porte in difficulties with the shee 
sects and their coreligionist Powers, 

1 saw also indications of an apprehension of the spread of Protestantism 
amongst the Mussulmans. | 


36 


T have obtained orders in favour of the Protestants ; but I saw that the 
only real guarantee of their mghts was the circulation of the firman. 

Anything like an assumption on my part of a protectorate of Turkish 
Protestants would have been futal to their cause. 

T calmed all unfavourable feeling, as much as I could, by disclaiming any 
official protection of Protestantism, oF the wish to encourage proselytism to it. 
| said to the Turkish Ministers that all I desired was that Protestants should not 
be oppressed because they were Protestants, and that they should enjoy the 
rights of liberty of conscience and of free agency conceded by the Sultan to all 
his subjects ; and, finally, that it was not fair to give the Protestants a firman of 
rights, and then with old all the advantages which must accrue to them from its 
publication and circulation. 

My first attempts failed; my last, I am very happy to say, have succeeded. 
I have obtained a promise, as appears by the inclosed extract of a report from 
Mr. E. Pisani, that the Protestant firman shall be circulated throughout the 
Empire. 

I cannot speak too highly of the valuable assistance which [ received in 
this matter from Mr. Pisani. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) HUGH ROSE,

Inclosure 1 in No. 43. 
The Rev, H, Dwight to Colonel Rose. 


My dear Sir, Orta Keuy, October 6, 1852. 

| HEREWITH inclose to youa memorandum of the Protestant Armenians, 
by which you will see what are some of their present grievances. I take this 
opportunity also to say, ‘1 reference to the oppressive Snlian tax at. Margevan, 
that Mr. Bliss, one of our missionaries residing in that place, wrote me recentl 
that he had just had an ‘aterview with the Pasha of Amasia, in which he too 
occasion to speak of the oppressiveness of this tax, and asked the Pasha if it 
could not be reduced to the same rate among the Protestants as in the other 
communities. 

The Pasha replied, that “if the Protestants were required to pay only as 
much as the others do, all the Armenians would at once become Protestants.” 
The Pasha thus acknowledged to Mr, Bliss the fact that the Protestants are 
required to pay more than the others, and also stated the reason for it, 

1 beg that you will allow me to say a word or two before closing, on the 
subject of putting this firman into the hands of all the Protestants throughout 
the Empire. 

The simple request of the Protestants, as 1 understand it, is this: that in 
every Pashalik where Protestants are found, an agent, selected by themselves, be 
appointed by the Porte, with an Imperial firman, as the recognised head of the 
Protestants in that Pashalik. | 

‘This plan is precisely analogous to that which is pursued in each of the 
other Rayah communities in the Empire, and itis only in the natural carrying out 
of the Sultan’s wishes, as expressed in the existing firman ; and as things are at 
present organised, I do not hesitat- to say that they can be carried out properly 
in no other way. 

'The Protestants ask for no new right or privilege ; they only ask that what the 
Sultan has graciously granted to them may be secured upon a permanent basis 
in every part of the Empire, as he evidently intended. And T confess, that the 
unwillingness of the Sultan's Government to order that such agents be appointed 
and such firmans granted for the interior, greatly shakes my confidence in its 
disposition to act in good faith towards the Protestants and Her Britannic 
Majesty’s Ambassador, through whom this charter of equal rights and privileges 
was obtained, 

[ remain, &c. 


(Signed) H. G, O, DWIGHT.
