Inclosure 2 in No. 33. 
The Rev. H, Dwight to Colonel Rose. 


My dear Sir, Orta Keuy, October 12, 1852 

| AM truly sorry to be called upon to trouble you again so soon with 
complaints of ill-treatment of the Protestants, but some events have just trans- 
pired in Broussa and Demirdesh which Lam sure you will wish to know; and 
perhaps the knowledge of them my aid you in impressing upon the Turkish 
authorities here the importance of officially communicating the firman to every 
part of the Empire. They would save themselves an infinite deal of trouble by 
taking such a step, and surely we cannot ask for a more striking commentary on 
its imperious necessity, than is furnished in the narrative of facts | now have the 
honour to submit to you, 

The ground taken by the Council in Broussa, that the firman was intended 
only for the Protestants of Constantinople, is entirely contradicted by the almost 
numberless Vizirial letters that have been issued font the capital and sent to 
every part of the Empire, enjoining upon the authorities to see that the 
Protestants are organised into separate communities, and protected exactly as 
the other classes of Rayahs are. And the expulsion of the religious teacher of 
the Protestants from Demirdesh is a direct infraction of the firman, which 
authorises them to hold their worship in their own way ; and it is only a month 
or two since a Vizirial letter was sent to Marash on this very point, ordering 
that the Protestants be permitted to have their own religious kG, 

But [ need not enlarge. 

Praying that Providence may crown with abundant success your generous 
efforts in behalf of the persecuted and despised people of God in this land, I 
remain, &c. 

(Signed) H. G. O. DWIGHT.

Inclosure 3 in No. 33. 
Memorial from the Armenian Protestants to Colonel Rose. 


OUR gracious Sovereign the Sultan, learning the unhappy state of our 
persecuted community, granted us a firman, the object of which was to deliver 
us from every species of persecution. 

In this document it is ordered that we be protected like all the other 
communities of Rayahs in the Empire, and enjoy the same rights and privileges 
with them. Such is the expressed will of our Sovereign coneerning us. 

But we are still persecuted, and in various ways o pressed in different parts 
of the country ; and when we appeal to the Porte our just rights are not granted 
to us, but, on the contrary, our enemies among the Armenians ure permitted to 
carry out all their unjust designs against us. For example: One of our number 
residing in Marsevan, Hadjy Serope by name, lived for eighteen years in the 
undisputed occupancy of a house he had purchased with his own money in that 
town, and during this period no one pretended to have any claim to it; but 
when he became openly known as a Protestant, one of the principal magnates of 
that place, Hohannes Daniel Oghloo by name, a rich man, and a member of the 
Medjlis of the town, an owner of many houses, farms, vineyards, &c., took 
possession of nearly one-half of this house by violence, and, during the absence 
of the real owner, by peraieion of the local authorities he actually partitioned 
off that portion which he filsely claimed, taking possession of it as his own, 
The owner, Hadjy Serope, being a despised and forsaken Protestant, has abso- 
lutely no redress. ‘The local authorities are wholly under the influence of his 
rich and lordly oppressor, who is himself a member of the Medjlis before which 
the case must be tried, if tried in Marsevan. What hope is there for Hadjy 
Serope when endeavouring to procure the restoration of his property before ia 
Court of which his oppressor is one of the judges. 

The house ig now legally the property of Stephen Agha, and according to 
Turkish law and usage he can require HE trial of Daniel Oghloo’s claim ta be 


38 


made here at the capital ; there would then be some hope that the iniquity of 
this whole proceeding might be brought: to light, and justice be done. But 
although the Government of the Sultan has been again and again solicited to 
transfer the trial to this place, they have uniformly declined. | 

___ As another example, may be mentioned the fact, that in various places the 
Armenians have been permitted by the local authorities to assess the tax called the 
“ Salian,” upon the Protestants, although this is directly contrary to the will of 
the Sultan, as expressed in the firman. As might have been anticipated, this 
liberty has been used by the Armenians as a most terrible instrument of  perse- 
cution, Protestant subjects of the Porte, who have been honest and industrious, 
and ready to pay the full amount of their true and proper taxes to the Govern- 
ment, have, through the active enmity of the Armenians, been required to pay 
such exorbitant sums under the name of “ Salian,” as to render a comphance as 
utterly impossible for them as it would be to create a world, and the consequence 
has been that some have lain for many months in prison, and others have been 
obliged to flee. 

In Marsevan very few of the Protestants are able to remain at home and 
pursue their necessary work for the support of their families, on account of the 

tly increased amount of taxes they are required to pay. The amount of 
“Salian” assessed upon the Protestants by the Armenians, would average from 
150 to 180 piastres per head, which is five or six times the average in the 
Armenian or Greek communities ; this average in Marsevan we have ascertained 
on good authority is 32 piastres 2 paras each year, It is perfectly easy for the 
Porte to set this matter right, if they are so disposed, All they have to do is 
to ascertain, by actual investigation, the whole number of Protestant Armenians 
linble to pay this tax in Marsevan, and then the average rate for the other 
cominunities of Rayahs there. At our request a Vixirial letter was once sent 
there, asking for this definite information, but the only reply made to it was 
that no more had been demanded of the Protestants than was justly their due, 

This reply was once, and probably ts still, in the hands of Mr. Stephen 
Pisani, to whom this matter was entrusted by the Ambassador, For tour or 
five months Mr. Pisani continued to assure Stephen Agha that the matter would 
be properly adjusted, but to this day no relief has been had for the poor suffering 
people of that town. 

Substantially the same thing has been done in Moossul, Dimbehr, and many 
other places. 

As another example still of our unprotected condition, we would refer to 
the fact that the firman granted us by the kindness of cur Imperial Sovereign 
the Sultan, has never vet been officially promulgated through the Empire. 

Soon after this firman was issued, it was understood that a promise had 
been given to the Ambassador of its being sent by the Porte to all the Pashaliks 
where Protestants were known to exist; at any rate Mr. Stephen Pisani assured 
Stephen Aga that this would be the case, and for three or four months we were 
encouraged by this hope. At length, however, Mr. Pisani gave us the infor- 
mation that the Porte had decided not to send the firman into the interior, but 
to protect the Protestants by Vizirial letters. This is plainly not carrying out 
the benevolent intentions of our Sovereign. Tt is by no means placing us on 
the same footing with the other subjects of the Porte; they all have their 
regularly appointed agents, backed by Imperial firmans, in every part of the 
country. Visirial letters are usually so worded as to have little real foree, and 
at the best they are ephemeral instruments ; they need to be renewed with 
every change of Pasha, and indeed with every new case of oppression or outrage. 

Our people in the iaterior are gencrally despised, because they are unable 
to show an Imperial firman, and for the same reason over against they numerous 
wen tay and powerful enemies, they are almost never able to maintain their 
rights. 

It is evident from the wording of the firman granted to us at the capital 
that it was never intended by our august Master that the rights and privi exes 
ewe vranted should be confined to the few Protestants living in Constanti- 
nople. 

Constantinople, October 5, 1852.

Inclosure 5 in No. 33. 


Mr. Pisani to Colonel Rose. 


Sir, Pera, November 3, 1852. 

IN pursuance of your deeree on the back of a memorial presented to you hy 
the Protestant Armenian community, under date of the 5th of October, I have 
the honour to state, after un attentive perusal, that its contents appear to me to 
be correct. 

The firman referred to therein was obtained at the express demand, and 
after many exertions on the part of Viscount Stratiord de Redcliffe in October 
1851. 

The provisions of this edict, according to Aali Pasha’s assurances (at that 
time Minister for Foreign Affairs), were to be applicable to the whole Empire, 
and legalized copies of that document were to be communicated to the several 
Governors of the Provinces where Protestant Rayahs existed ; but this promise 
has never been fulfilled; and when I lately, and a little before the Ambassador's 
departure, reminded Aali Pasha of his promise to have the firman promulgated 
throughout the Empire, his Excellency said that the thing could not be done 
without the Sultan’s sanction, and that he would therefore apply for permission, 
which, I am inelined to think, he never nsked, 

With reference to the affair of Marsevan, | have the honour to say that I 
was instructed by the Ambassador to ‘nterfere officiously at the Porte for an 
order to the Pasha of Amasia, under whose jurisdiction Marsevan 18, to remove 
the grievances complained of : and T even obtained a Vizirial letter, copy of whieh 
I then communicated to the Embassy, hut the Governor of Amasia took no 
notice of it. 

Subsequently, when M. Guarracino, Her Majesty's Vice-Consul at Samsoon, 
came to Constantinople, he was requested by Viscount Stratford to call on Aali 
Pasha and the Grand Vizier Reshid Pasha, and stated to them what he knew 
respecting the affair of the house at Marsevan, and the improper conduct of the 
Pasha of Amasia towards the Protestant Armenians. 1 was directed to seoom- 
pany this gentleman, whose representations resulted In 8 final promise from 
these Ministers that orders would be forthwith transmitted to the Governor of 
Amasia to examine the title-deeds said to be in possession of the contending 
parties, and, after duly and impartially examining the documents, to report. 

T afterwards several times called on Aali Pasha, to inquire whether he had 
fulfilled his promise, and whether the Governor of Amasia had sent in his report. 
No satisfactory answer was ever returned. 

Regarding the complaints against the unlawful and arbitrary exaction of 
more Salian than was due by the Protestant Armenians, several app icallons were 
mace to the Porte, which resulted in constant assurances that ean would be 
given, 

I have, &e, 
(Signed) ET. PISANI.

nelosure in No, 34, 
The Rev. H. Dwight to Colonel Rose. 


My der Sir, Orta Keny, November 18, 1852, 

IT is with noemall degree of satisfaction, I assure you, that I have perused the 
contents of your late note, informing me of the successful result of your generous 
efforts in regard to the firman for the Protestant, subjects of the Ottoman Porte. 
Its extension throughout the Empire will be an era in the history of the refor- 
mation in this land, reflecting honour on yourself, who have been the direct 
instrument of so great a blessing, and also upon the Government you represent. 

I confidently hope that by this measure, much suffering for religious 
opinions will be preventer in the provinces of this Empire, and that Protestants 
will now enjoy the same rights and privileges with the other classes of the 
population, 

Allow me, in conclusion, for myself, and in behalf of my colleagues, to con- 
gratulate you on having gained so important a point from the Sultan’s Govern- 
ment, and to assure you of our high appreciation of your energetic and persevering 
efforts in the great and good cause. 

lam, &e.

No. 36. 
Colonel Rose to the Earl of Malmesbury.—( Received January 5 


(No, 5.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 4, 1853. 


IN the sense of your Lordship’s instructions, I have never ceased to urge 
on the Porte to remove causes of complaint existing among the Christians of 
Bosnia and the surrounding districts, by relieving them from unjust taxation, 
doing justice to their wrongs, and allowing them to build churches. T have 
represented to Fuad Effendi that it would be, besides, a fatal military error to 
leave discontented populations on the flanks and rear of the Sultan's troops 
engaged in winter in an arduous contest and in a difficult country, 

"M. de Lavalette has made similar representations, and, with great dis- 
interestedness and good feeling for the Porte, deferred sending M. Wiett, the 
new French Consul at Bosna-Serai to his post, in order that the Porte might 
have time to send an order for the redress of the Christian grievances before 
M. Wiett arrived, and thus exclusively gain the credit for having shown this 
interest for the Christian subjects. ae 

The Porte now informs me that a Tatar has been sent to Kiamil Pasha, 
who is somewhere near Monastir, to proceed instantly to Bosnia, to redress the 
Christian grievances there, and at once, without waiting for the firman, empower 
the Christian communities to build churches. 

Your Lordship would gratify me were you pleased to inform the French 
Government of the very favourable impression produced here by M. de Lava- 
lette’s laudable conduct. 

[ have, &e.

decorum, the parties have overcome their repugnance to that proceeding, and

Inclosure 1 in No. 41. 
Colonel Rose to Consul Longworth, 
Sir, Constantinople, April 2, 1853. 


WITH reference to your despatch No. 1 to me, T have the satisfaction to 
inclose to you a Vizirial letter, with ils translation, to Djemail Pasha of Roumelia, 
Rifaat Pasha has had the kindness to allow me to transmit that letter through 
you to Djemail Pasha, although the matter to which it relates is a purely 
Turkish one. 

I request you to deliver the letter in person to. Djemail Pasha, and to 
impress cn his Excellency, first, that the Grand Vizier declares therein that 
he disapproves the attempts that have been made by the authorities at Monastir 
to force a helpless woman to change what all men ought to value most—their 
religion, His Highness declares that such conduct is opposed to the holy law 
and to the wishes of the Sultan. Secondly, that the Imaum is reproved for his 
conduct to Vasilika, and that “les ordres les plis rigaureux”’ are to be given to 
him to take good care not to repeat such conduct. 

You will have the goodness to arrange unotherally with Djemail Pasha that 
the rules protecting Cliristians when they are examined before Medjlises as to 
their adherence to or renunciation of their faith be strictly adhered to in the case 
of Vasilika; that the head of the Greek Church at Monastir may confer with 
and examine Vasilika as to her supposed intention to quit her Christian faith, 
and that she be told that she stands before the Medjhis a perfectly free agent, 
enjoying the right granted her by a paternal Sovereign to adopt the faith which 
conscience tells her is the best, 

You will also tell Djemail Pasha that the British Representative at the 
Court of his Sovereign deeply regrets that he has to forward a letter to his 
Exeelleney which proves that he has deprived a weak friendless Rayali of the 
liberty of conscience which the Sultan has told his Chvistian subjects is their 
right. And should you be of opinion that it would have a gocd effect on 
Djemail Pasha, and not hurt your position, you may say io him that a desire to 
promote the welfare of the Gultan’s Government has induced me more than once 
to bring to the notice of the Sublime Porte the misrule which has rendered the 
Sultan’s Government unpopular in Roumelia, and nullified His Majesty’s exeel- 
lent intentions in favour of his Christian subjects. And you need not conceal 
trom his Excellency that his Government, knowing that Iam actuated by the 
anxiety of a friend for the good of Turkey, rather than by a spirit of interference 
7 her alfairs, have honoured my representation with their serious attention. 

I request you to inform the Protos Singelos of the Greek Church at 
Monastir, and the Greek clergy at Monastir who addressed you us to the matter 
of Vasilika, of the favourable result of their application to you; adding that 
they will always find Her Majecty's servants ready to ask for justice for the 
Christians when they are deprived of it by misrule or fanaticism; thut we shall 
never be deat’ to their appeal fur mercy and justice; that all we ask in return is, 
that they will continue to live in good neighbourhood with their Mussulman 
countrymen ; and that they will make allowances for the great difiiculties which 
encompass a Sovereign who wishes to illustrate his reign by civilization, and 
who, in spite of the domination of {fanaticism for ages, is steadfastly endeavouring 
to substitute tolerance for bigotry, and mercy for oppression, Finally, you will 
tell them that the wish of Her Majesty’s Government, in giving benevolent. 


45 


assistance to Christians in Turkey, is to render them happy and contented 
subjects under the Sultan, and thereby to strengthen the mutual obligations 
which make the Sovereign the happy father of a grateful people. 
I have, &c.

Memorandum of the Porte to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, dated June 12, 1853. 


(Translation.) 


HIS Imperial Majesty the Sultan, in order to afford a fresh proof of his 
Royal benevolence towards all classes of his subjects, has issued Imperial orders 
adorned with the Royal cipher, addressed to the Greek, Armenian and Catholic 
Patriarchs, to the Protestant Agent, and to the Chief Priest of the Jews, confirming 
their religious privileges, and the rights and immunities belonging to their 
churches and other places appertaining to them. 

Copies of these firmans are inclosed for your Excellency’s perusal.

Inclosure 2 in No, 43. 
Imperial Firman addressed to the Agent of the Protestants. 


(Translation. ) 
TO the Agent of the Protestants, Stephen; may his honours increase ! 


Decreed :-— 

God, the dispenser of mercies, in placing my august person, in pursuance of 
His eternal will, upon the glorious and exalted throne of the Imperial Caliphate, 
having confided to my royal hands and sovereign rule as a sacred and @ special 
deposit, —to him be the thanks and glory,—numerous countries and cities and 
many people of all classes and nations; ever since the glorious day on which | 
ascended the throne, according to the Imperial duties imposed upon the Caliphate, 
my Government has never ceased (by the Divine grace), conformably with 
my Royal will and sincere and benevolent intentions, to give practically their 
constant and careful attention, that all classes of my Imperial subjects should 
enjoy full protection, and that they should be, more especially, one and all, at 
perfect ease with rega rd to their religious and spiritual atlairs. 

The beneficial effects and advantages accruing thereby have been at all times 
manifest, and itis my ardent Imperial desire that no abuses whatever should 
arise out of negligence or carelessness. I therefore wish and require that the 
special concessions cranted by me in favour of my faithful Protestant aubjects, 
concerning their worship and religious affairs and all other concerns relating 
thereto, should be at al times preserved inviolate ; and my peremptory Imperial 
orders have been issued, to wit, that no contravention whatever of those conccs- 
sions should take place, and that any persons acting in contravention thereto 
should know that they will thereby incur my Royal displeasure. 

In order to remove all grounds of excuse on the part of those who should 
he guilty of negligence in these mutters, communication hereof has been made 
to the mecessury muthorities ; and this high decree hus been issued from my 
Imperial Divan, in public confirmation of my Royal intentions, for the complete 
and truthful execution hereof. 

You, the aforesaid Agent, shall, on receipt of these presents, act always in 
conformity with this Imperial order, and be careful in abstaining from an 
contravention thereof, On the occurrence of anything inconsistent with this 
peremptory decision, you will hasten to make immediate representation thereof to 
our Sublime Porte, 

Be it therefore thus made known unto you, giving full eredence to my 
linperial cipher. 

Given in the third decade of the ‘Ilustrious moon of Shaban, in the year 
one thousand two hundred and sixty nine (beginning of June 1853).

1 AVAIL myself of this opportunity to forward sone of two instructions 
addressed by me to the Acting Chief Interpreter of Her Majesty's Embassy, 
respecting the alarms entertained in some parts of the Empire by the Sultan's 
Christian and other tributary subjects ; the comparative want of protection in 
districts from which the regular troops have been necessarily withdrawn ; and 
the number of erimes committed, particularly m the Provinee of Bulgana, by 
‘Vurks suspected of maui 

These instructions, as your | 
uication to the Turkish Government, and | shall not fail to follow them up, if 


necessary, with other steps calculated to enforce the suggestions which they 
contain. 

Apprehensions, not resting, as far as I yet know, on any well-averred facts, 
are entertained in the capital as well as elsewhere, of an intended rising against 
the Christians, and 1 have been urged by some well-informed persons to call up 
Her Majesty's squadron, with a view to their protection. It may before long be 
found advisable to do so, but J am unwilling to resort to such a measure without 
a palpable urgency, considering that it might be construed into an act of menace 
against Russia, and diminish what slender chances still remain of a successful 
negotiation.

Inclosure | in No. 47. 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to Mr. Pisani. 


Sir, Therapia, June 22, 1893. 
YOU will communicate to Reshid Pasha the several extracts of Consular 

reports from Scutari, Monastir, and Prevesa, annexed to this instruction. You 

will observe that they relate in part to those acts of disorder, injustice, and 


- corruption, sometimes of a very atrocious kind, which I have frequently brought 


by your means to the knowledge of the Ottoman Porte. The assurances given 
to me by the late Grand Vizier, the appointment of a more trustworthy Pasha 
at Salonica, and the order for Hazzi Hussein Pasha to repuir forthwith to 
Constantinople, had warranted a hope that the grievances so justly and loudly 
complained of could at length be effectively redressed. But it is with extreme 
disappointment and pain that | observe the continuance of evils which affect so 
deeply the welfare of the Empire, which assume a deeper character of import- 
ance in the present critical state of the Porte’s relations with Russia. You will 
read this instruction to his Highness, you will communicate fully the contents 


uf the accompanying extracts, and you will press upon his mind the urgency of 


adopting adequate measures for the repression of crime and the protection of 
the Sultan's loyal and peaceuble subjects without further delay. Tam aware 
that the Porte’s attention is principally directed, at present, to those defensive 
preparations which cannot with safety be dispensed with, but if cannot be denied 
that the matters naw brought into notice have an immediate bearing on the same 
great interest, and cannot be neglected with safety more than with justice. 

You are at liberty to leave a translation of this instruction with Reshid 
Pasha, and also copies of the annexed extracts, should his Highness appear to 
require them. 


Tam, &e, 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE 
——— 


Inclosure 2 in No, 47. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Mr. Pisani. 


Sir, - Therapia, July 4, 1853. 

| HAVE frequently had occasion of late, and indeed for some years back, 
to bring to the knowledge of the Porte such atrocious instances of cruelty, 
raping, and murder, as I have found with extreme concern in the Consular 
reports, exhibiting generally the disturbed and misgoverned condition of many 
parts of Roumelia, and calling loudly for redress from the Imperial Government. 
The character of these disorderly and brutal outrages may be said with truth to 
be, in general, that of Mussulman fanaticism exeited by -upidity and hatred 
against the Sultan's Christian ‘subjects. T will not say that my friendly and 
earnest representations have been entirely disregarded, On the contrary, | 
have sometimes had the satisfaction of being instrumental towards the repression 
of crime, the alleviation of individual suffering, and the recall of incapable 
magistrates. But the evil, nevertheless, has not been permanently removed, 
and the elect of every partial check has been of short duration, It is evident 


49 


that the present political circumstances of the Empire must tend directly to 
increase the temptations to crime, and to bring a much wider extent of country 
within the range of disorderly and outrageous passiohs. The Mussulmans are 
excited by the prospect of war. ‘The Rayahs, from their numbers, especially in 
the European provinces of Turkey, are objects of fear no leas than of hatred and 
Sung The movement of troops, the calling out the Rediff, the concent tion 
of Regulars on the frontier, the demand of supplies, the scarcity of money, the 
pressure for peer of arrears, the anticipation of falure taxes, coneur to spr 
alarm among the peaceable, and to hold out a clearer prospect of impunity to 
the violent. The Pashas and subordinate Governors are perplexed, individuals 
possessing local wealth acquire a licentious influence, and unless some powerful 
remedies be applied without further delay, it is to be feared that the authority 


of the Central overnment will be completely overpowered in some of the 
provinces, and that the people, despairing of protection, will augment the disorder 
by resorting to lawless means of self- reservation, 

For the sake of the Empire itself no less than from motives of humanity, 
1 am inex, ressibly anxious to engage the Porte’s attention, ere it be too late, to 
this abundant source of evil and danger. Iam well aware of the difficulties 
under which the Government labours, and that it is far more easy to deseribe 
any mischief and to trace it to its causes, than to find an efficient remedy. Yet 
such is the magnitude of the evil, and such the danger of its extension under 
present circumstances, that the necessity of checking its progress, and restoring 
some degree of confidence among the tributary classes, is scarcely subordinate 


to the duty of preparing the means of resistance against an invading foreign 
army. 

In addition to the numerous extracts of my Consular correspondence which 
T have furnished to the Office of Foreign Affairs, with reference to this subject, 
I now inclose for special consideration the intelligence which has reached me 
from an official source in Bulgaria, Reshid Pasha will learn from its contents 
how crying is the evil, how urgent the peril, even in a province where a very 
considerable portion of the regular army 15 being at this moment collected. 

The more pressing and obvious wants are these -—The correction, by means 
of explanation and control, of that fanatical and licentious spirit which now 
‘nflames the Mussulman population; some special means for the protection of 
the loyal and peaceably disposed, whether Museulmah or Rayah ; an efficient 
responsibility on the part of the Local Governors and Magistrates towards the 
Supreme Government ; a more regular aud judicious exercise of authority in the 
collection of supplies, and the direction of persons acting in concert with the 
army ; relief for the labouring and the rural classes, by means of extraordinary 
aids obtained by the Porte from other sources, and applied to the payment of 
the forces, and other immediate wunts of the State. 

_ T abstain from offering any suggestions of a more particular kind, in the 
belief that the Porte is the best judge of its own resources, and in the hope that 
it will adopt every remedial measure to be found within the range of those 
resourees. 

You will leave a copy of this instruction with Reshid Pasha, after reading 
and fully explaining its contents to his Highness ; and you will request that it 
may be brought without delay before His Majesty the Sultan and the Council of 
State, to the end that it may be taken into serious consideration, und that the 
Porte’s decision may be communicated to me for the information of Her 
Majesty's Government. 


Yours, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE. 


No, 48. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon. —( Received July 30.) 
(No, 158.) 


My Lord, Therapia, July 18, 1853, 


RUMOURS, on which I place but little reliance, continue in some measure 
to prevail here of an intended massacre, in which the Turks would be the 
executioners and the Christians the principal victims, There is no doubt that 

Q 


50 


considerable excitement exists among the Mussulman population, and it is more 
likely to increase than to diminish ; but the Porte ig deeply interested in 
preventing any kind of disorders here, and hitherto, with the exception of some 
trifling incidents, the public tranquillity has been admirably preserved. 

n the occasion of a visit which | made to the Capudan Pasha a few days 
ago, on board his magnificent flag-ship, a three-decker, I passed close in front of 
the whole crew, amounting to 1,100 men, and witnessed the exercise of the 
guns, without remarking a single glance or gesture of ill-will, and much gratified 
by the zeal, activity, and skill displayed throughout. ) 

[ have, nevertheless, been careful to provide, as far as I can, against the 
worst, by requesting Admiral Dundas to station a couple of large steam-vessels 
in these waters, and by prevailing on the Sultan to issue an explanatory procla- 
mation calculated to remove erroneous impressions from the minds of the 
people. The execution of this last measure has been delayed, but it is now on 
the point of being carried into effect, and I hope to good purpose. 


T have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe.

My Lord, Foreign Office, Auguat 5, 1853. 
YOUR Excellency will have received from Mr, Consul Saunders a copy of 
his despatch to me, No. 16 of the Oth of July, containing an account of the 
barbarous outrages and exactions to which the Christian subjects of the Sultan 
within his district continue to be exposed; and I now inelose, for your informa 
tion, a copy of a despatch which I have addressed to Mr. Saunders in reply. 
Her Majesty's Gaver’ are well aware of the financial embarrassments 
under which the Porte must now labour, and the subjects of the Sultan must 
make sacrifices for the public service. But it is not be endured that the 
Christians alone should be called upon to make these sacrifices, or that they 
should be enforced by torture and the barbarous treatment described by 
Mr. Saunders, nor must the Porte expect aid from Christian Powers if 
Christianity is to be made the object of insult and oppression by ‘Turkish 
authorities. 


Iam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


(No. 169.) 


My Lord, Foreign Office, August 18, 1853. 

I HAVE to acquaint your Excellency that Her Majesty's Government 
approve the advice respecting the maintenance of order and the protection of the 
Christians in the districts denuded of regular troops, which you have given to 
the Sultan in the memorandum of which a copy 1s maloead in your despatch 

o. 192 of the 4th of August. 

Her Majesty’s Government trust that advice may be adopted; but the 
enactment of good measures will go but little way towards effecting the improve- 
ment that is so much needed, and for which, as well as for retaining the 
sympathy of Hurope now excited in favour of Turkey, the present moment is 
singularly opportune. The Porte should now be most careful in securing 
obedience to its order, and in punishing those who fail in carrying them out, 
particularly as regards the Christians. 

Her Majesty's Government do not doubt that your Exeellency’s vigilance 
will be directed to this point, as a few examples made of delinquents would 
show the local authorities that the Sultan was in earnest, and give to those 
whom they oppress a confidence in the justice of the Porte that unfortunately 
they have not yet had reason to entertain. 


lam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

Inclosure in No. 53. 
The Reyah Protestants to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


(Translation.) 
Most noble Lord, Constantinople, July 1, 1853. 

THE Evangelical Armenian subjects of His Highness the Sultan joyfully 
give thanks to the God of Glory and Merey, who in his good providence affords 
ns such an occasion as this, in which we may have the honour to express our 
heartfelt gratitude to your Excellency for the kind interest you have felt, and 
the unceasing efforts you have made, even from the first, in behalf of our small and 
persecuted community, in order to secure our religious freedom, and establish 
our eivil recognition and existence, 

While Divine Providence, in its merciful and all-wise arrangements, is 
permitting us to constitute an Evangelical or Protestant cominunity, under the 
shadow of the Ottoman Empire, enjoying civil and religious rights and privileges, 
and free in a good measure from violence and oppression, and especially now 
that we are entering upon the enjoyment of still greater rivileges and of firmer 
security, through your Lordship's special efforts and the kindness of our 
Sovereign, we cannot close our lips, or look upon these unspeakable gift swithout 
the deepest sense of gratitude towards the honoured instruments whom Divine 
Providence has selected in order to confer them upon us. 

And truly the grateful bonds which unite the Ottoman Empire to the wise 
and humane Government of Her Britannic Majesty are not felt simply by our 
own community, which, more than all, should be sensible of them, but they 
reach deep into the hearts of all the people of Turkey. 

Turkey lies under great obligations to your Lordship and to the Govern- 
ment of Her Britannic Majesty, not only for the reason w iich has now brought 
the chiefs of its various commiunities to your Lordship’s presence with words of 
thankfulness on their lips and gratitude in their hearts; but for many and older 
reasons too, And thanks be to God that Turkey is not insensible of its obliga- 
tion. It is with great pleasure that we every day hear the people's spontaneous 
expressions of praise, of thankfulness, and of affection, ‘Turkey knows her true 
friend, and her people recognize her sincere well-wisher, We hope that these 
words will not be deemed flattery by your Lordship, for our religion forbids it. 
We only wish to be the interpreters of the people, and we are greatly mistaken 
if the feelings of by far the greater portion, if not the whole, of the people 
of Turkey do not bear witness to the trath of our assertion. 

We rejoice that the new firman bestowed upon the head of our community 
by His Highness the Sultan, has afforded us this opportunity of giving a faint 
expression of our humble attachment and heartfelt gratitude to your Lordship 
and the noble country which your Lordship so honourably represents; and 
remain, &c. 

The head of the Protestant nation, 
(Signed) STEPHEN SEROPIAN, 


The National Representatives and Counsellors, 
AVEDI

. GARABEDEAN.

No. 59. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received March 11.) 


(No, 74.) 
My Lord, Constuntinople, February 15, 1894. 

1 HAVE much satisfaction in stating that the firman which I obtained 
about four years ago, for placing Christian evidence in matters of eriminal juris- 
prudence on the same footing as that of Mahometans, is now to be extende 
throughout the Empire, for the benefit not only of foreign Christians 1 Turkey, 
but for all classes of the Sultan's tributary subjects without exception. When 
the firman was originally ranted to me for the regulation of proceedings im the 
Mixed Criminal Courts of Alexandria and Cairo, | obtained a promise that it 
should be extended, after a time, to the Rayahs of the whole Empire, Hitherto 
my repeated efforts to secure the performance of this promise failed, and the 
present appearance of success is owing to a strong appeal in writing, which I 
addressed to Reshid Pasha several weeks ago. It is altogether paintul to 
observe with what unreasonable tenacity the professors of Islamism still adhere 
to their misapplication of religious principles. In the present instance, however, 
| trust that the prejudice is practically surmounted, and that the firman, caleu- 
lated as it isto remove the most erying injustice under which the Rayahs of 
the Empire have laboured for conturies, will shortly receive the Sultan's sanction, 
in addition to the approval of the Council, with which it has been submitted to 
His Majesty. 

T have, &c. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure 2 in No, 61. 


Regulations. 


(Traduction.) 

MINUTE des Devoirs imposes aux Medjlis (Cours) établis pour la vérification 
exacte et préalable, et le jugement, des Sujets de la Sublime Porte qui se 
geront rendus coupables, les wns elvers les autres, de Crimes tels que, 
Assassinats, blessures, Vols, &2,, ainsi que des auteurs de Crimes sembla- 
bles connus entre Sujets Ottomans et Sujets des Puissances Etrangeres : 


Cette Cour, qui n’aura rien de commun ni avec le Grand Conseil 
Municipal ni avec le Tribunal de Commerce, devant Ctre établie AVinstar de celle 
de la Police de Constantinople, pour vérifier, comme ila été dit plus haut, les 
cireonstances des crimes dont on se sera rendu coupable, se réunira dans certains 
jours fixes de la gemaine; elle sera composee de ceux des membres du Conseil 
du chef lieu de la province qui seront jugés convenables, et d'autres personnes 

‘ane extréme prabité qui seront élues parmi les notabilites honorables du pays, 
et qui sont en état de remplir les obligations de Véquité et de la justice ; et elle 
sera sous la présidence du Vali. 

On procédera lors de examen et du jugement des accuses, dans leur interro- 
patoire, ainsi que dans V'interrogatoire des inc ividus qui donnent des informations, 
avec la plus grande impartialite et la plus grand équite, 

Si is majorité des membres de la Cour a quelques doutes sur la véracité des 
témoins i charge, la Cour leur fera prendre serment, dans les formes usitées, 
comme quoi ils diront la vérité, quwils ne diront rien contre la pure vérite, quils 
ne cacheront point la verité, et qu'ils diront, sans rien omettre, tout ce quils 
savent du cas dont il s’agit ; aprés cela, elle éeoutera ce qu’ils auront a dire sauf 
3 les punir s'il conste qu'ils mont pas déclaré la vérite. 

‘ant qu'il ne sera pas devenu nécessaire que les individus venus pour donner 
des informations soient présens ensemble, ils ne seront pas introdutts tous 
ensemble & la Cour, mais ils seront interrogés séparcment. Aucun deux n'y 
sera introduit pendant qu'un autre subit son interrogation, Les témoins seront 
interrowés en présence de Vaccusé. 

Siil se trouve des personnes se faisant fortes de prouver que les dépositions 
dea témoins & charge ne sont point véridiques, on écoutera aussi ce qu’clles 
aurontadire & cet égard, apres leur avoir fait prendre serment de la maniére sus- 
mentionnée; eb la Cour prononeer conformément & opinion de la majorite de 
sea membres sur la véracité de lune ou de l'autre partic, 

On prendra en considération tout ee qui peut éiublir Vinnocence de accuse 
avec autant de soins qu’on a mis A entendre les dépositions qui ant été faites 
contre lui. 

8] 


28 


Afin de tirer au clair la culpalilité ou l'innocence d'un accusé, c'est-i-dire, 
afin qu’un homme vraiment coupable n’échappe au glaive de la loi, et qu'un 
innocent ne soit puni injustement, on prendra les dépositions des individus de 
toutes nations et milléle, sans aucune exception, qui ont des notions personnelles 
ct particuliéres sur le cas dont il est question seulement pendant que la Cour est 
i examiner l’affaire, et pour qu'elle obtienne toutes les informations positives 
qu'il faut qu'elle se procure, 

On obligera de venir 4 la Cour pour déclarer ce qu'ils sayent, les individus 
qui auront éé désignés par le demandeur ou le défendeur comme ayant 
connaissance du fait dont il s‘agit. 

La Cour aura A prononcer la peine qu’aura encourrue l’accusé, convaincu, 
lors de Vexamen qui a eu lieu en sa présence, de culpabilité suivant le degré de 
sa culpabilité, et conformément aux lois pénales de la Sublime Porte; et elle fera 
exécuter la sentence aprés qui cette sentence aura été confirmée par le Vali. 

Quant aux crimes qui exigent le “kissas” (la peine capitale d’aprés la loi du 
“talion”), ou bien le “diyéte” (prix du sang de "homme tué), la Cour ne décidera 
rien li-dessus, elle ne prononcera aucune sentence ; et elle se bornera a présenter au 
Vali le résultat des srfoe Hon positives qu’elle aura acquises sur l'un ou autre 
de ces deux cas, ct le Vali les mettra sous les yeux du Grand Conseil, qui aura a 
prononcer une sentence conforme aux lois pénales de la Sublime Porte. 

Toute question concernant le sujet d'une Puissanee ¢trangére, soit comme 
plaignant, soit comme accus¢, sera diseutée dans cette Cour en présence du 
Consul du Gouvernement protecteur, ou devant le délégué de ce Consul, ct cela 
en vertu des Traités qui exigent sa présence, et il deyra y avoir aussi au préalable 
son concours et son adhésion, soit pour la sentence qui doit étre prononcée, soit 
pour l’exécution de cette sentence. 

Si individu qui est sous jugement est un sujet d’une Puissance étrangére, 
ou Un sujet Ottoman qui aurait commis quelque attentat contre un sujet ¢tranger, 
l'interrogatoire et le jugement se feront d’apres les Traités en vigueur, devant le 
Consul, ou devent le drogman délégué de sa part, du Gouvernement dont 
Vindividu, soit plaignant, soit accuse, est le sujet. Et le Consul, ou son délégué, 
aura la faculté, tout comme les autres membres de la Cour, dinterroger les 
parties, et de poser des questions aux personnes qui font leurs dépositions, et 
d’exprimer, s'il faut, sa propre opinion et ses réflexions. 

Si la majorité des membres de la Cour est d’avis, d'aprés les informations 
obtenues, et par la force méme des raisons, qu'un accusé est innocent, et ai cet 
accusé n'a pas des antécédens, la Cour représentera au Vali la nécessité que le 
détenu soit mis en liberté. 

Si un accusé s'avoue, de sa propre bonne volonté, coupable, il n’y aura 
rien a dire. Sevlement s'il est constaté qu’en réalite cet individu n'est pas 
coupable, et que l’abus qu'il a fait est uniquement di a des menaces ou A des 
promesses, et que c'est malgré lui, ou pour avoir été induit & erreur, qu’il a fait 
cette confession, un pareil aveu fait & son propre préjudice ne comptera pour 
rien, Et s'il conste, de son propre aveu, qu'il est vraiment coupable, et apres 


cela il prétend qu'on I’a engagé a faire cet aveu, ou bien qu'il l’a fait pour avoir 


été induit A erreur, on ne fera aucun cas de cette assertion. 

A l'occasion de V'interrogatoire et de la punition des coupubles, la Cour ne 
fera usage ni de coups de baton ni de la bastonnade; et surtout elle ne leur fera 
donner aucun genre de question. 

Lorsqu’il sera devenu nécessaire d’arréter quelque individu, il sera mené 
devant une autorité qui décidera sur le champ s'il ya lieu de le mettre sous 
jugement ou s'il faut le mettre en Liberté,

My Lord, Foreign Office, October 21, 1854. 

| HAVE to inform your Excellency that a report has reached Her Majesty's 
Government, that Mehemed Raghib Pasha, the new Turkish Governor at Soutari, 
has commenced persecutions on account of religion, and that he has tortured 
Christians on the accusation of their having once professed Islamism. 

I have desired Mr, Consul Saunders to inquire strictly into this matter, and 
to report to your Excellency whether there is any truth in these charges, and 
whether any instances of cruelty or oppression have occurred on the part of this 
Pasha, or of any Turkish aatlinties in Albania, towards Christian subjects of 
the Sultan; and [have directed Mr. Colquhoun to inform your Excellency whether 
during his mission to Serajevo, or since, any authentic cases have come to his 
knowledge of such barbarities being committed. 

If it should prove to be true that atrocities of this nature have actually been 
pie by the Sultan’s servants, your Excellency will represent their conduct 
in the strongest terms to the Turkish Government, and you will require that the 
officers guilty of such crimes, which are repugnant to humanity and in direct 
opposition to the Sultan’s orders, shall be visited with the severest punishment 
and disgrace.

No, 70. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received December 10.) 
(No. 701.) 


My Lord, Therapia, November 25, 1854. 

I AVAIL myself of the present opponents to forward herewith in copy, 
for the information of Her Majesty’s Government, a circular which | have 
addressed to Her Majesty's Consuls in Turkey, for the purpose of fixing attention 
upon the importance of their contributing, by all proper means, to the enforce- 
ment of four very important measures which, at the suggestion of Her Majesty's 
Embassy, have emanated from the Sultan and hie Government within the last 
few months. . 

The measures to which I refer are, the admission of Christian evidence in 
criminal cases, the repression of outrage, the free exportation of grain, and the 


abolition of traffic in slaves from Georgia and Circassia, 

In justice to the Consuls, I cannot conceal my persuasion that they might 
have been safely left, in this instance, to the working of their own sense af duty. 
But an official communication of the firmans and other documents relating: to 
the measures in question, and a formal neers susceptible of being cited in 


a .. ~- 


62 


their eventual discussions with the local authorities, can hardly fail to strengthen 
their hands, and to render their exertions more efficient in those matters which 
do not come under a right of formal interference on our part. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure 1 in No. 70. 
Circular. 


Sir, Therapia, November 20, 1854. 

WITHIN the last few months four important measures, emanating from 
the Turkish Government, and calculated, if enforced with suitable energy, to 

roduce a large amount of benefit to this Empire, either by their immediate or 
their eventual consequences, have been communicated, by command of the 
Porte, to Her Majesty’s Embassy. 

By the first of oe measures, a signal improvement has been introduced 
into the procedure of criminal jurisdiction throughout the Ottoman Empire. 
New securities have been given to the protection of innocence and for the 
discovery of guilt in trials of that description ; and, above all, the testimony of 
witnesses to whatever class of the Sultan's subjects they may belong, has been 
thereby placed on a footing of perfect ec uality. 

The second is directed against the disorders and acts of eruel violence but 
too frequently perpetrated by irregular soldiery im the Porte’s service, or b 
unemployed individuals abandoned to the indulgence of their passions 
addicted to plunder and sanguinary erime. 

The third has established the removal of every kind of restriction attached 
of late to the free exportation of grain from the ports of Turkey. 

The fourth, and not the least beneficial, is a complete interdiction of the 
traffic in slaves from Georgia and Circassia. 

As Her Majesty’s Government have taken a deep interest in the adoption 
of these measures, they cannot but feel an earnest desire to have them carried 
seriously and permanently into effect, to the end that their salutary results may 
be thoroughly realized, not only for purposes of general humanity, but also for 
the welfare and improvement of an Empire whose preservation in a right spirit is 
now the leading principle of European policy. | 

To the reap pishcient of this grand object, I conceive that your watchful 
attention and the exercise of your unbiassed influence may be essentially 
conducive. ‘The Turkish provincial authorities, on whom, in the first instance, 
devolves the faithful enforcement of the measures in question, have some 
traditional prejudices to overcome, and many adverse interests to withstand, in 
the execution of their duty. Ata distance from the seat of Government, they 
naturally stand in need of support, admonition, and encouragement; vor, in the 

resent most intimate and cordial state of our relations with Turkey, can aids of 
this kind descend to them from a better source than from youand your colleagues 
elsewhere, animated, as no doubt you are, by the spirit of that alliance which 
holds the two countries together in close co-operative union. 

T ought, however, to remind you that the most friendly interference in those 
matters which immediately concern the internal affairs of the Empire require to 
be condueted with prudence and a ee respect for rights of sovereign or of loc 
jurisdiction. Your vigilance may be exercised, your advice may be given, the 
language of remonstranee even may be used, without transgressing the limits of 
propriety, and, in case of failure, your representations to the Government at 
some, and also to the Embassy here, will afford that prospect of redress which, 
though more distant, may not on that account be of less certain effect. 

Copies of the firmans, ‘nstructions and notes relating to the four measures, 
respectively, are inclosed herewith, as well in the original ‘Turkish as in English 
or French translation. 

They would have been sent to you at an earlier period had not some 
obstacles, difficult to remove In a moment, been occasioned by incidents on 
which it is not necessary to dwell. 

Although the measures themselves are already known to you and to the 


63 
public at large, the present Circular will reach you at a time when some 
experiehe af their working will enable you to act at once with practical 
judgment and efficacy in furtherance of their enforcement. 


Lam, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure 7 in No. 70. 
Note of the Porte to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe, September 24, 1854. 


(Translation.) 

A 'TEMPORARY prohibition had been issued against the export of ou 
from certain places pointed out, until the army arrangements should «be 
completed for obviating any difficulttes on the score of supplies to the Turkish 
and the auxiliary troops, and this restriction was communicated at the time to 
all the Foreign Legations at Constantinople. 

The arrangements for supplying the Turkish and the auxiliary forces of the 
‘Illustrious Powers will have been completed by this. The Imperial troops in 
Roumelia have gone into the Principalities, and the auxiliary armies and fleets 
have proceeded to their destination; there are not many troops remaining at 
van and the prolongation of the restriction might bring on penury in every 

irection. 

The time has therefore come for removing this restriction altogether. Fis 
Majesty the Sultan has, therefore, ordered that every one should be at liberty 
to purchase grain, after proper provision shall have been made for seed and for 
local consumption, and on condition that the arrangements for supplying the 
Imperial army are in no way disturbed. 

This is everywhere made known, and is communicated to your Excellency 
as well as to the other foreign Representatives.

Inclosure 8 in No. 70. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Reshid Pasha. 


Therapia, September 25, 1854, 

THE Undersigned has received the Porte s official note dated yesterday, 
announcing the cessation of the temporary restriction which for certain specified 
reasons bad been laid on the exportation of gram from Turkey. He has received 
this notification with pleasure, being convinced that the continuance of the 
prohibition would have been uselessly prejudicial to the interests of commerce 
and the obligations of treaty. : te 

The Undersigned observes that although the general prohibition has ceased, 
which it necessarily would have done of itself, as it was originally adopted only 
for the grain of last year’s prods: certain reservations are still made as to 
the purchase of that article for exportation. It is therefore his duty to state that 
all restrictions on the exportation of grain, as uppD any other article of Turkish 
yroduce, are contrary to the stipulations of the ommercial Treaty, and only to 

e justified by special necessity and during the undoubted existence of that 

necessity. If the Porte's note is to be understood in its literal sense, the ‘Turkish 
authorities are still instructed to prevent the purchase of grain for exportation, 
until they are satisfied that provision 18 made for local consumption, seed, and the 
arrangements for supplying the Imperial army. As this state of things would 
open a wide door to arbitrary proceedings and exclusive s ulations, it is 
desirable that some further explanation should be afforded, an that whatever is 
really objectionable in those respects should be removed. 

The Undersigned, Xe. 

(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

Inclosure 9 in No. 70. 
Reshid Pasha to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


Translation.) October 5, 1864. 

I HAVE received your Excellency’s reply to the cireular communication 
made to the foreign Representatives announcing the abolition of the prohibition 
on the export of grain from the Imperial States. 

It was not meant by the passages in the Porte’s note, in which it is said 
that the arrangements for supplying the Imperial army and for meeting local 
exigencies were to be looked after, and that the necessary quantity of grain for 
seed was to be reserved, to put any limitation on the export of grain from the 
Ottoman States. The object was to communicate to your Excellency the 
instructions of the Porte to certain of her authorities to provide for the wants of 
the people, and the necessary quantity of grain for seed, conformably with their 
duty, and to see that any arrangements or contracts for supplying corn to the 
Imperial or to the auxiliary armies should not be impaired. 

In repeating to your Excellency that the prohibition on the export of grain 
is abolished generally throughout the Empire, [ have, &c.

Inclosure 2 in No. 71. 
Consul Moore to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe, 
My Lord, Beyroul, January 2, 1855, 


I HAD the honour to receive yesterday, by the Austrian steamer vid Alex- 
andria, your Excellency’s despatch of the 12th ultimo, instructing me to inquire 
of the Pasha of Saida whether the firman respecting Christian evidence is now 
officially in his Excellency’s possession. 

1 have the honour to state that on making this inquiry, Wameck Pasha 
replied that he had received the above-mentioned firman. [I learn from him 
privately, that the firman only arrived on Friday last, the 29th ultimo, and that 
he intends giving it publicity this week. 3 

My interpreter Kava this day called wpon Wameck Pasha to ask for an 
early answer to my inquiry, his Excellency took occasion to remark that he did 
not take the firman in the absolute sense which Europeans seem to attach to 
it; for instance, the Pasha conceives it as applicable only to cases heard at the 
Grand Medjlis of the Eyalet—the criminal and commercial courts—but not at the 
alabemig , nor even at the Grand Medjlis of the Eyalet, when the Cadi presides 

ere, 

It ia therefore important that I should observe that in all suits or claims 
involving the tenure and transfer of houses and lands, inheritance, and liquidation 
of estates, Christian evidence, in mixed cases with Mahometans, would not be 
received under the firman, nor is it at present accepted. 

Lhave, &. 


(Signed) N, MOORE,

No. 73. 
Consul Plowden to the Earl of Clarendon.— (Received February 13, 1855.) 


(No. 41.) 
My Lord, Massowah, November 29, 1854. 


THE law apparently not permitting in any case, at least as it is interpreted 
at Massowah, that Christians should witness against a Mussulman, a serious 
inconvenience may arise. European residents here have none but Christian 
servants in their establisnments ; a Mussulman is therefore at liberty to enter and 
do what he pleases, as conviction as impossible. Nor is this an imagimary case, 
as | have more than once been troubled by this regulation, if it exists. 

Nor is your Lordship to suppose that any exception is made in courtesy or 
otherwise in regard to Consular Officers here: on the contrary we are continually 
referred to the Cadi, the Mufti, and the Koran. : 

Having pointed out this, I wish to remark, in case of ay law being passed 
in Turkey that shall admit Obristians of all denominations and nations to witness 
in a Mahometan Court of Justice, which I hear is probable, that unless the 
Abyssinian Christians be specially named, no phrase, however comprehensive, 
will induce the rulers of Massowah to admit his claim to that distinction. 


I have, &c. 
(Signed) WALTER PLOWDEN.

Inclosure in No. 75. 
Consul Longworth to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Monastir, January 25, 1855. 

1 HAVE more than once had occasion to represent to your Excellency the 
prevalence of the crime of abduction (1 mean the forcible abduction of Christian 
women by Mussulmans) in this province. Three districts, viz., Kopruly, Tikvish, 


# No. 73. 


72 


and Perleppe, have, by addition to such practices, rendered themselves peculiarly 
infamous, 

Within the last month five cases have occurred. They are not all of 
them, as may naturally be supposed, cases of simple abduction: the criminality 
is shared sometimes by more than one party. There may be profligacy on the 
side of the women or that of their parents, who, moreover, from sordid conside- 
rations too common among the Bulgarian peasantry, make their daughters lead 
a life of drudgery instead of giving them in marriage. Be the causes, however, 
what they may, the apology as well as the cloak for all these offences, and conse- 
quently an encouragement to them, is religion, All these women are brought 
to make a profession of the Mahometan faith before the Pasha and the Medjlis, 
who if they had any real respect for it, could not fail to see that no greater 
insult could be offered to that religion, 

With a view of checking these disorders, I had some time ago insisted that 
the women thus dragged hither should, previously to their being taken to the 
Medjlis, be consigned to the care of the Greek Bishop for three days. The 
effect of this arrangement, to which the Pasha consented, had in some measure 
answered my expectations. With a single exception these poor women, no 
longer under the influence of terror or a worse motive, had refused to abjure 
their religion—a result at which great umbrage seems to have been taken by 
some fanatic and eyil-disposed persons of the Medjlis. 

On the 15th instant another woman, who had been carried off by force 
more than three weeks before in the district of Perleppe, was brought by a 
Turk to the Serai at Monastir, for the purpose of making her apostatize. My 
dragoman, who was present, requested that she should be sent to the Bishop’s 
residence for three days, as had been agreed upon; but the fanatic members of 
the Medjlis would not allow of it, alleging that intimidation had been resorted 
to by the Bishop towards the women who had been previously sent thither. 
After some discussion the Pasha, by way of compromise, had the woman 
conducted to the British Consulate, where, he said, he knew she would not be 
subjected to restraint or coercion. She was accordingly brought there veiled as 
a Turkish woman; but though at first she obstinately persisted in declaring 
herself a Mahometan, yet after an interview with her sister and mother she 
finally relented, and resumed the dress of her nation. 1 found on inquiry that 
she had been treated with great barbarity, On the day of her marriage two 
Turks had entered her husband's habitation with drawn yataghans, and carried 
her off in her wedding clothes, with the ornaments on her person constituting 
her dower to the value of 600 piastres. Two persons were wounded in the 
scuffle. She had been afterwards, for the space of three weeks, dragged from 
village to village, and so much misused and terrified, that even after being 
restored to her parents, and assured of her salety within the walls of the 
Consulate, she continued to express her belief that they would all of them be 
murdered if she publicly declared herself'a Christian, 

The atrocious nature of these details, forcibly illustrating the general 
character of these proceedings, determined me to take decided steps with respect 
to them, 

On the day therefore appointed for the judgment of this affair, I did not 
permit the woman to be taken to the Serai, but went to the Pasha myself, and 
told him before the Medjlis that I should thenceforward consider all such 
questions not as questions of a religious but criminal nature; that it was most 
disgraceful to aceept of proselytism which was the result of seduction, profligacy, 
outrage, and intimidation. I demanded therefore that in all such cases, for the 
future, the ravishers, whether principals or abettors, who brought women to 
make Mussulmans of them, should be seized and punished in the most exem- 
plary manner, and the women restored to their husbands or parents; that if they 
were produced at all before the Medjlis, it should not be for the purpose of 
declaring themselves Mussulmans or Christians (which was, under the cireum- 
stances, a crucl mockery), but simply as witnesses against the men by whum 
they had been trepanned or violated. In conclusion, | urged them to come to 
an understanding on the spot, and not compel me to refer the matter to Con- 
stantinople, which might cause great seandal, and, at this juncture, no little 
embarrassment to ther own Gavernment, 

These observations were apparently taken in good part by the Pasha and 
the Medjlis; and I left them under the impression that they had entirely 


id 


acquiesced in the course recommended to them. After my departure, pomer 
the fanatic party still held out, and a scene of violent altercation ensued, ‘he 
frmans and Vizirial letters that had been promulgated at different times with 
reference to these matters were then produced, and, read by the Pasha; and J 
am told that one of them (constructively at least) bore out and justified the 
views | had expressed on the subject. I may incidentally take the liberty of 
observing that firmans of this nature, which it may have cost your Excellency 
much trouble to obtain, are, unless Her Majesty's Consuls be simultaneously 
advised of their being issued, very likely to be suppressed in the provinces. 

My authority on this point, as well as in regard to the debate in ue 
Medjlis, was the Greek Bishop. 1 received, likewise, @ message from the 
Pasha, entreating me not to persist in my demands, but to allow the woman, 
who had been sent to the Consulate, to make her profession of faith before the 
Medjlis, The Pasha’s weakness on this occasion seemed at first unaccountable, 
but | was soon enlightened with respect to it by a confidential communication 
from a trustworthy quarter. Your Excellency will, | doubt not, concur mn the 
sropriety of secresy with respect to my informant, who 1s no other than Halil 

ey, the most influential member of the Medjlis. He let me know privately 
that a seditious movement was to be apprehended on the part of the Mussulman 
population, and that Vedgee Effendi, the leader of the fanatical party, had been 
inciting them in order to overawe and control the authorities. He had 
convoked an assembly of Imaums, Softas, and a mob of other Mussulmans 
in the principal mosque (which gathering for religious” objects uf oe a 
“djumhour”), and proclaimed to them that Islamism was in jeopardy, ang their 
rizhts and privileges were sacrificed to the Ginours. It was in consequence ol 
this demonstration that the Pasha had entreated me not to press my demand in 
the abduction aflair. I saw the danger, however, of yielding to pressure of 
this description, and felt that if a stand were not made, we had little further to 
hope in the interests of order, progress, and Christan emancipation here. 

I accordingly waited upon my colleagues the French and Austrian Consuls, 
and, having secured their cordial cooperation, proceeded with them to the Berai, 
where we laid before the Pasha the information I had received as to the 
movement, and give it as our opinion that the author of it should be punished 
by immediate expulsion from the Medjlis, The Pasha and the Defterdar, by 
whom he was attended, both frankly acknowledged (a most important admission 
on their part) that the accounts I had reeeived and the dangers to be appre- 
ended, were by no means exaggerated ; but they hesitated to comply with our 
suggestions ‘n regard to Vedgee Etfendi, whereupon we went so far as to 
intimate that it might be a question with us how far we were justified in keeping 
our flags up in a place where the Sultan’s authority was in abeyance, and his 
firmans could no longer be executed. |, of course, had no intention of adopting 
an extreme measure on my own responsibility, but the hint was sufficient for 
the Pasha, But on that person presenting himself with his habitual air of 
insolence; he relapsed into his indecision, and seemed incapable of addressing a 
word to him. I asked him, therefore, whether he would communicate what had 
heen determined upon, or wished me to do it for him. He begged me, by all 
means, to speak for him, {did so as briefly as possible, telling Vedgee Effendi 
that he was no longer to consider himself a member of the Medjlis, whose 
independence und authority he had sought to subvert by an appeal to the 
passions of the populace, I recommended him, also, to be earetul how he 
committed himnself by the slightest word or action of an inflammatory tendency, 
as he would be made to answer for any disorder or commotion in the town. 

[am happy to inform your Excellency that the disgrace of Vedgee Efendi 
has been so far productive of the best results. Orders have been transmitted to 
the District Mudirs to discountenance and dismiss on the spot all cases of torced 
proselytism, and to punish all parties concerned in them. | : 

Vedgee Effendi has met with little sympathy, even among the Turks, His 
rancorous and tyrannical disposition, for the indulgence of which the state of 
society unhappily offered him too many facilities tn regard to the Rayals, has 
not served to propitiate the Mussulmans, a 

It. is not nBLKCH however, that in tines like the present he may find an 
opportunity for doing mischief, to which he is naturally prone. | 

I am of opinion, with my colleagues that le should be expelled from the

rebellion, would fully justify the measure ; and the French and Austrian Consuls 
have written to this effect to their respective Legations at Constantinople,

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No. 197.) 


My Lord, Foreign Office, March 7, 1855. 

HER Majesty's Consul at Monastir has transmitted to me a copy of a 
despatch which he addressed to your Excellency on the 25th of January, on the 
subject of the forcible abduction of women, and their persecution by Mussulmans 
in the district of his Consulate ; and | now transmit for your information a copy 
of a despatch® which has been addressed to Mr. Consul Longworth, approving 
his firm and judicious conduct. } 

Mr. Longworth's report shows how little reliance can be placed on the 
Sultan’s firmans, or the measures said to be taken for the reform of abuses. 

In the present instance it is clear that but for the judicious and determined 
intervention of Her Majesty's Consul, atrocities against Christians worthy of the 
worst times of Ottoman history would have been perpetrated, not only with 
impunity, but with the sanction of the highest Turkish authority in the district; 
and this state of things, revolting as it is, and hopeless as it appears to he, 
assumes a most serious aspect now that the question has to be considered of 
reconciling the independence of the Sultan with the amelioration of the condition 
of his Christian subjects. ‘The one is a political obligation, the other isa duty 
imposed upon us by religion and by Christian charity; and if com elled to 
choose between the two, there can be no doubt on which side our option must 
lie; but they do not aoe be irreconcileable if proper measures for that object 
are taken and enforced. 

If, however, the Turkish Government are unable to control, or are unwilling 
to punish, Turkish authorities who set the Sultan's decrees at defiance, the Sultan 
will have no cause of complaint against the Christian Powers when they determine 
themselves to protect their co-religionists, and to carry out the benevolent 
intentions towards all classes of his subjects that the Sultan professes, but 1s 
powerless to execute. 

{ have to instruct your Excellency to make known the contents of this 
despatch to the ‘Turkish Government in whatever way you may think best caleu- 
lated to produce the desired effect. 

Lam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

Consul Muore to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Beyroul, March 8, 1855. 

EMIR BESHIR KASSIM SHEHAB, late Governor of Mount Lebanon, 
recently applied to me for aid to recover a property near Beyrout, which, during 
his absence at Constantinople in 1842, was seized upon, and has since been hel 
in possession by Mustafa Er-Rufiay, a Mahometan belonging to a religious 
order. 

The case is stated in the inclosed copy of the Emir’s letter to me, to which 
L annex a translation. : 

[ beg to draw your Excellency’s attention to the statement in the letter, 
that the present usurper had hired the land from the Emir, and paid him @ rent, 
as proved by a bond written by a Mussulman notary of Beyrout, and corroborated 
by Mahometan witnesses, who cannot now be induced to give their evidence 
before the Court. . ai 

As the Emir states that the Grand Medjlis of the Pashalic had refused to 
entertain his claim, because he had only Christian evidence to offer in support 
thereof, | advised him to make a direct application to Wameck Pasha, and urge 
on his Excellency’s consideration the difficulty, or rather the impossibity, on his 
part of inducing Mahometans to bear evidence in the case, as Sheik Mustafa 
Er-Rufady now pretends the land belongs to a mosque, ‘The Pasha replied to 
the Emir that although Christian evidence is accepted against Mahometans at 
the Criminal Court, yet in civil suits, which must be heard before the Cuar either 
at the Mehkemé or al the Medjlis, the testimony of Christians could not be 
received, 

ee Wameck Pasha having made a similar statement to me, as reported in my 
despatch of the 2nd January last, relative to Christian evidence in civil suits, | 
deemed it superfluous to renew the question on this point, 

But as a general impression prevailed among the Christians in Syria that 
the admission of Christian evidence was to extend to the protection of property 
as well as to life, L have thought it expedient to submit the present case to your 
Excellency. 

I venture at the same time most respectfully to observe, that so long as 
this important right ‘; denied to the Christian subjects of the Sultan, the efforts 
of the Turkish Government, or of the Agents of their allies, must always be 
unsuecess(ul in securing the sympathy and loyalty of the Christian population of 
the Empire. On this ground I have taken the liberty of bringing the uffair to 
your Excellency’s notice, as one well calculated to exemplify the insecurity of 
the tenure of property held by Christians in the Turkish dominions. 

have, &e, 


(Signed) N. MOORE.

Emir Beshir Kassim to Consul Moore. 


(Translation) 
(After the compliments.) Sebney, near Beyrout, March 8, 1855. 

| HAVE a property, consisting of a piece of land, near the mosque 
El-Ouzai, bought from my cousin, Emir Abbass Assaad, upwards of thirty years 
ago. My cousin owned this property by inheritance from his forefathers, who 

ossessed it from time immemorial. We have always disposed of this piece of 
tnd in every legal manner. 

During my stay in Constantinople in 1842, Sheik Mustafa Er-Ruthay began 
to dispose, by violent usurpation, of the land, alleging as a pretext when asked, 
that it belongs to the mosque. | 

On my return to this country I was informed by the overseer of my property 
of this proceeding on the part of Sheik Mustafa, Out of deference, however, to 
the constituted authorities, I refrained from putting a stop to this interference 
hy means of my own attendants, and [ ultimately addressed to his Excellency 
the Mushir of the Eyalet a petition, bringing to his notice the above act of 
violence. His Excellency reterred my memorial to the Grand Medjlis. My 
agent presented himself before the Court, accompanied by witnesses, to corro- 
borate the fact of my having constituted him my Vakeel (Procurator) for the 
prosecution of this affair. The Court declined to entertain the affair in any way 
whatever, until I should send Mussulmans to bear witness as to the validity of 
the procuration, because the affair is to be heard against a Mussulman. [tis 
impossible for me to send Mussulmans to bear evidence either as to the appoint- 
ment of the Vakeel to represent me, or to prove the act of usurpation in this 


“The Mussulmans who had borne evidence to a bond of Sheik Mustafa 
Er-Rufady, to whom I had farmed the land in question some time before m 
departure for Constantinople, had actually ventured to disclaim and deny their 
evidence, in order to assist my opponent to succeed in defrauding me. 

Although my case is a8 clear as the sun at noonday, I am in danger of 
losing my property altogether, because I cannot get Mussulmans to be witnesses, 
and the evidence of a great number of respectable old Christian citizens who 
know all the truth in this matter, is rejected. 

I am therefore under the necessity of bringing this affair to your notice, 
leaving it contidently under your benevolent consideration, for ] am persuaded 
of vour wish to support the ends of justice and right ina similar case, 

May God prolong your days! } 
(Signed) BESHIR SHEHAB.

No. 84. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved May 4.) 


(No. 310.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, April 23, 1855. 

AGREEABLY to your Lordship's instructions, | have appt to Saffet 
Effendi and also to Reshid Pasha, in order to obtain an official declaration as to 
the admission of Christian evidence in Turkish Civil Courts of Justice. The 
Grand Vizier, referring to the case brought forward by Mr. Moore, expressed his 
conviction that the Pasha of Saida had committed an error, and the Secretary of 
State declined giving an official answer until he had communicated with his 
colleagues. Both thought it necessary to ascertain what new regulations are in 
course of adoption by the Reform Council. 

What I understand to be the present state of the case is this: the Porte has 
not yet introduced any general alteration of its fundamental laws with respect 
to Christian evidence in civil cases; but it has for some time past adopted a 
system of evasion, by which lawsuits involving Christan interests are submitted 
to Mixed Courts, where the evidence is not confined to Mussulmans, and cases of 
that kind are no longer referred to the Mehkemé, or Turkish tribunal, the 
procedure of which ts strictly regulated by Turkish law. ‘This exceptional mode 
of proceeding obtains principally in the capital, and also in some provincial cities, 
such as Smyrna and, I believe, Beyrout; but its application has nol been 
generalised. Other and more complete amendments of the law are now in 
contemplation, and indeed in progress also. The supposed error of the Pasha of 
Saida probably was that he referred the Emir Kassim's claim to the Mehkemé 
instead of having it judged by a Mixed Tribunal. 

[shall return to this subject on a future occasion, 

I have, &e. | 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,
