No, 85. 


Memorandum respecting Christians in Turkey —( Communicated by Lord Stratford 
de Redeliffe to Lord John Russell on the 19th of April, with reference to the 
Fourth Point.) 


ANY one who has looked into Turkish history must know that several 
classes of population, or rather distinet races, differing in religion as well as in 
origin and language, but intermingling to a certain degree with each other, 
inhabit the Sultan’s dominions. they are all, with few exceptions, subject: to 
Mussulman law. They are, in general, amenable to the same courts of justice, 
whether civil or criminal, as their Mussulman fellow-subjects. In all that 
relates to life, property, and personal liberty, they are governed, ultimately, by 
the same authority. There are cases, however, where in principle, but more 
frequently in practice, important differences exist in the application of the laws 
to the Mussulman on one side, and to the Rayah on the other. 

Of Christians, the most influential and numerous classes are the Greeks and 
Armenians; the former located more generally in Europe, the latter in Asia. 

The Greeks, in their political relation to the Porte, are considered with 

reference to their religion, and comprise other races besides their own, the 
Slavonians, for instance, and the Bulgarians, ‘There are shades of difference 
between the component parts of this community; but the Sultan’s Government 
takes little notice of them. The Greek Patriarch residing at Constantinople, 
and assisted by his Synod of Archbishops, appointed with independent forms, 
but subject to Turkish confirmation, is the oven through which the regular 
authority of the Porte is exercised over the Greek, or Romatle, population, His 
Holiness, as he is styled, and also the Bishops, each im his diocese. retain some 
vestiges of original jurisdiction over their respective dependents They can 
impose regulations, levy contributions, and punish, within certain limits, by fine 
and imprisonment, in practical subordination, however, to the Turkish magis- 
trate. In country towns and rural districts, the notables (Kodja Bashis) are 
answerable for the good behaviour of their dependents, and it is to them, when 
supplies of any kind are wanted, that the Turkish authorities address their 
requisitions in the first instance. These privileges of national authority, together 
with others of a more popular description, such, for instance, as the rights of 
separate worship, of repairing churches, of collecting their own taxes, and 
uniting some laic representatives with the Synod on certain occasions, are 
enjoyed in virtue either of ancient firmans or of the modern charter, called 
a Tanzimat Hairiyé,” which is applicable to the Rayah as well as to the 
Mussulman. 
___ The first Iinperial firman conferring privileges on the Greek Church and 
nation emanated from Mahomet the Second, immediately after the capture of 
Constantinople. It is addressed to the Head of the Greek Church, and renewed 
on the appointment of every successive Patriarch. Its principal Tovisions are 
stated in a separate paper annexed to this memorandum.* ‘The Danubian 
Principalities, although they may recognise the Patriarch of Constantinople as 
the Head of their Church, are pechanlly under the spiritual jurisdiction of their 
respective Mctropolitans, 

dvantages similar to those enjoyed by the Greeks have been successively 
extended, in a similar form, to the Armenians, to the Jews, to the Roman 
Catholic Armenians, who have a separate spiritual chief, and finally to the 
dissenters from all, under their generic denomination of Protestants. 

The several religious communities, which are considered subordinately, in a 
national point of view also, have separate places of worship and separate places 
of burial. Their internal authorities are also separate, and the quarters which 
they inhabit are not indeed exclusive, but tending to separate aggregation, 

The Israclites, owing to their peculiar tenets, customs, and habits, are more 
completely separate than the Christians, and their tenure of privileges may not 
be exactly the same as that of the Rayahs. With respect to the Tanzimat, all, 
whether Mussulmans or Rayahs, are, in theory, on the same footing. 

Tn parts of Syria and Koordistan, there are portions of population which 
do not class with the large communities, but maintain, either in fact or by the 
Porte’s recognition, a distinct existence. Of these, the more known are the 


* See Ubicini's * Lettres sur ta Turquie,” vo!. il, p. 485. 


‘iG aa 


a 


io ee 


ar 
& 


2 


80 


Maronites, the Druses, the Metualis, the Nestorians, the Jacobites, and the 
Chaldeans. Some, as the Maronites and Druses, are allowed to enjoy a separate 
jurisdiction, regulated by express enactment, and virtually held under foreign 
protection. 

Comprising the Rayahs in one general view, it may be stated with sufficient 
exactness that they all live under Turkish authority, in most of the leading 
respects under Turkish law, with the enjoyment, varied as to some minor sects, 
of similar privileges, similarly derived. 

Under this system, if they enjoy some advantages extended and confirmed 
by the improved spirit of administration accompanying the adoption of the 

unzimat, they are still subject to legislative hardships which not only make 
them a degraded cast, but deprive them of all reliable security in point of life 
and property. 

[t is true that, in common with the Turks, they are no longer subject to 
arbitrary confiseations, foreed contributions, or summary punishments. They 
are exempt from military service ; they are no longer marked by any distinctive 
dress ; they are occasionally employed in the Civil and Diplomatic service of the 
Porte; their representatives are not excluded from the Provincial Councils. 

But in other respects they do not partake of the civil rights enjoyed by 
their Mussulman fellow-subjects, over whom they are not permitted to exercise 
the slightest authority or jurisdiction. : 

They are subject to the exclusive payment of haratch, a tax of onerous 
effect and odious origin, practically mitigated by the present mode of collection, 
but operating as a barrier between the Mussulman, whom it inflates, and the 
Christian, whom it degrades, 

Till lately, they could not give evidence in Courts of Law against a Mus- 
sulman, either for accusation or for defence, and the evidence of Mussulmans 
against them was held to be decisive; the law, which has relieved them from 
this enormous injustice, is still but partially known in the provinees, and even 
when known, but lightly enforced. 

In point of property they are not yet placed on equal terms with the 
Mussulman. ‘The latter can hold and purchase land and houses wherever he 
likes. The Christian can neither purchase nor succeed to real property in a 
Turkish quarter. 

In matters of inheritance, the Turkish law prevails. Whatever the deceased 
proprietor may leave, is divided into three portions, two for the heirs male, one 
for the female, and of each portion the subdivisions are equal in either case, the 
heirs being more than one. 

‘The consequence of this unfair and tyrannous domination are not confined 
to the mere execution of the law; they perpetuate the inferiority of the sub- 
jugated classes, and engender never-failing sentiments of contempt on the one 
side, and of hatred on the other, Hence flow evils in practice which far exceed 
the pretensions of the law, and which, in their turn, are causes of suffering and 
crime, of disorder and weakness, injurious to individuals, but still more fatal to 
the State. 

Legislative amendments of a very important bearing are in contemplation, 
and are partly, indeed, adopted, though not yet promulgated, by the Sultan and 
his Government. Administrative regulations favourable te the Rayahs are also 
on the eve of being published. 

The law is to be rendered equal in operation to all parties. The Civil 
service of the Porte is to be opened to the Christians as high as the five classes ; 
what is called Bala, or the eminent, and the grade of Mushir, being still 
reserved. The haratch is to be abolished in principle as well as in name, and a 
war tax to be substituted. Christians are to be admissible to the army, and to 
hold rank as high as that of Colonel. The right of building new churches, on 
demand, is to be added to that of repairing the old ones. ; 

These, no doubt, are precious and salutary concessions; but mere enact- 
ments, however necessary as foundations, will never suffice to cure the deep- 
rooted evile originating in fanaticism and conquest, confirmed by rehgious legis- 
lation, and perpetuated by the habits and traditions of several unbroken centuries. 

It is absolutely necessary that the Porte should be required, in respeettul 
forms but with a decided intention, to organize the means of giving effect to its 
improved legislation and new administrative measures, and to extend its amended 
system to matters of property. It is, moreover, indispensable that the Porte 


81 


hould be made to feel, in some effective and durable manner, her responsibility 
eaters the allied Powers of Christendom. Those Powers have set aside all 
distinctions, in order to rescue the Ottoman Empire from an imminent peril, 
and to afford its Sovereign an opportunity of reviving its impaired strength, by a 
system of judicious and effective reform. The Sultan, by lane: himself of the 
occasion thus generously offered, and by assuming his place de nitively among 
the leading Powers of Europe, will best respond to their wishes, and discharge a 
debt of gratitude which he cannot leave unpaid without exposing them as well 
as himself to the most disastrous consequences.

Aali Pasha to M. Musurus—(Communicated to the Earl of Clarendon by 
M. Musurus, May 9.) 
Monsieur, Vienne, le & Mai, 1855. 


VOUS trouverez ci-jointe en copie une dépéche de Lord Clarendon. Elle 
vient de m'étre communiquée par le Comte de Westmorland, en réponse a un 
mémoire que j’avais présente ean Jobn Rusgell 4 mon arrivée 4 Vienne. 

Je ne puis que rendre justice aux sentiments bienveillants qui ont dicté cette 
dépéche, et en témoigner ma reconnaissance au Principal Secrétaire d’Etat de 5a 
Majesté Britannique pour les Affaires Etrangeres. 

Avant d’entreprendre la tache de donner des éclaircissements sur les différents 
points auxquels touche la piéce en question, je vous dirai franchement la peine 
que j'éprouve de voir tous les jours combien on cherche & nous dénigrer auprés du 

touvernement de Sa Majesté Ia Reine d’Angleterre et i dénaturera ses yeux 
tout ce qui est deji fait en faveur des Chrétiens. Ainsi on lui a rapporte: 
1. Que le temoignage des Chrétiens n'est pas admis dans plusieurs endroits ott 
les juges sont encore exclusivement Masaliians, et dans les causes civiles dans 
lesquelles ils peuvent étre plus fréquemment lesés que dans les affuires crimi- 
nelles. 2. Que les seuls Tribunaux Mixtes sont les Tribunaux de Commerce 
dont les fonctions sont trés limitées. 3. Que toutes les autorités de provinces 
sont encore composes des Musulmans, et cela encourage oppression et l'imjustice 
contre les Chrétiens. 7 

Il est vrai quil y a des Tribunaux Civils régis par la loi religieuse, qui 
n’admettent pas le témoignage des non-Musulmans contre les Musulmans; mais 
les Chrétien ne sont nullement obligés d’y recourir; ou ails y sont condamneés, 
‘Ws ont ln faculté de faire appel en derniére instance jusqu’au tribunal le plus clevé, 
au Conseil Supréme de Justice. Dans les provinces, la plupart des proces civil 
sont jugés par les Conseils Locaux, ott toutes les diftérentes communautés sont 
représentées par des membres élus par elles-mémes. Ces Conseils réunissent 
leur pouvoir judiciaire des fonctions administratives tres ctendues, Les Gouver- 
neurs sont tenus de ne rien faire sans avoir pris d’avance leurs avis: ce sont eux 
qui président 4 la répartition et a la perception des impéts. Les membres non- 
Musulmans de ces corps y remplissent déja la mission de défendre les intertts de 
leurs coreligionnaires ; tout sntivide sujet de Sa Majesté le Sultan, de quelle

No. 92, 
Lord Stratford de Redelijfe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received May 21.) 


(No. 345.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, May 10, 1855. 

IT is with no small degree of satisfaction that I forward to your Lordship, 
herewith, a printed official copy of the Imperial proclamation by which the 
abolition of that odious tax upon the Rayah subjects of the Porte, known under 
the name of haratch, has been promulgated. That badge of degradation is 
removed, | trust for ever, from the Christian population of this Empire ; and the 
Sultan, in thus redeeming the pledge which he deigned to give me several 
months ago, has acquired a fresh title to the affection of his people and to the 
sympathies of Kurope, 

Your Lordship will observe that the moral advantages derived from those 
sources are accompanied with measures calculated on the one side to meet the 
loss of revenue incident to the abolition of the haratch, and, on the other, to open 
a new source of recruitment for the Sultan's army. The principles on which 
these provisions are made to rest have not been concealed, They appear to be 
essentially sonnd, and a just application of them is to be secured by subsequent 
regulations, on the character and prudent enforcement of which the success of 
the entire measure will greatly depend. 

It is satisfactory, moreover, to observe that the present reform is announced 
as part of an improved system already in progress of adoption, and destmed to 
be carried gradually into effect. 

Commissioners, to the number of about twenty, are appointed to watch 
over the enforcement of the general measure in specified localities, and among 
the individuals designated for that service ure some distinguished by their 
abilities and other qualifications. 

It will probably be necessary for me to return to this subject at some later 
period, 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE. REDCLIFFE.

PARML toutes les questions qui intéressent avenir de Empire Ottoman,

Vexamineret d'étudier dans toute son étendue.

plus mortelle & la liberte action et au prestige si nécessaires a existence de 
tuut Gouvernement ?

Lord Siratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received May 24.) 
(No, 356.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, May 14, 1859, 

| HAVE the honour to reply to your Lordship's inquiry on the subject of 
Christian evidence in Civil Courts of Justice in this country. I directed Mr. Pisani 
to read your Lordship’s despatch to Fuad Pasha, whose answer was couched in 
terms of characteristic levity. 1 replied with some expressions of surprise for 
further explanation, and 1 beg now to state the substance of the Minister's 
answer on both occasions, 

The Porte, he declared, is preparing to establish Commercial Tribunals, like 
that which already exists here under the denomination of “'Tidjaret” in all the 
provinces of the Empire, and separate courts of novel formation for questions 
relating to landed property. y 

Fuad Pasha excused the slowness of the operation by alleging that caution 
was necessary in thus introducing novelties distasteful to the Mussulman 
population, and that the Porte found it politic to throw the old tribunals 
eradually into disuse rather than submit the feelings of the Prophet's disciples to 
a trial too rude for their prejudices. 

In the meantime at Bryne Beyrout, and Salonica the Court of * Tidjaret” 
has been for some time established, é 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

I TRANSMIT to your Excellency the draft of an instruction which Thad 
proposed to address to you, respecting the recommendations to be made to the 

orte in favour of its Christian subjects. 

| desired Lord Cowley to show this draft to Count Walewski, in the hope 
that if it met with the concurrence of the French Government, similar instruc- 
tions might be given to the French Ambassador at Constantinople, : 

Your Excellency will see by the inclosed Copy of a note verbale given by 
Count Walewski to Lord Cowley, the light in which the French Government 
look upon the suggestions stated in my draft; but I am happy to say that, 
although uot wholly concurring in the views of Her Majesty's Government, 
Count Walewski has proposed that the English and French Ambassadors at 
Constantinople should consult together as to the advice which should be tendered 
to the Porte upon the points in question, and should draw up a statementof 
those on which they may agree, and report to their respective Governments for 
decision those on which their opinions may not coincide. 

Her Majesty's Government, as you will perceive by the inclosed copy of a 
despatch which I have addressed to Lord Cowley, have concurred im this 
proposal ; and your Excellency will therefore on the arrival of M. de Thouvenel, 
which will shortly take place, enter freely into communication with him on these 
matters, and in due time report to me the result of vane deliberations.

Inclosure in No, 96. 
Draft of Despatch to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


My Lord, Foreign Office, May , 1855. 

M. MUSURUS placed in my hands a few days ago the memorandum of which 
a copy is inclosed,® and which had been sent to him by Aali Pasha, for commu- 
nication to Her Majesty’s Government. 

The object of this paper is to show the course which the Porte has habitually 
adopted towards its Christian subjects, and the further benefits which it proposes 
to bestow upon them, and to deprecate the insertion in a formal Treaty between 
the Sultan and other Powers of any stipulations specially in favour of this eluss 
of the Sultan's subjects, as being derogatory to the dignity of the Sultan, and 
at variance with the principle on which the present war was undertaken. 

Although as regards the last point it might be observed that a guarantee to 
be enforced by five Powers might be attended with less embarrassment to the 


* Toclowure im Mo. oh, 


' ore 


a ea 


, 
— 


a 
, 


eee Se 


H2 


Porte than a right of interference vested in one Power alone, it cannot be denied 
that in principle at least it would be no less an infringement of Turkish inde- 
pendence. Her Majesty’s Government are certainly of opinion that the true 
way of settling the matter would be that the Sultan should by bis own sovereign 
power do for the Christians all that is required, and that he should then com- 
municate officially to his allies that which he had done. This would be a moral 
guarantee that would not derogate from the Sultan's independent authority. 

But this course, if adopted, should at once be carried into execution, in 
which ease it would have one great advantage, which would be that the nou- 
Mussulman populations would owe their privileges and advantages, not to Russia 
hut to their own Sovereign and to the good offices of England and France. 

The points to which it would seem most important to attend, in addition to 
those of the abolition of the haratch, and the admission of Christians inte the 
military service by voluntary enlistment, which seems to have been provided for 
by the Sultan’s late firman, as reported in your Excellency’s despatch No. 
of the instant, are— 

1. The eligibility of the non-Mussulman subjects of the Porte to rise to any 
rank in the army. ) 

4. ‘The admission of non-Mussulman evidence in civil as well as in criminal 
eases. 

2 ‘The establishment of Mixed Courts of Justice for all cases in which 
Mussulmans and non-Mussultnans are parties. 

4, The appointment of a Christian officer as Assessor to every Governor of 
a Province when that Governor is a Mussulman, such Assessor to have full 
liberty to appeal to Constantinople against any act of the Governor, unjust or 
oppressive, or corrupt. ile oes 

5, The eligibility of Christians to all places in the Administration, whether 
at Constantinople or in the Provinces, and a practical application of this rule, us 
well as of the military rule, by the appointment of Christians at once to some 
places of trust, civil and military. 

6. The total abolition of the present system, by which offices at Constanti- 
nople and in the Provinces are bought and sold, and given to unfit and unworthy 
men for money paid or promised. Such men become tyrants in their olfices, 
either from incapacity or bad passions, oF from a desire to repay themselves the 
money paid for their appomtments: 

Furthermore, Her Majesty's Government consider that there should not 
only be complete toleration of non-Mussulman religion, but that all punish- 
ment on converts from Islamism, whether natives or foreigners, ought to be 
abotished. 

Such are the points to which Her Majesty's Government conceive the 
attention of the Porte ought more particularly to be called ; and in bringing them 
under the early consideration of the Sultan and his Ministers, your Excellency 
will add that it is of the utmost importance, in the opinion of Her Majesty's 
Government, that the Sultan should lose no time in giving effect to them. By 
doing so the Sultan would deprive Russia of one of the strongest engines for 
working on the sympathies of the Christian subjects of His Highness, and he 
would enlist the public opinion in Europe in favour of Turkey, 

Tam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

Inclosure in No. 97. 
Acting Consul Hughes to Lord Stratford de Redclifie. 


My Lord, Uskup, May 28, 1855. 
INFORMATION reached me a few days ago, that a Christian girl had 
been imprisoned during three months at Kalkoudeli, a town situated about eight 
Turkish hours north of Uskup, with the view of forcing her to embrace Islamism; 
that in cansequence of a petition addressed by her brothers to Akif Pasha, for 
inquiry into her ease, she had been conveyed to Uskup, and had already been 
detained here fifteen days, closely confined in the Imaum's house, and debarred 
from all intercourse with her relations. I lost no time in applying to Akif Pasha 
for her immediate liberation, The answer I received was not satisfactory. On 
the following day, however, his Excellency ealled to return the official visit of 
Captain Rhodes, and on the subject being introduced, assured me that I had 
been misinformed ; that the girl was really desirous of changing her faith ; and 
that she was about to be brought before the Medjlis to make a declaration to 
that effect. 1 insisted on her being previously consigned to the care of the 
Greek Bishop, in order that she might no longer be under the influence of the 
intimidation which 1 was certain had been exercised in her case. His Excellency 
declared that the Sultan's firman did not warrant this proceeding ; whilst | 
urged that not only such was the usage at Monastir and at other places, but 
that the illegal constraint to which the girl had already been subjected, rendered 
this course more than usually imperative in the present instance. The Pasha 
was at last induced to promise that she should be brought to the Consulate, in 
order that I might interrogate her myself, and thus ascertain her real sentiments. 
I was therefore not a little surprised and indignant at receiving. about two 
hours after his departure, a message from his Excellency, to the effect that the 
promise he had just made me was unofficial, and that owing to opposition on the 
part of the Medjlis, it was not in his power to fulfil it, Shortly afterwards he sent 
the Bishop, to represent to me the danger of a popular outbreak in the event of 
his complying with my wishes, and to propose that the girl, with her brothers, 
should be conveyed to Constantinople, at his, the Pasha's, own expense, in 
order that the matter might be decided there. I treated these messages with 
the contempt they deserved, and sent im an official note, couched in strong 
language, and calling upon the Pasha to abide by his promise. He held out 
two days, during which time I spared no efforts to bring him to a sense of his 
duty, and the serious consequences which night ensue im case he obstinately 
persisted in a refusal. Finally { succeeded. ‘The Bishop called on the evening 
of the 26th instant, to inform me that the Pasha and the Medjlis had agreed to 
consign the girl to him. for three days. 1 readily assented to this arrangement 
and the more so as it is desirable to establish this as the peneral rule, whilst 
her conveyance to the Consulate could only have been looked apon as 
exceptionable. | 
Accordingly on the following morning she was conducted to the Metro- 
politan residence, and L had the satisfaction of hearing, that no sooner was she 
reassured by the presence of the Bishop and her friends, and informed that the 
English Consul had interfered im her behalf, than she tore off her Turkish veil 
and embracing her brothers wept with joy at her unexpected deliverance. 
On questioning lier mysell, In presence of the Bishop, it appeared from her 
story that being an orphan, she dwelt with her two brothers, at a village neur 
Kalkoudel ‘ and that one night, whilst they were absent at the market, the 
house was forcibly entered, and she was carricd off bya Turk, who had conceived 
a passion for her, assisted by three other ruffians, ‘Two days subsequently this 
2b 


ov 


ee oe 


94 


individual was taken to serve in the Rediff, and sent to Shumla, but his family, 
with the connivance of the local authorities, endeavoured by menaces to induce 
her to become a Mussulman. She was four times dragged before the Medjlis 
to make the declaration required, but each time temporized, as she feared to 
refuse absolutely. After three months’ persecution she was conveyed to Uskup, 
in consequence of her brothers’ petition to the Pasha. The Zabtiehs who 
conducted her, threatened to drown her in the Vardar, if she cried out or spoke 
to any one on the road. Since her arrival here she had been kept in strict 
confinement, forced to observe the fast of Ramazan, and subjected to the same 
sort of harsh importunity as at Kalkoudeh. 

Finding that she possessed sufficient courage to appear at once before the 
Medjlis, | sent word to the Pasha to summon all the members without delay. 
At my request, Captain Rhodes accompanied me to the Serai, to witness the 
proceedings. When the members had taken their places the girl was introduced, 
not, as is usual, in the Turkish dress, but, as i had arranged, in the simple 
costume of her native village, The question was then put to her by the Bishop, 
whether or not she was a Mussulman. She replied with great fervour, “I am 
a Christian, and I will die a Christian.” 1 then applauded her for the constancy 
and fortitude she had displayed under wicked persecution, and for the good 
example she had thus afforded. Before quitting the room she ste “ forward, 
and throwing down the bundle containing the Turkish dress, which she had 
worn so long against her will, said with admirable courage and scorn, “Take 
back your emanet,”” or pledge, After telling the Pasha that I should hold him 
responsible for the safe conduct of the girl back to the Bishop's residence, I 
turned to the members of the Medjlis and upbraided them at some length for 
their cruelty and fanaticism at a time when thie Christian Powers were fighting 
their battles, and for their insolence in trying to induce the Pasha to violate a 
formal promise made to Her Majesty's Acting Consul. 

I received no reply beyond a few faint excuses offered by the Pasha, On 
the next day, however, Galib Bey, the most influential member of the Medjlis, a 
man notorious for his hatred of Christians, called upon me with two other 
members, to beg that I would not address an official complaint against. him to 
your Exeelleney, as I had threntencd to do. He promised, moreover, that | 
should never again haye reason to complain of his conduct, and that in future 
he would use all his influence to further my wishes. 

The Christian population, who have watched with intense interest this 
struggle with the Turkish authorities, are overjoyed at the result, which they 
regard, and perhaps with truth, as the first blow given to Mussulman injustice 
and fanaticism in this part of Albania. 1am requested by some of the principal 
pcre of the community to express to your Excellency an anxious hope that 
Jer Majesty’s Government will not leave them much longer without an efficient 
protector to reside permanently among them, The presence of a British Vice- 
Consul at Uskup is, indeed, much called for, not only that he may foster trade 
and aid the development of the great natural advantages of this magnificent and 
fertile country, which have already been pointed out in Mr. Longworth’s reports, 
but that he may be the guardian of the rights and privileges accorded by the 
Sultan to his Christian subjects. In these remote provinces, without the 
surveillance thus exercised, decrees and firmans in their favour are but a 
mockery and delusion. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) T. F. HUGHES. 


P.S.—I have received assurances from the Pasha that all the persons 


concerned in the outrage upon the Bulgarian girl shall be arrested and severely 
punished, 


T. F.H.

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No, 464.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, July 14, 1855. 

THE inelosed memorandum® respecting Turkish atrocities committed on 
Christians in Albania and other provinces has been placed in my hands by Count 
Colloredo, who was instructed to say that it had been forwarded to the Inter- 
nuncio, with orders to lay the facts it contains before the Turkish Government, 
and fo express a hope that measures will be taken to put an end to this 
hornble state of things. 

The Internuncio is further instructed to ask for the support of Her Majesty's 
Aimbassador in the representation which he will have to make to the Porte. 

Her Majesty’s Government unfortunately sce no reason to question the 
necuracy of the facts contained in the memorandum, as they resemble but too 
closely the uninterrupted and unpunished harbarities against which they have so 
often remonstrated, and which the Porte has not the goodwill or the good sense 
te control, even at a moment when the existence of the Ottoman [Empire 
depends upon the sympathy and the sacrifices of Christian Powers, 

You will support any representation that may be made on this subject to 
the Turkish Government by the Internuncio, and you will give a copy of the 
accompanying memorandum to the Turkish Minister for Foreign Affairs. 

Tam, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

Inclosure in No. 101. 
Consul Wood to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Damascus, July 4, 1555. 

CONFIDENT that your Lordship will be pleased to appreciate my motive, 
| may venture to draw attention to the offensive term employed in the Turkish 
oiticial * Gazette," No. 521 of Rejeb, with reference to the estates of demised 
non-Musulman subjects, Aree Fe 

In the enactments lately promulgated for the administration of the estates 
of Turkish subjects, the word ‘‘tewafa or mat!’ is used for deceased Mussul- 
tans, which means died: but the term “halik” is used for Christians, 

Although the expression *' halik”’ in literal Arabic means. “ruin, loss, or 
inclination downwards to a pit,” and is employed in that sense in the Koran in 
speaking of animate and inanimate objects, yet in the vernacular language when 
employed, a8 in the present instance, with reference to bishops, priests, ane 
Christians, it means those whose souls are lost or damned, 1 have discussed 
the matter with some literary Etfendis here, and they have admitted that the 
common people understood it in the above sense. | 

It is, perhaps, unfortunate that, when the hopes of the non-Mussulman 
subjects of the Porte are being raised to expect some immunity from public 
degradation, the Turkish Government should have undeceived them by the 


* No. 99. 


law

reintroduction of offensive and humiliating terms in its enactments concerning 
them, and which the common law courts are actually using in their decrees, 
thereby maintaining old and impolitie prejudices. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) RICHARD WOOD,

No. 103. 


Memorial addressed to the Earl of Clarendon by the Committee af the Turkish 
Missions Aid Society. 


To the Right Hon. the Earl of Clarendon, one of Her Majesty's Principal 
Secretaries of State, &ec. 


The Memorial of the Officers and Honorary and Managing Committee of 
the Turkish Missions Aid Society, 


Showeth, 


THAT your Memorialists acknowledge with gratitude the services rendered 
by your Lordship and Her Majesty’s Government to the cause of liberty, both 
civil and religious, in the Ottoman Empire, procuring as they have done from 
the Sultan, many wise and salutary concessions, which have already made His 
Highness’ name memorable in the annals of enlightened legislation, 

That these concessions must, however, be in a great degree incomplete so 
long as the Mahometan code retains the barbarous statute which condemns a 
born Mussulman to expiate by death the profession of any other creed than that 
of the Koran, 

Your Lordship is doubtless aware, that notwithstanding the reasonable 
expectations which were raised by the pledges of the Sublime Porte (wide 
Appendix), especially that of March 21, 1844, viz.: “The Sublime Porte en 
to take effectual measures to prevent henceforward the execution and puttmg 
to death of the Christian who is an apostate,” cases have occurred at Aleppo 
and Adrianople, proving that this sanguinary law is still in force, and has been 
within a recent period actually carried into effect. 

That your Memorialists are given to understand that the French Ambas- 
aador at the Porte hus already taken some measures to bring before the notice 
of the Turkish Government a law so utterly revolting to the spirit of the age, 
and have every confidence that Her Majesty’s Government will be found ready 
to employ their powerful influence for the completion of the great work already 
ao well begun. ; 

They therefore most earnestly and most respectfully entreat your Lordship, 
at this opportune moment, to use your best endeavours with the Ottoman Porte, 
with the object that the conscientious convictions of all classes of its subjects 
shall be respected, and that liberty to adopt another profession of faith shall be 
extended to all, Moslem as well as Christian, without fear of penal consequences. 

On behalf of the officers and Committee, 
(Signed) SHAFTESBURY, 


July 27, 1855, President,

henceforward neither should Christianity be insulted in his dominions, nor 
should Christians be in any way persecuted for their religion.

Official Declaration of the Sublime Porte relinquishing the practice of Executions 
for Apostacy, March 21, 1844, 
(Translation,) 
The Sublime Porte engages to take effectual measures to prevent hence- 
forward the execution and putting to death of the Christian who is an apostate.

Acknowledgment of the Sublime Porte's Official Declaration respecting Executions 
for Apostacy, Marek 22, 1844. 
(Extract) 


The official declaration communicated by his Excellency the Minister for 
Foreign Affairs, shall be transmitted to the British Government, who will under- 
stand with satisfaction that the Sublime Porte, in taking effectual measures to 
prevent heneeforward the execution and putting to death of any Christian, an 
apostate from Islamism, relinquishes for ever a principle inconsistent with its 
friendly professions.

On the one hand, we have perceived the difficulty, not to say the imposst-

the law of the Koran relating to apostacy; on the other hand, we cannot but 
raise our voice when it is a question of inflicting the penalty of death upon 
individuals who, in embracing Christianity, or in returning into the bosom of 
the Church, appeal to our protection, and impose upon us the duty of withdrawing 
them from the rigours of a barbarous legislation. ee * Let it 
(i. e. the Porte) comprehend in fine the necessity of allowing to become obsolete 
antiquated enactments of the Mahometan Law which cannot be upheld but in 
disregard of the unanimous representations of all the Powers.

1 AM directed by the Earl of Clarendon to acknowledge the receipt of the

Aleppo and Adrianople, proving that the law of death for apostacy is still in 
foree, and has been within a recent period actually carried into effect. 

I am to state to your Lordship in reply that all instances of ill usage of 
Christians in the Ottoman territory which come to the knowledge of Her 
Majesty's Government are immediately represented by them to the Sublime 
Porte in terms befitting the gravity of the ease; and before taking any steps upon 
the cases of capital punishment for apostacy said to have recently occurred at 
Aleppo and Adrianople, Lord Clarendon would be obliged to the Society to 
furnish him with the dates at which the events in question took place, and with 
all other particulars concerning them which have come to the knowledge of the 
Society.

Mr. Young to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved August 7.) 


The Turkish Missions Aud Society, 
My Lord, 7, ddam Street, Adelphi, August 7, 1855. 

IN compliance with your Lordship’s wish to be furnished by the Turkish 
Missions Aid Society with the dates of the two cases of capital puuishment for 
apostacy which occurred at Aleppo and at Adranople, I am directed by the 
Committee to state that the former occurred ubout the beginning of the year 
1852, and was witnessed by two French gentlemen, Messrs. Pons and Riche, 
residents at Aleppo, and that Mr, J. H. Gibb, an English gentleman, was also 
present in the Aleppo bazaar at the time and saw the corpse. Mr. Sola was the 
English Chancellier offiiating at the time for Mer Majesty's Consul, The 
offender, who. is stated to have been an inoffensive man and blind, was con- 
demmed for blaspheming the Prophet. 

‘The second case occurred at Adrianople towards the end of the year 1853, 
and gave rise to a correspondence between Lord Stratford de Redelitte and the 
Ottoman Government. ‘The following extract is taken from the |“ Christian 
Times” of December 16, 1853 :— 


2D 


102 


‘A "Tunn Bengapep AT ADRIANOPLE FOR PROFESSING CunisTiANityY. 


* Constantinople, November 24, 1853. 

“4 circumstance has just oceurred which will open many eyes to the real 
charcter of the Turk in the middle of the nineteenth century. A Mussulman 
has just been beheaded at Adrianople for professing the Christian religion !! 
Happening at this precise moment of time, when the fleets of England and 
France are lying in the Bosphorus, having come to preserve the Turkish Govern- 
ment from CHRGSHOn, it must be regarded as critical. A providence is in it, 
we depend upon it great results will follow, The circumstances are briefly 
these :— 

“ A Mussulman young man, of the village of Eski-Zaghara, near Adrianople, 
belonging to a sect of ‘Turks called Taffani, declared publicly that Mahomet 
was a false prophet, and that the true prophet was Christ, and that alter him 
there was no prophet. The Turks warned him to beware, but he persevered in 
his profession of Christianity, and was, at last, seized and thrown into prison. 
Subsequently, he was removed to Adrianople, where he was tried by the highest 
court of the Pasha. He steadily persisted in his previous declaration, adding 
that, ‘so long as we have Christ, we have no need of Mahomet,’ He also said 
Mahomet was a false man, and he exposed the corruptions of the Mahometan 
system. He was remanded to prison and cruelly tortured, to induce him to 
recant, but in vain. He was afterwards brought out and beheaded, and with his 
last breath he protested against Mahomet, and said, ‘1 profess Jesus Christ, and 
for him I die?” 


This statement is further confirmed in a subsequent letter, published in the 
“Christian Times” of January 27, 1854, in which occurs the following 
paragraph — 


“THe Martryrpom at ADRIANOPLE. 


“ Constantinople, January 5, 1854. 
“There ig nu doubt of the truth of the story I related to you of the 
beheading of a Turk in Adrianople, for changing his religion, ‘The Turks 
themeelves, both there and here, and even the Ministers of the Porte, make no 
secret of it.” 


Count Zuylen de Nyevelt, Dutch Ambassador at the Porte, in a letter dated 
“Constartinople, May 22, 1855,” quotes the following extract from the official 
communication of one of the European Consuls at Adrianople addressed to his 
Minister in the capital, dated '' December 12, 1853”:— 


“Le Mahométan qui a été décapité ici avant peu, m’a pas dit quil voulait 
ombrasser le Christianisme, Mais il préchait ouvertement que cest le Christ 
qui est le vrai prophéte et non Mahomet, malgré que les auterités lui ont 
sugedrd tout moyen pour lui éviter la mort. Cet homme a tenu ferme presqu'au 
moment qu'on I'a décapité ; on m assure quiils y en a bien d'autres qui sont dans 
les mémes idées.” 


The Committee would again respectfully urge that they do not rest their 
appeal to your Lordship upon any individual case, which it 18 too late to recall, 
but desire that the general principle of religious liberly, as recognised in every 
civilised country, should be admitted by the Sultan, and accorded to all his 
Highness’ subjects alike. They press this the more from the knowledge they 
have that many Mussulmans would be prepared at once to make a public 
profession of the Christian religion were it not for the fear of capital punish- 
ment, 

I have, &e. 
By order of the Committee, 
(Signed) CUTHBERT G, YOUNG,

No. 107, 


Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—{ Received August 13.) 
(No, 561.) 
My Lord, Therapia, July 31, 1855. 

THE Austrian Envoy, Baron Koller, having informed me that he was 
instructed to bring under the notice of the Porte that catalogue of disorderly and 
criminal acts which your Lordship received from Count Colloredo, and trans- 
mitted to me with your despatch No. 564 of the 14th instant, I assured his 
Excellency that I was prepared to associate myself with the representation which 
he was charged to make on that subject to the Turkish Government, 

I have since redeemed my pledge by directing Mr. Pisani to read your 
Lordship’s instruction, with its inclosure, to the Grand Vizier and Fuad Pasha. 
It appears that in so doing I anticipated the communication which Baron Koller 
intended to address to the Porte. I have since learned that he has applied for 
support to M. de Thouvenel, who has stated to me that he is quite ready 
to meet his wishes in concert with me, though not, perhaps, to the proposed 
extent of giving in a collective note. 

It is my duty, meanwhile, to apprise your Lordship of the terms in which 
the Grand Vizier and Fuad Pasha have replied to the communication made to 
them on my part by Mr. Pisani. For this purpose I inclose herewith that 
gentleman's official report, submitting it to your Lordship’s perusal without 
meaning to be understood as intimating any opinion whatever on the merits of 
their statement and remarks. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

It is true, added they, that many things are wanting in Turkey, and uy 
disorders exist in several of its provinces; but some allowance must he made 
for the difficult circumstances under which the Ottoman Government 1s placed. 
Notwithstanding, the Porte has done and is doing all she possibly can in the 
way of reform and for the promotion of the welfare of her ieaauy subees ’ 
more, perhaps, than some more civilized countries, In illustration, Aa A va 
cited the case of the Swedish Chargé d'Affaires at Constantinople, who a ‘a 
twenty-five years of uninterrupted service cannot be named Minister because he 
isa Catholic. Whereas M. Musurus:and Prince Caradja, the former Minister in 
London, and the latter at the Hague, are enjoying the same privileges and 
rank as would any Mahometan subject of the Porte. And, besides, al pian 
employés holding service under the Government, are placed on an equal footing 
it Tussulmans. . ; 
te witk reference to the atrocities said to have been committed in Albania 
and other provinces, as stated in the Austrian memorandum, the Pashas observed 
that most of the disorders complained of are the acts of Christians themselves 
against their own coreligionists. These men, said they, associate eles Mee to 
highway robbers of all sorts and creeds, being interested in keeping up such a 
state of things, in order to rouse up Europe against Turkey, and by so doing 
promote the views of Russia. oa as. ee e 
Far from pretending to excuse the state of things in the Provinces, the 
Porte, added the Pashas, will doall she possibly can to restore tranquillity in those 
parts of the Empire, Meanwhile, the Grand Vizier could not help observing, 
that Austria has less right than any other Power to remonstrate on such

No. 112. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Recetved September 3.) 


(No. 633.) 
My Lord, Therapia, Angust 21, 1855, 

I HAVE the honour to reply to your Lordship’s instruction No. 643 of the 
Oth instant, in so far as it is in my power to do so at the present time. I express 
inyself with this limitation because I cannot yet answer for the Porte’s construc- 
tion of the solemn assurance which it gave Her Majesty's Government in the 
spring of 1844 with respect to apostates. I can only declare my own manner of 
interpreting that engagement, and await the result of my inquiries on the former 
point. 

More than eleven years have elapsed since the Sultan made his well-known 
declaration of the 2lst March, 1844, and no clear instance of execution for 
apostacy has come to my knowledge since. In the only two cases cited by the 
‘Turkish Missions Aid Society in their correspondence with your Lordship, the 
crime imputed to the sufferers was blasphemy, and not apostacy. Such, at 
least, was the information which I received in answer to my inquiries respecting 
the occurrence at Adrianople, of which I had no intimation till after the sentence 
had heen executed. ‘The correspondence itself bears witness to the exhibition 
of the same charge against the Aleppo victim. This, it is true, may be a mere 
pretext contrived to mask the application of the old unrepealed Inw; but 
unfortunately itis one which may be more easily employed than exposed. 

As to my own interpretation of the Sultan’s promise, Ido not hesitute to 
declare the sense in which I accepted it, notwithstanding the manifest intention 
of the Sultan's advisers to usc an equivocal form of words. The Porte’s 
declaration carefully translated contains this phrase, “to prevent henceforward 
the execution and putting to death of the Christian who is an apostate.” 
These words, referred to the execution which brought them out, might imply 
a restriction to the case of one who, having been originally a Christian, had 
apostatized from the Mahometan creed after professing it. In my official 
acknowledgment of the Porte’s declaration I purposely used the phrase of * any 
Christian an apostate from Islamism,” which clearly enough comprises the case 
of any one who, having been originally a Mussulman, had apostatized from that 
faith and professed himself a Christian, 1 had great difficulty in constraining the 
Ottoman Secretary of State to reeeive that paper, and [ fear it cannot be 
doubted that whatever may have been the personal wishes of the Sultan and his 
Ministers, the latter sought to reserve the right of dealing with Mussulman 
converts to Christianity according to their law, not, I believe, of the Koran, but 
of its more esteemed commentators. 

L have always been prepared to act upon my inferpresetion of the Sultan's 
promise, and it appears to me that there is no real difference with respect to 
incurring punishment between the two cases discriminated above. The Christian 
who turns Turk, is, according to the notions of Islamism, as completely a 
Mussulman as if he had been so from his birth. 

Few things would afford me greater satisfaction than being instrumental to 
a more complete and practical recognition of the rights of conscience in this 
Empire; and if I leave the subject now, it is with the intention of resuming 
it on no distant day, not only for the purpose of transmitting the additional 
information required at my hands, but also for that of suggesting whatever 
it may still be thought possible to effect in vindication of so just and sacred a 
principle. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

No. 118. 
Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received September 8.) 


(No. 1,187.) | ; 
My Lord, Paris, September 7, 1895. 

THE day before yesterday a deputation from the Society of Friends of 
Religious Liberty, which Society has lately been holding meetings at Panig, 
headed by Sir Culling Eardley, came to me with a request that I would transmit 
the inclosed Address, through your Lordship, to the Queen, 

Your Lordship will observe that the Address is signed by persons from 
different parts of Europe, and that its object is to induce Her Majesty to use her 
powerful influence with the Sullan to obtain the enjoyment of full religious 
liberty throughout the Turkish Empire, and to prevent the infliction of capital 
aOnieencne upon Mahometans who may embrace the Christian faith—a punish- 
ment which, the Address asserts, is still practised. 

The deputation stated that similar Addresses were about to be forwarded to 
the Sultan himself, to the Emperor of the French, to the Emperor of Austria, to 
the King of Prussia, and to other European Potentates. — 

I informed the deputation that I would transmit the Address to your 
Lordship by the first opportunity ; but with respect to that part of it which 
alleged the continuation of the punishment of death on apostates from the 
Mahometan religion, I said that I had lately seen a despatch from Her Majesty's 
Ambassador at Constantinople, in which his Excellency stated that no execution 
of the kind had been brought to his knowledge since the Sultan's promise had 
been obtained not. to enforce the law in that respect. 

{ was told, in reply, of two—one at Aleppo, the other at Adrianople. In 
regard to the one at Aleppo no proof was offered ; but with respect to that at 
Adrianople, Mr. Schaufiler, one of the deputation, and a very respectable 
missionary, established for many years at Constantinople, whom | knew well at 
the time 1 was in Tarkey, assured me that he had seen a letter from the Dutch 
Consul in that town to the Dutch Minister at Constantinople, in which the 
whole particulars were given. | could not learn the date of the alleged 
transaction, 

I think it right to mention this circumstance to your Lordship, though it 
appears to me impossible, as | mentioned to the deputation, that any such exe- 
cutions could have taken place in towns in which Agents of Her Majesty's 
Government were residing, without their being brought to the knowledge of Her 
Majesty's Ambassador. | ) me, 

| should add that Sir Culling Eardley observed that he was aware that it 
was not in any way necessary to stimulate the activity of Her Majesty's Govern- 
ment upon either of the matters. to whieh the Address referred, but it had been 
considered, he said, respectful to memorialise the Queen at the same time that 
the Society addressed itself to other Sovereigns. 

] have, &ce: 


(Signed) COW LEY,

No. 116. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No, 723.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, September 17, 1855. 


THE memorial to Her Majesty* of which I inclose a copy for your Excel- 
lency’s information, and which is referred to in Lord Cowleys despatch 
No. 1,187, of which a copy was transmitted to you inmy despatch No. 709 of the 
8th instant, relates to a subject to which publie opinion is now anxiously directed; 
and it will be necessary to come to an understanding with the Porte respecting 
the extent to which the law inflicting capital punishment on apostates from 
Islamism is really repealed. 

The intervention of Christian Powers has already secured for those converts 
from the Armenian and Greck Churches who embrace the Roman Catholic 
religion within the Turkish dominions, immunity from the persecutions to which 
such persons were formerly exposed ; but some cases to which public attention 
has of late been directed seem to show that secedera from those Churches in 
‘Turkey who become Protestants do not enjoy a like immunity, and this mequality 
ought immediatety to be rectified, But it is not to this particular grievance, 
however much it may require to be redressed, that Her Majesty’s Government 
confine their representations. There is an evil still more urgently demanding 
attention which you are instructed to bring under the serious notice of the Porte. 
The Turkish Government assured your Excellency some years ago, that the 
Turkish law which inflicted the severest punishment upon Mussulmans who 
might become Christians had been repealed. Great doubts are entertained as to 
the correctness of that assurance; and instances are alleged to have happened 
since the date of those assurances, the one at Aleppo, the other at paatOn: 
in which seceders from Islamism to Christianity were punished by death. Her 
Majesty’s Government are aware that it may be said by the ‘Turkish Government 
that in these cases the victims suffered death not for chance of religion, but for 
blasphemy; but such an excuse would be mere evasion, because a departure 
from Mahometanism and the profession of Christianity must be accom- 
panied by circumstances which a Turk would easily represent as constituting 
blasphemy. | 

This subject is one which must be pressed on the most serious and imme- 
diate attention of the Porte, The Turkish Government cannot expect that the 
great Christian Powers of Europe, who are making gigantic efforts and 
submitting to enormous sacrifices to save the Turkish Empire from roi and 
destruction, can permit the continuance of a law in Turkey which is not only a 
standing insult to them, but a source of cruel persecution towards’ their 
co-religionists, which they never can consent to perpetuate by the successes 
of their fleets and armies. 7 

They are entitled to demand, and Her Majesty's Government do distinctly 
demand, that no punishment whatever shall attach to the Mahometan who 
becomes Christian, whether originally a Mahometan or originally a Christian, any 
more than any punishment attaches to a Christian who embraces Mahometanism. 
In all such cases the movements of human conscience must be left free, and the 
temporal arm must not interfere to coerce the amnion decision, 

“If the Turkish Government should plead as a dithculty preventing com- 
fiance with this just demand that to yield to it would require a violation of the 
bw of the Koran, the ready answer would be that the Turkish Government can 
mould the law of the Koran aa it pleases, when it suits its convenience to do 
so; and your Excellency might allege as an instance of this, that although Her 
Majesty's Government do not for a moment place the act of borrowing money 


* Loclosure in Wo. 114. 


11 


on interest and the punishment of men for changing their religion upon a footin 
of similarity, yet nevertheless the borrowing of money on interest is as muc 
and as directly forbidden by the Koran, as a change from Islamism to another 
faith. But the Turkish Government, when pressed by the exigencies of the 
service, and obliged to raise a loan for carrying on the war, has found means to 
overcome the obstacle erected by the prohibition in the Koran; and they could 
doubtless find in the precepts of the Koran itself good and sufficient grounds on 
which to rest an abolition of the barbarous law which inflicts punishment for 
changes of religion. ; : 

ou will inquire into and make a detailed report respecting the cases said 
to have occurred at Aleppo and Adrianople. | 

Lam, &c. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.

No. 118. 


The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Cowley. 
(No. 1,033.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, September 17, 1855, 

I HAVE to state to your Excellency that in consequence of the repre- 
sentations contained in the Memorial inclosed in your despatch No, 1,187 of the 
7th instant, and of similar representations which have reached Her Majesty's 
Government from other quarters, I have instructed Viscount Stratford de 
Redcliffe to call the serious attention of the Porte to the persecution to which 
Mahometans in ‘Turkey who may renounce Islamism and embrace Christianity 
are still exposed, in violation, if not of the strict letter, at all events of the 
spirit of the firman granted by the Sultan in the year 1844, at the instance of 
Her Majesty’s Ambassador, declaring that the punishment of death should no 
longer be inflicted on seceders from Islamism. 

Her Majesty's Government doubt not that the Government of the Em ror 
will share with them in the conviction that if on general principles the al ga- 
tion of such an odious practice as that of putting persons to death for chan .og 
their religion might justly be urged at any time on the Porte, the Christian 
Powers by whose assistance alone the Porte has been enabled to avert the 
destruction with which it was menaced, have at present a peculiar right to press 
the question on the serious attention of the Turkish Government; and your 
Excellency will therefore suggest to Count Walewski to instruct M. de 
Thouvenel to join his efforts to those of Her Majesty’s Ambassador to induce 
the Porte formally to declare that heneeforth no punishment shall be inflicted on 
persons who may renounce the Mahometan religion. 


I aim, &. 
(Signed, CLARENDON.

SINCE I last had the honour of addressing your Lordship I have prepared 
the draft of a note for presentation to Fuad Pasha, with the view of carrying into 
effect your Lordship’s instructions on the subject of religious persecutions in 
Turkey, and particularly of punishment inflicted, in pursuance of the law, on 
apostates from Islamism. 

Yesterday I called upon the French Ambassador by appointment, and 
resumed, amongst other matters, the short conversation which I had previously 
had with his Excellency on that subject. On the former occasion he told me 
that he had not yet received any special instruction, but that he thought himself 
at liberty to enter upon the question under his general instructions, At our 
interview yesterday he repeated that he was st'll without instructions authorising 
him to act with me for the special object preseried by your Lordship; and 
when | showed him Lord Cowley’s despatch reporting Count Walewski's assent 
to your Lordship’s proposition, and his promise that corresponding instructions 
should be sent to the Ambassador forthwith, he persisted in declining to take 
any part in the affair, and expressed, on the contrary, opinions directly and 
strongly adverse to the demand which I am enjoined to press upon the Porte’s 
acceptance, Having failed to produce any change of purpose on his part by 
reading such passages from your Lordship’s despatch to me as best displayed the 
strength of argument and force of resolution which characterize that important 
instruction, | dropped the discussion without declaring any intention as to the 
course | should pursue, or communicating the draft, which T had in my pocket, 
of the official note prepared for Puad Pasha, 

Another mail will, perhaps, bring the corrective of this defect in my 
colleague's position ; but, judging from the decided manner in which M, Thou- 
venel expressed his personal objections, I feel how important it is that the 
instructions from Paris should jeave no room for any essential deviation on his 
part from the course of proceeding, or at least from the line of argument and 
requisition, which | am commanded to pursue. 

| have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

No. 126. 


Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Receiwed October 25.) 

(No. 1,398.) 
My Lord, Parts, October 24, 1855. 

1 TOOK a copy, on its passage through Paris, of Viscount Stratford de 
Redeliffe’s despatch No, 813 of the 11th instant to your Lordship, in which his 
Excellency relates the conversation which he had had with M. de Thouvenel on 
the subject of religious persecution in Turkey, and that Ambassador's refusal to 
join him in addressing the Porte to prevent the infliction of punishment on 
future apostates from Islamism. 

As M. de Thouvenel assured Lord Stratford that he had received no 
instructions to act in this matter, | reminded Count Walewski of what had taken 
plete on this subject between his Excellency and myself, and asked him how it 

apnened that the promised instructions had not been sent. His Excellency 
replied, that he was afraid he must admit that he had forgotten to transmit 
them. Upon my urging him to forward them at once, he said that he had heard 
from M. de Thouvenel on the subject, and that he thought there were cogent 
reasons for not pressing the question upon the Porte at the present moment. When 
he had expressed to me some weeks ago an agreement in your Lordship’s views, 
he had understood the question to be limited to the abolition of the punish- 
ment of death in eases of apostacy, but he now found that it had assumed a 
much wider range, and that the Porte was to be asked to make a formal 
declaration that apostates should be visited with no punishment whatever. He 
did not think that, supposing it possible to obtain such a declaration, it would 
be prudent to risk at the present moment the outcry which would be raised 
against the Sultan for giving it, by the more fanatic portion of the Turkish 
population. It would, perhaps, be better to urge it when the question of religious 
toleration came to be discussed in the consideration of the fourth point. 

If Count Walewski made any mistake as to the'scope of the instructions 
which your Lordship desired should be addressed to the French Ainbassador at 
Constantinople, it is his own fault, for he had read your Lordship’s despatch 
No. 1,083 of the 17th ultimo, stating the views of Her Majesty's Government 
on the subject. 

Upon the general question | observed to him, that Her Majesty's Government 
considered that there could not be a more fitting moment to bring the matte, 


* No, 19. 


115 


before the Porte than the present ; that I presumed he was of the same opiniot 
as Her Majesty’s Government that no punishment should be inflicted on 
Mahometans who embraced Christianity; and that I could not conceive why 
MI. de ‘Thouvenel should not be instructed to support any representations which 
Lord Stratford might make upon the subject. 
Count Walewski rejoined that he assented, of course, to the general prin- 
ciple, but that he must leave to M. de Thouvenel to decide the mode and 
moment in which it should be pressed upon the Porte. He again adverted to 
the discussion of the fourth point, as furnishing, in his opinion, the dest 


opportunity. eee

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Eart of Clarendon.—( Received October 29.) 


(No. 832.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 16, 1855, 


THE French Ambassador continuing to declare that no instructions similar 
to mine on the subject of punishment for apostacy lad hitherto reached him, 
notwithstanding the assurances given in that respect to Lord Cowley by Count 
Walewski, and communicated to me by your Lordship, I have not felt myself at 
liberty to bring the question forward in as formal and decided a shape as | 
might otherwise have done with a view to producing the necessary etfect ; but 
neither could L reconcile it with my sense of duty to keep your Lordship’s 
representations in a state of abeyance until the result of a reference to London 
could be known at this distance. 

Instead, therefore, of presenting an official note or demanding a conference, 
| called without notice on Fuad Pasha, and subsequently on the Grand Vizier, 
for the purpose of making inquiry as to the present state of the Turkish law 
concerning apostacy, and of making known to each of those Ministers the senti- 
ments and expectations of Her Majesty's Government, ath 

Having these objects in view, and guided by your Lordship's instructions, | 
entered into conversation with the Ottoman Secretary of State, who received 
my remarks and suggestions with much courtesy of manner and liberality of 
expression. He assured me that he had not heard of the executions at Aleppo 
and Adrianople; that the intentions of his Government were opposed to such 
acts of mistaken severity; and that far from yielding to a spirit of religious 
persecution or even of religious proselytism, he, und those who thought 
with him, did all in their power to disauade Mahometan converts from carrying 


out their purpose, and to save apostates, whether originally Mussulmans or not, 
from punishment. He made no distinction between the two cases. He would 
not admit that Protestants were viewed with less favour than other religious 
classes in ‘Turkey; but asserted, on the contrary, that the principle of Protes- 
tantizm was one which the Porte found it her interest to encourage. If the 
Protestants really had reason to complain, their vexations were not attributable 
to the Turks so much as to their fellow-Cbristians of the Chureh they lett. The 


2 eae ———— 


Se 


pon Aa ee 


116 


Porte’s system was essentially tolerant: the abolition of the haratch and the 
admission of Christian evidence had removed the principal grievances; those 
which remained were the effects of an imperfect administration, operating quite 
as much on the Mussalman as on the Christian, ‘The Government was anxious 
to improve its administrative system ; but so great a work could only be brought 
about gradually by the introduction: of successive reforms, leading to a total 
change. The only punishment to which offenders were liable on the score of 
religion was that inflicted on persons cuilty of blasphemy, by which was meant 
the offence of publicly disparaging the Prophet of Mecea, or either of his inspired 
precursors, Moses and Our Lord. 

Such was, in substance, the language of Fuad Pasha, who, in the course of 
discussion, fully acknowledged the claims of Great Britain and its Christian 
allies to the gratitude of the Porte; and also the necessity in which the latter 
was placed, of putting itself in harmony with the Powers of Christendom. He 
ended by remarking, in proof his Governments sincerity, that while the Porte 
was urged only to treat its Christian subjects with equity, measures were in 
progress for investing them with a share of administrative power. 

The Grand Viaier, whom I visited at a later hour, held much the same 
languaze as his colleague. Some shades of difference were, however, observable, 
arising perhaps from difference of position, or, it may be, trom difference of 
character and opinion in the individuals. There was the same declaration 
as to religious tolerance, administrative reform, and the necessity of Euro- 
pean connexion; the same acknowledgment of benefits derived from the 
alliance, and the same assertion that blasphemy alone, of religious offences, 
was now, in practice, lable to capital punishment, But there was by ne 
means the’same clear admission of the extent which [ felt myself at liberty 
to give to the Sultan’s promise and to the Porte’s engagements of March 1544, 


nt change of creed, [He ‘ntimated, nevertheless, that the Jaw was stubborn, if 
not unalterable; that the Christrins among themselves were more intolerant and 
actinonjous than were the Turks towards them; that if liberty ol conscience 
were to be generally established, there were countries in Christendom, such, for 
instanee, as ‘Tuscany, where improvement was more to be required than in 
Turkey ; and that, considering the ignorance and fanaticism whieh still prevailed 
in parts of this Empire. it was more prudent, and perhaps in the end it would 
prove more effectual, to let the law become obsolete, as sometimes occurred even 
in England, than to shoek the national prejudices by repealing it at ence. 

In reply to both Ministers I employed the arguments which obviously 
ocourred + and T read such passages of your Lordship’s instruction as were best 
calculated to place our demand in its proper light, and to show with what 
conviction and sevious, though friendly, determination Her Majesty's Govern. 
ment are prepared to urge its acceptance by the Porte. They would not admit 
that in making a loan they had departed from the law of the Koran, which, 
according to them, forbids only to take, but not to cive, interest. The distine- 
tion, whether correct or otherwise, ia of litthe consequence, as the Porte, in 
abolishing haratch, tore out a leaf, as Puad expressed it, from the sacred volume. 
They know well, as 1 have often told them, that the regeneration of this Empire 
‘s, humanly speaking, impossible while the principles und forms of legislation 
applied to administrative and judicial matters are invested with the inflexibility 
of divine truths: It has been found necessary to ascribe o suspending oY 
dispensing power to the Head of the State, and in the exercise of that prero- 
wative tics a principle of vitality capable of imparting fresh vigour, under new 
forms, ta the most decayed institutions. Soliman the Magnificent departed as 
much trom the established maxims of the Koran, when he made his first durable 
Treaty with France, a3 Sultan Abdul-Medjid would do in setting his seal on the 
perpetual abolition of penal enactinents against apostacy. ‘That great and 
glorious Monarch pereeived the necessities of his Empire in their very germ, 
and with the foresight of genius laid the first stone of that broad causeway over 
which the Western nations were in due season to advance to the succour of his 


— i 2 


descendants, not only with the pomp and powers of war, but yet more effectually 
with the arts of peace and the miracles of science. 

When Aali Pasha made the invidious remark that much remained to be 
done in Christian countries for the emancipation of the human conscience, I 
reminded him of the total wart of analogy which existed between the relations of 
those countries towards each other and their common relation towards the 
Ottoman Empire, especially under the present circumstances of undeniable 
weakness on the one side, and of sacrifices not to be exaggerated on the other. 
I claimed, moreover, for England, however it might be with other States, the 
right of pleading her own example in support of the representations which I was 
instructed ta make, not only as to freedom of conscience in spiritual matters, 
but as to the removal of civil disabilities on grounds of religious difference, and 
the benefits derived from a comprehensive system of government. I undertook 
to obtain more positive information with respect to the executions before alluded 
to, and | drew attention to the harsh treatment, independent of penal infliction, 
experienced in many instances by those who were charged with saieous offences 
in some provincial town or district, particularly by Protestants, who were not 
sufficiently protected by the Turkish authorities. 

__ Redress will, I trust, be obtained in the latter respect. ‘The former com- 
plaint was met with an excuse, which it 1s dangerous to accept. The confine- 
ment of accused individuals, their removal to a distance for trial, and their 
temporary exile, were treated as means employed of necessity by the Govern- 
ment to evade the violence, or to deceive the bigotry of an exasperated 
populace. 

_ Upon the whele, my pro osals were received with fewer appearances of irrita- 
tion and repugnance than I had ventured to expect. 1 was careful not to excite

more easy,

Lord Cowley to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Reeeived November 6.) 
(No. 1,442.) 


My Lord, Paris, November 5, 1855. 

REFERRING to your Lordship's despatch No. 1,267 of the 2nd instant, I 
have the honowr to state that I do not apprehend that there is any material 
difference of opinion between Her Majesty's Government and that of the 
Emperor, respecting the necessity of obtaining from the Porte, before the reeam- 
mencement of negotiations for peace, the settlement of all doubtful and difficult 
questions relating both to the treatment of the Christian subjects of the Porte, 
and to religious matters in general. 

Count Walewski has written more than once lately to M. de Thouvenel in 
the most pressing terms to urge the immediate consideration by the Porte, and 
by the two Embassies, of the best means of attaining the objects contemplated 
by the first and fourth points, to the principles of which the Porte has assented. 
His Excellency states that the Austrian Government is anxious to join the 
Western Powers in this work of humanity, and he thinks that the best means of 


120 


obtaining the objects which all have in view will be by laying down general rules 
for the future protection of the Sultan's subjects of all denominations, rather than 
by attempting to obtain the open repeal of an odious law, which no Turkish 
Government now dare to enforce. 
I have, &c. 
(Signed) COWLEY.

(No. 889.) 
My Lord, Therapia, October 31, 1855. 

INCLOSED herewith, in translation, is copy of a circular addressed by 
the Porte to its subordinate authorities, and communicated to Her Majesty's 
Embassy. 

It relates to the use of insulting terms in issuing permits for the bunal of 
deceased Christrians, as complained of by Mr. Holmes, justly reprobated by your 
Lordship, and remonstrated against by me, 

Henceforward all burials are to take place without special permits, The 
abuse complained of is properly characterized and decisively corrected in the 
Porte’s instruction. 

I have written to all the Consuls apprising them of the Porte’s circular, 
and requesting to be informed whether it has been receiver, made known, and 
acted upon. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


Inclosure in No, 135. 


Circular of the Porte to the Governors and Governors-General, dated 
September 1855, 
( Translation.) 


IT has been ascertained that, in some places, a permit of the Naib or 
Mufti is required for the burial of deceased Christians; that in others these 
permits are forced (upon the people), and that they contain insulting and 
degrading expressions, not fit to be used towards any men whatever. 

It is needless you should be told that it 18 a necessity amongst men that 
the dead should be buried, without exception, and there is no need of any 
permission for such a purpose, ‘The law does not impose in any way the obliga- 
lions of permits being issued for the burial of the lead, and the Governors do 
so with the sole object of getting money by it. The insertion of the expressions 
referred to is most unseemly, unwise, and contrary to holy law ; and the officers 
of the law have no right to interfere in the matter of interring the dead, Such 
ia the statement of the Elders of Islam; and as the holy law does not prescribe 
the issue of these permits, and as the continuance of such o practice, unbecoming 
towurds the Christian subjects without any cause, will be a source of discontent, 
you will put a stop to this improper impost. No interference is to be allowed 
in the interment of any of the Sultan’s subjects, or of any foreign subjects; and 
in future no insulting Aare of the nature is to be used with respect to His 
Majesty's faithful subjects, in any writing or statement. It has been preseribed 
and peremptorily enjoined in every quarter that the expressions “ perished” or 
" deattoved” should not be used, and that the common word “ dead’ should 
be employed. | 

ou will publish and duly impress these matters on the Officers of the Law, 
the Kaimakams, and others under your jurisdiction, and see that such things do 
not occur any more,

my despatch is inclosed herewith.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Consul Neale,

INCLOSED herewith is a copy of a report communicated to me by 
GBrigadier-General Storks, You will perceive that it mentions the state in 
which the grave of the late Honourable Colonel Maule and another British 
officer, buried last year not far from Varna, were found by the serjeant or 
sarpornl pent to find out the spot where the remains of those officers had been 
eposited,

unful to contemplate. I have brought it to the notice of the Turkish 
linisters, and they have promised to write to the Governor of Varna on the 

subject in becoming terms, } 

My object in writing to you is not only to draw your attention to what | 

am stire must shock your feelings as much as it does mine. I have also to 
request that you will communicate thereon with the local authorities, and obtain 
from them whatever may be found practicable to prevent any further violation of 
the respect due to those sacred remains which enlist every sentiment of decency, 
honour, patriotism, humanity and religion, for their protection from insult, |

Lo ae ietch No 1,442 of the 5th instant, that the Government
