123 


Your Excellency will consult Count Walewski as to any amendments or 
additions which he might desire to introduce, and you will suggest that no time 
should be lost in instructing the two Embassies formally to bring the whole 
subject under the consideration of the Porte. 

Her Majesty’s Government would haye wished to decline the cooperation 
of Austria in these matters, first, because that cooperation was probably offered 
with reference rather to the settlement of the first point than of the fourth; and, 
secondly, because it does not seem politic to allow Austria to enjoy the advan- 
tages of being a belligerent, without bearing any of the burthens of that 
condition. She has not placed herself in the same line with England and France, 
and does not take part in the war, and us she does not share in the sacrifices 
and exertions, she is not entitled to share in the fruits which those sacrifices and 
exertions may produce, 

Her Majesty’s Government will not press their own opinion upon this 
subject, as Count Colloredo has read to me a despatch from Count Buol, by 
which it appears that Count Walewski has expressed to Baron Hiibner the 
readiness of the French Government to accept the Austrian offer of cooperation 
upon the fourth point, but the intervention of Austria with respect to the 
settlement of the first point is open to grave objections. 

We have already had ample evidence that the arrangements desired by 
Austria are inconsistent with the wellare and good government of the Princi- 
palities; that in fact she aims at transferring to herself the power hitherto 
exercised by Russia over the Principalities, or at least of sharing that power with 
Russia; and England and France could not, with any regard to justice or 
humanity, be parties to au arrangement which would deprive the Principalities of 
all hope, by consigning them to a yoke more grinding and oppressive than that 
which they have hitherto undergone, 

It would seem expedient, therefore, that in the first instance the two 
Embassies should communicate confidentially with the Turkish Minister upon 
this important subject, and invite the Porte to draw up a plan tor the future 
administration of the Prinaipalities, which scheme should be submitted for the 
consideration of the English and French Governments, 

Your Excellency will further state to Count Walewski that Her Majesty's 
Government are convinced that no improvement in Wallachia is possible so 
long as Prince Stirbey continues to govern that country, and their desire is, that 
the Porte should be enjoined not to reappoint the Hospodar when his turn of 


office shall expire next spring, and moreover not to nominate his son in his 
place. 


lam, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON, 


No. 140. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received November 27.) 
(No. 924.) 
My Lord, 


Therapia, November 13, 1855, 
THE French Ambassador informed me on the 8th instant that he had 
received an answer from Count Walewski, to his despatch about the Mussulman, 
Christian, and other renegades; that he was authorized to invite the Porte, by 
means of friendly representations, to display its tolerance, but that Count 
Walewski was of opinion that the principle involved therein should not be 
discussed before the settlement of the fourth point. ML. Thouvenel added, that 
the Grand Vizier and Fuad Pasha had shown him documents, already commu- 
nicated to me, which, in his opinion, were calculated to satisfy the expectations 
of Her Majesty's Government. : 

I have since applied to the two Pashas, through Mr. Pisani, for a specifi. 
cation of the documents which appear to have atforded such entire satistaction 
to M. Thouvenel. They turn out to be a Vizirial letter to the Pasha of Candia, 
respecting certain famihes which had renounced the profession of Islamist in 
favour of Christianity ; and another abolishing the practice of treating deceased 


, 


| 


124 


Christians with indignity in the interment of their remains, Translations of the 
two letters are inclosed herewith, 

In reply to a declaration from me that I could not receive these documents, 
however valuable in themselves, as full and sufficient answers to my repre- 
sentations on the subject of punishing apostates, Fuad Pasha has sent me word 
that the papers in question had not been shown to M. Thouvenel as connected 
with my present demand on that head, but to prove that the Porte was 
endeavouring to accustom the Turks to a manifestation of better feelings towards 
the Christians. 

L have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


Inclosure in No. 140, 


Translation of a Letter to the Pasha of Candia, dated 11 Ramazan, 1275 
(May 27, 1855), 


I HAVE had the honour of receiving and reading a despatch from your 
Exeelleney, relative to a statement as to the treatment pursued towards certain 
people of the town of Papaziana, belonging to the district of Prepeza (?) in the 
sandjak of Candia, situate in the Island of Crete, with reference to their known 
conduct, Under the circumstances, and in consideration of their being religious 
people, it was quite right and in accordance with the spirit of the age to treat 
them mildly; out as it is probahle that their continuing to reside in that spot 
may give rise to some future disputes, a corresponding treatment should be 
adopted towards them, and, if necessary, they should be sent with a suitable 
sum of money to some island not inhabited by Mussulmans—whichever they may 
choose,—after which their property and effects may be sold, and the proceeds 
sent after them. Such a line of conduct seems most advisable, and as it is most 
desirable that it should be carried out in such a manner that no ground of com- 
plaint may occur in any quarter, it is hoped, from your well-known sagacity, 
that you will execute this instruction in a proper manner,

Inclosure in No, 14%. 


Translation of a Turkish Document, purporting to be a Copy of a Firman 
confirmatory of the Privileges of Roman Catholics in Turkey. 


(The Imperial gracious autograph of His Majesty the Sultan was placed 
above this firman, giving embellishment to its page, in the following terms :— 
“ Let care be taken for the constant and continuous unalterable observance of the 
tenour of this my august firman, and let any infringement thereof be avoided and 
guarded against,” ) 


TO the priest Nikoghoss (Nicholas), actually Roman Catholic Patriarch of 
Constantinople and its dependencies. May his dignity be enduring ! 


Since the Lord of truth, the Absolute Disposer of benefits, by reason of His 
most glorious divine goodness and of His eternal and unfading will, has conferred 
upon my august Imperial person the honour of rulership and dominion, putting 
me in possession of the glorious station of Emperor and Caliph; and since, 
thanks and praise be to God! He has delivered, as an especial divine trust, into 
my pontifical hands, which cleave unto justice, so many countries and territories, 
and such a variety of classes of subjects, and nations, and servants of His will; 
therefore, a5 is consonant with a doe zeal for the duties of the caliphate and 
sovereignty, and is so absolutely required by the august principles of rulership 
and dominion, | have, ever since my auspicious and august Imperial succession 
to the throne, always exerted and deemed fit to put in force, with the assistance 
of the divine succour, and the aid of the heavenly grace, an active energy and a 
constant State supervision, in accordance with the requirements of my Imperial 
sincere and benevolent desire, as of my august heartfelt wish, so that every class 
of the subjects of my Empire should enjoy a perfect protection, and, above all 
things, that they should, without exception, possess a perfect tranquillity in their 
religious rites and spiritual affairs, as has been the case heretofore, And since 
it is the chief of my Imperial desires that the good effects and beneficial results. 
of this circumstance should constantly be visible and manifest ; as also that, in 
case of any neglect or inattention, certain abuses, which may have gradually 
arisen, should be entirely removed and extirpated, so as never again to recur; 
therefore, it is my Imperial desire and particular wish that the especial spiritual 
privileges granted by my august and glorious oncestors, and established and 
confirmed by my Imperial self, te the priests of the faithful subjects of my 
Empire who are of the Roman Catholic persuasion, their churches and monas- 
teries situate in my Imperial well-guarded dominions, the lands anil possessions 
attached thereto, as well as other places of devotion, and the rights and privi- 
leges peculiar to these kinds of places of worship and priests; in short, that 


those sorts of privileges and favours which are set forth and specified in the 
diplomas (berat) of the Patriarch and Bishops, enunciatory of their ancient rules, 
should be at all times protected from infringement, 
And therefore, my peremptory re command has been issued and giver 
P| 


126 


In justice, to the effect that this my Imperial desire be once more reiterated and 
made public, so that no infringement of the same may in any wise occur, and 
that any persons acting in contrariety thereto may know that they will incur my 
Imperial displeasure. And furthermore, this matter having been made known to 
the necessary functionaries, in order that, should any kind of inattention relative 
thereto take place on their Parts, they should be left without excuse ; therefore, 
in the view of declaring an establishing that the complete and real execution of 
this matter is my august Imperial desire, the present my august rescript has 
been issued from my Imperial Chancery. | 
Thou, then, who art the said Patriarch, so soon as the matter becomea 
known to thee, conduct thyself and aet always in conformity with the sense and 
tenour of this my sacred decree, avoiding and abstaining from any contravention 
ofthe same. And, in ease of anything occurring at variance with this peremptory 
decision, use thou all diligence in immediately reporting and making known the 
sume at my chief office of Government. ig

Inclosure | in No. 143% 


Consul Wood to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Damascus, November 9, 1855. 

1 HAVE the honour to transmit to your Excellency a petition and a 
statement of grievance, in translation, which the Patriarchs and the delegates of 
the several Christian communities have addressed to your Lordship as well ag 
to the French Embassy, praying for relief from the hardships complained of, 

Tt would appear that on the demise of a Christian, the Cadi and Common 
Law Courts, for the sake of the lees, claim the right, on pretence that there are 
minors or absent legatees, of taking an inventory of the dacaset's estate, in the 
execution of which the distressed members of his family are subjected to much 
unnoyance and insult, ‘They therefore pray that, like the Hebrow community 
they may be exempt from such visitations of the law ollicers, And they claim it 
on the ground that, as the Cadi is by law the guardian and trustee of Mahometan 
minors and absent legatees, the Patriarchs or their delegates should hold the 
same position towards minors of Christian parents, In this manner they would 
enjoy the privileges conceded to the Mussulman Cadis, and be, in re to 
matters appertaining to the estates of deceased Christians, upon a fooling of 
equality with their Mussulman countrymen. 

Caking this view of the question, the concession sought would only be a 
development of the principle of equalization of rights and privileges, but it being 
difficult to foresee by what fiction of the Turkish law the concession might be 
resisted, particularly when it involves the loss of no inconsiderable amount of 
fees to the officials of the Mehkémés, it muy be proper to state that in many 


127 


of the districts in Syria the Cadis do not pretend to the same right of interven- 
tion, and in Alexandria the Christians have purchased an exemption from it by 
an annual fine of 2,000 piastres. 
But whatever may be the nature of the objections that might be raised 
against the prayer of the Christians, they might be overcome by the adoption of 
a course which, while it secures to the law officers their fees, removes the main 
grievance complained of,tat the same time that it offers sufficient protection to 
the memorialists, namely, that the Patriarchs, or their delegates, should alone, 
in virtue of the power granted to them by firman to superintend the spiritual 
as well as the temporal concerns of their respective flocks, be entrusted with the 
care of drawing up the inventory of the estate of deceased Christians for regis- 
tration at the Mehkémés, and that the Cadi and law officers should accept such 
a document as authentic, and be satisfied with the fee (as regulated by the Porte) 
which will accompany it, without any further claim or interference on their 


It is likewise stated that the non-Mussulman subjects in the districts of 
Homs and Hamah pay 15 piastres on the demise of iny of them, as a commuta- 
tion of all fees, by which arrangement they are freed from molestation and 
annoyance, 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) RICHARD WOOD, 


Inclosure 2 in No, 148. 
Petition, 


(Translation.) 
To the Right Hon. Viscount Stratford de Redcliffe, Her Majesty's Ambassador 
at the Ottoman Porte. 


YOUR Memorialists, the heads of the Christian communities in Syria, first 
present the thanks due to your Lordship's kindness and zeal, frankly acknow- 
ledging the favour of the British Government, through whose justice and zeal all the 
Christian sects have obtained 1 good measure of quiet, and have been saved 
from much injustice and insult, and especially that recently an order has been 
issued from the Sublime Porte forbidding the officers of justice to add oppro- 
brious epithets to the names of living and deceased Christians—an order 
doubtless due to your Lordship’s care and zeal. This has been a great fayour to 
us, and has emboldened us to present this petition to make known the contempt, 
injury, and injustice we suffer from the officers of the Courts of Law in the 
matter of the property of deceased persons among us, any of whose heirs are 
minors or absent persons. And accompanying this Memorial is? a hst of seven. 
particulars which will explain the insults and injury we endure, though our 
description falls greatly short of the reality, And if your Lordship will 
consider this statement, we doubt not that in conformity with the excellent, 
reforms (Tanzimat), and in consequence of your alliance and friendship with the 
Sublime Porte, and out of compassion for the Chiistians, your Excelleney will 
use your influence to relieve us from an unjust practice adopted by the 
Common Law Courts (Mehkémés) from a desire to appropriate property under 
pretence of securing the rights of minors and absent persons. Her Majesty’s 
Government will not desire the continuance of a practice so oppressive and 
insulting to the obedient and faithful subjects of the Government, who continu- 
ally pray for the permanence of the friendship of the allied Powers, and for the 
return of a general peace. 

We, therefore, and all the members of the Christian sects, entreat your 
Lordship to endeavour to procure for us an Imperial order from the Ottoman 
Government that, in case of the death of any protected subject who may leave 
among his heirs a minor or an absent person, it shall be sufficient for the Head 
of his Sect, and its elected Agent, to make an inventory of his effects, and divide 
them among his heirs according to law, and to subscribe the inventory of the 
effects and the division of them with their names and seals, and preserve it 
among the records of the Patriarchate, and this without the interposition of any 
person on behalf of the Courts of Law, and that the judge shall not put his seal 


128 


on the property, nor levy a tax on it either for himself or for the servants of 
the Court, especially as the Imperial Ottoman Government has now assigned to 
all judges liberal fees on sales of property, on drawing up of deeda and on the 
decision of lawsuits and other such matters. 

This is the relief which the protected subjects seek from the Imperial 
clemency, and which they hope to obtain thectigh the kind interposition of the 
British Government, and they will not cease to repeat their appeals to your 
Lordship’s compassion till they secure this favour so manifestly needful. 

And as to the share of an inheritance belonging to a minor or an absert 
yerson, it shall be kept under the care of the Head of the Sect till the minor 

comes of age, or till the return of the absent person, when it shall be delivered 
up to him without any deduction as a tax or under any other pretext, with all its 
proceeds and without any charge for keeping and managing it. In this mode the 
Imperial intentions for the benefit of minors and absent persons shall be fully 
carried out. ) 

This is the object of your memorialists, which we hope to obtain from your 
Lordship’s zeal and the clemency of the British Government, and for which all 
the Christian sects will feel under obligations to the British Government. 

(Signed) HIEROTHEOS, Patriarch af the Orthodox Greek 
Church in Antioch, Damascus, and their Depen- 
dencies, 

BISHOP MAKARIUS, Agent of the Greek Catholic 
Patriarch in Damascus. 

PRIEST PHILIP, Agent of the Syrian Catholic Sect 
in Damascus, 

PRIEST JOSEPH ANTHONY LABETY, of the 
Latin Church. 

PRIEST JOHN SADEH, Agent of the Maronite 
Sect in Damascus. 

WORTABET, Agent of the Armenian Catholic Sect 
in Damascus. 


Inclosure 5 in No. 143, 


Statement of the grounds of Complaint of the Christians against the Officers of 
the Courts of Law, 


(Translation. ) 


Ist. WHEN a Christian dies leaving among his heirs a minor or an absent 
person, immediately persons from the Courts of Law rudely put the eorpse out of 
the house, put a seal on all the apartments of it, address those present in 
opprobrious and insulting language, and act as if the property had become theirs. 

ow much is the grief of the relatives of the deceased increased by this 
oppressive practice when in the time of their sorrow they see the Mahometans 
entering to their wives and daughters, who have their hair dishevelled, their 
faces disfigured, and their tears flowing in mourning for their dead, 

2nd. When the officers of the Courts of Law take the inventory of the 
property of deceased persons, they estimate it much higher than its real value, in 
order to increase the tax upon it, and of late years they value also the house of 
the deceased, which is to be the dwelling of the heirs, as if it were to be sold at 
once, and yet should it be afterwards sold they levy the tax on it a second time, 
In such cases mediators are obliged to intercede with the officers of the Courts 
till the amount of the tax is decided, and even that amonnt the family of the 
deceased is often obliged to borrow at interest in order to obtain the removal of 
the official seals from the property. While in the Imperial commands the 
judges are not permitted to levy any tax on the dwelling-place of the heirs left 
by the deceased, and it was not formerly the custom to do so, und even yet this 
unjust practice ia contined to some districts and not to others. 

drd, Sometimes in order to diminish the tax the family of the deceased 
abstract articles of value from the property and conceal them, and thus portions 
of the inheritance of minors or absent persons are lost in consequence of the. 
property not being correctly registered in the Court, 

4th. These injurious and unjust practices sometimes oblige the heirs to 


129 


employ bribery in order to lessen the tax, and to have the seals removed from 
the property. | 

5th. In making the return of the debts due to the deceased, sometimes the 
names of some of the debtors are omitted, or their debts are represented as less 
than they are for the purpose of diminishing the tax, and thus the legal claims 
of the heirs to those debts are invalidated, and minors and absent persons are 
prejudiced in consequence, : ’ 

Gth. The judge in each city appoints a guardian for every minor by a deed 
from the Court, and for this he takes large fees not proportioned to the means 
either of the minor or the guardian. And even if the deceased has legally 
appointed a guardian, the judge, for the sake of obtaining fees, requires him to 
have this deed also. ! 

7th. In Damascus, contrary to the practice in other parts of Syria, the judge 
requires every guardian to furnish him with a yearly account of the receipts and 
disbursements on behalf of the minor; and he levies on the amount a new tax 
yearly, so large that by the time a minor who inherited when an infant comes 
of age, the yearly tax to be paid to the judge amounts to about one-half of the 
inheritance. 

In the case of property left to an absent person the Government takes 
possession of it; and when the person returns or sends a legal power of Attorney, 
it is impossible for him, even if he employ many entreaties and influential inter- 
cessions, to obtain his property, for he is put off from day to day with vain 
pretexts and many insults. ey 

These are some of the injuries to which all Christians are exposed in this 
matter. It would be tedious to mention others, or that the bailiffs of the judge. 
going into the house at the time of the death, and at the sale, entering into all 
the apartments and mingling with the family as if partners in the inheritance, 
take openly and abstract secretly, both trifling and valuable articles of property. 

The Christian communities, in seeking that the judges should cease to 
interfere with the property of their dead, are not demanding a great favour, but 
asking only equality with the Jewish community, with whose dead the judges do 
not interfere at all, whether the deceased be poor or rich, and whether or nat 
there be minors or absent persons among his heirs. Surely the Christians deserve 
to obtain from the Imperial clemency, through the good offices of the Dritish 
Government, that which the Jews have already secured. 


(Signed) HIEROTHEOS, Patriarch of the Orthodox Greek 
Chureh in Antioch, Damascus, and their Depen- 
dencies. 


BISHOP MAKARIUS, Agent of the Greek Catholic 
Patriarch in Dameascts. 

PRIEST PHILIP, Agent of the Syrian Catholic Sect 
in Damascus. 

JOSEPH ANTHONY LABETY, of the Latin 
Church, and Agent af the Sect. 

PRIEST JOHN SADEH, Agent of the Maronite 
Sect in Damascus. 

WORLABET, Agent of the Armenian Catholic Sect 
in Damascus.

Consul Neale to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Varna, November 20, 1855. 


I HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Lordship’s despatch 
of the 31st ultimo, transmitting to me a copy of a report communicated to your 
Lordship by General Storks, upon the subject of the state in which the grave of 
the late Colonel Maule was found. 

| have likewise the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your Lordship’s 
subsequent despatch of the 6th instant upon the same subject, and inclosing a 
Vizirial order, directing the Governor of Varna to take stringent measures for 
the protection of the graves of our officers and soldiers buried in this neigh- 
bourhood, 

While I have to deplore the correctness of the report communicated to 
General Storks in respect to the state in which we found the grave of the late 
Colonel Maule, I have the satisfaction to assure your Lordship that no similar 
desecration has been practised in any other instance. The other grave probably 
alluded to in the report, both in the opinion of Colonel Havelock and myself no 
burial had ever taken pluce;* in every other locality the graves have remained 
entirely undisturbed. 

\ have, in accordance with your Lordship's instructions, handed to the

Pasha of Varna the Vizirial order, and his Excellency has assured me that he 
will adopt the necessary measures to discover the perpetrators of the outrage 
referred to, and to avert the repetition of a similar occurrence for the future.

graves of British officers at Varna, and that they approve of the course which 

your Excellency has taken in this matter, 


Tam, &c.

No. 148. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received December 14.) 
(No. 997.) 
My Lord, Therapia, December 3, 1855. 


IT has come round to me, as the result of a recent conversation between 
the Grand Vizier and a candidate for official employment who is viewed favour- 
ably by the Sultan, that the Turkish Ministers are very little disposed to mect 
the demands of Her Majesty’s Government on the subject of religious persecu- 
tion; that they pretend to entertain apprehensions of popular discontent among 
the Mussulmans, if they were to give way; and that they are preparing to make 
representations to your Lordship, through M. Musurus, in order to obtain the 
transmission of other instructions to me than those under which | am now 
authorized to act upon that important subject. 

_T am assured that they talk of resigning in case of being pressed to a 
decision ; and that one of the elements of their anxiety is the presumed deter- 
mination with which I shall insist upon a decision accordant with our sense of 
aes and the duties if not the rights, of what may be termined a “ sacrificial” 

lance, 

_ Most assuredly, my Lord, I shall persist, so long as I am permitted to do 
80, In urging compliance with the wishes and demands of Her Majesty’s Govern- 
ment, | hope to perform this duty with becoming consideration for any real 
difficulties which may belong to the subject. Hitherto I am exposed to censure, 
rather for remissness than for intemperance in the prosecution of our suit. I 
have taken no further step than what has been reported to your Lordship, 
perce to wait until I could effect a thorough understanding with 

. Thouvenel, in so far as that may be practicable, 

Meanwhile the Duteh Minister has sent in a written communication, 
agreeing in spirit with the views of Her Majesty's Government; and | am 
informed that the American Minister intends to tuke a step of the same kind. 
Count Zuylen de Nyevelt has sent mea copy of his note to the Porte, and I 
have the honour tu inclose it herewith. 

_ Before your Lordship receives this despatch, I trust that I shall have 
actively resumed my negotiation with the Turkish Ministers, in concert with 
the French Ambassador, 

What 1 am instructed to require is nothing more, in reality, than a frank 
and entire confirmation, in practice, of the promises virtually made to me ten 
years ago, as well by the Sultan himself as by the Porte, before there was any 
Sueaicn of the present war, its alliance and its sacrifices. With respect to 

anger from popular discontent among the Mussulman population, I do not 


12 


believe that, even supposing its existence, it would extend beyond some insulated 
examples in the provinces, easily repressed, and unlikely to be attended with 
Serious Consequences,

Count Zuylen von Nyevelt to Fuad Pasha. 


Le 24 Novembre, 1855. 


LE Gouvernement Néerlandais a appris avec satisfaction et un vif intéret les 
démarches qui ont été faites par les alliés de la Turquie, pour engager Sa 
Majesté Impériale d’accorder & toutes les classes de ses sujets les droits qui 
découlent naturellement du principe de la lberté de conscience. 

Si les jours sont passés ob les Pays-Bas prenaient l’initiative pour defendre 
les droits de la conscience, comme du temps de la République, conséquente avec 
ses antécédents historiques, la Néerlande continue A faire entendre une voix 
parmi les avocats du principe d'une tolérance religieuse, dont elle donne un 
noble exemple dans ses propres domuines, 

En effet, sanctionnant le principe posé par un des plus grands hommes des 
temps modernes, I'Empereur Napoléon, que !'empire de la lor finit li on 
commence celui de la conscience, on y compte des membres non-Chrétiens dans 
la représentation nationale et dans les Conseils Communaux, et dans les Colonies 
de [Etat plusieurs millions de Mahomeétans jouissent de la liberté de conscience 
la plus étendue, 

Est-ce a dire que le Gouvernement Néerlandais reconnaisse daprés une 
locution admise de nos jours, que I'Etat est athée? Est-ce lindifférence en matiere 
de religion qui a créé cet état de choses? Loin de 1a, la Néerlande, fidele & son 
origine, est restée un pays Protestant et essenticllement religieux, mais on y 
comprend que les devoirs de la eréature enyers Dieu sont d'une nature trop 


sacrée, trop intime et trop individuelle, pour qu’il soit loisible a aucune autorite 


humaine de se placer en arbitre entre la conseience de Vhomme ect son 
Créateur, 

Fort de la rectitude de ce principe, et de son propre exemple, le Gouverne- 
ment Néerlandais vient maintenant appuyer auprés d'une Puissance amie, des 
démarehes qui, loin de nuire a ses intéréts bien entendus, ne tendent en défini- 
tive qu’A lui assigner une place honorable et respectée varm les nations 
civilisées du monde, et A lui acquérir de nouveaux titres \ la sympathie de 
l'Europe. 

La Turquie, de son propre aveu, a occupé jusqu’ici une position isolée, 
dont il lui tarde de sortir. Elle désire entrer en relations plus intimes avec la 
grande famille Chrétienne de l'Europe et de toute part on a applaudi a ce desir, 
parcequ’on 6 compris que si la Turquie vient de son propre mouvement 
tendre la main 4 l'Europe, elle ne peut avoir l'intention de blesser en méme 
temps la sociéte Européenne dans ses susceptibilités les plus légitimes, comme ce 
serait le cas, par exemple, par de nouvelles exécutions capitales pour cause de 
religion. Et cette impression est d’autant plus forte que les conversions A 
I'Islamisme ne rencontrent aucune entrave légale, et qu'on peut citer de 
nombreux cas de Chrétiens, Néerlandais et autres, qui ont renié leur foi sans 
étre inquictés en aucune manitre par leurs Gouvernements. 

Qu'il y aurait du reste quelques difficultés 4 introduire le principe si 
simple et si juste, cependant, que tout habitant de l’Empire aura le droit de 
choisir sa propre religion on ne yeut pas le nier, mais ces difficultés seraient- 
elles surmontées si le Gouvernement Ottoman fermait loreille aux exigences de 
jour en jour plus fortes de l’opinion publique sur ce point important ? 

On peut admettre, au contraire, que des dangers plus sérieux pourraient en 
résulter pour la Turquie. Mais ici la question se présente s'il n'y aurait pas 
moyen d'atteindre Je but désiré, en évitant de heurter de front les anciennes 
institutions de |’ Empire, et sans révoquer expresscment Ja législation sanguinaire 
existante, 

Peut-Ctre ce résultat pourrait étre atteint par une déclaration solennelle du 


133 


Souverain, que désormais personne dans son Empire ne sera pumi ou persécuté & 
cause des opinions religieuses qu’il croit devoir confesser. 

Mais ce sont JA des détails d’exécution qui dépassent: les limites des observa- 
tions que le Gouvernement Néerlandais a eri devoir présenter, se flattant que la 
Sublime Porte ne meconnaitra pas les sentiments d’intérét et d’ancienne amitié 
qui y seront aisément discernés.

Inclosure in No. 149. 
Memorandum. 


SEVERAL years have passed away since the execution of a young 
Armenian at Constantinople, who had renounced the Mahometan faith and 
returned to that of Christ, followed shortly afterwards by another similar 
infliction at Brussa, excited the indignation of Europe, and induced the Govern- 
ments of England and France to unite their efforts in order to prevent the 
repetition of so great a scandal. The Sultan and his Ministers were not deaf 
to their persevering remonstrances. The promise given personally by His 
Imperial Majesty to the British and also to the French Ambassador, in addition 
to the official engagements of the Porte, had every appearance of suflicing to 
secure the Christian ‘subjects of the Empire from religious persecution, and, in 
particular, to abolish the barbarous practice of putting seceders to death. 

It cannot, however, be concealed that in practice these diplomatic agree- 
ments between the Porte and its allies, though crowned with the Sultan's pledge 
and hailed with general acclamations, have not been attended with results by 
any means so steady or effectual as the French and British Governments were 
entitled to expect. In two known instances individuals have lately suffered 
death by the hand of the executioner on account of their religious opinions. In 
other cases, imprisonment, ill-treatment, and forced removal to a distance have 
been employed, either to obtain an unwilling recantation, or to appease the 
malice of a fanatical populace. Whole families of Catholic converts were on 
one occasion driven from their homes and sent across the sea into Asia under 
circumstances: revolting to humanity, and operating on their lives with fatal 
effect. Protestants, though recognised by Imperial firmans, and recommended 
to the local authorities, have been exposed to unmerited vexations, while 
Turkish functionaries, far from protecting them against the violence of their 
Christian persecutors, have themselves but too often connived at the work of 
persecution. 

Surely the timy is come when these remnants of an ignorant age should he 
permanently set aside, when the Porte’s enlightened declarations must be 
carried into full elfeet. Acts which, to speak plainly, are absurdities in the view 
of reason, and horrors in that of humanity, can no longer prevail against the 
feelings und common sense of nations united by the closest tics, True policy 
condemus and civilization rejects cai Ee legislative system which retains the

No. 152. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received January 22.) 


(No, 29.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 9, 1856. 

THE meeting appointed for a preparatory consideration of the question, 
known to Europe as the fourth point, relating to the privileges of the Sultan's 
non-Mussulman subjects, and connected with the atill broader question of 
administrative reform, took place this morning at the G rand Vizier’s house. In 
addition to his Highness and myself, the French Ambassador, the Austrian 
Internuncio, and Fuad Pasha, were present. 

I should have proposed a previous interchange of opinions, by means of a 
confidential meeting between the Representatives of the three Powers more 
immediately engaged in the war, as calculated to elicit and put into train their 
joint views, irrespective of those which may be entertained elsewhere. | had 
indeed suggested this mode of proceeding to my French colleague, as applicable 
to the question of the Principalities also, which it is evidently desirable, with 
reference to the eventualities of negotiation, to bring under deliberation without 
delay. But M. Thouvenel preferred the course which we are now following, 
under an impression, as he stated, that by acting separately, though confidentially, 
we might give umbrage to Baron Prokesch, at the same time that we could not 
make progress without knowing whether the question of the Principalities was 
to be treated on the principle of a barrier against Russia, or on that of a simple 
improvement of their condition. Not thinking it worth while to lose time in 
maintaining a diseussion on points which could not be set aside as wholly 
insignificant, I acquiesced in the course which was finally adopted by common 
consent. 

It affords me the liveliest satisfaction to state that in general the disposition 
manifested by my two colleagues, and also by the ‘Turkish Ministers, was such 
as to warrant a sanguine hope of our ultimate success in the great twofold work 
to which our exertions are now more particularly directed. Our consultation 
was carried on for the most part in a conversational form, as best suited to a 
frank and mutual expression of our respective ideas. We all in turn expressed 
a desire to bring the various classes of the Sultan's subjects into harmony and 
confidence with each other as a source of prosperity to the Empire, whose 
Government would find its advantage, both internally and externally, in acting 
no longer on the principles of religious exclusiveness ar predominance of rice, 
but on those broader, sounder maxims, which have only to be carried out 
with perseverance in order to produce a full measure of national unity and 
strength. . 

Some other important points appeared to offer little or no diversity of 
opinion. “Ihose which recur to my recollection may be stated here without wny 
unseasonable pressure on your Lordship’s time, 

We all agreed that Ottoman ascendancy could not be dispensed with in 
the Government; that there was less room for extension of privilege to the 
Christians than want of security for the enjoyment of those which had been 


ee < 
Be Fg ce oy 


a 
Ss 


Fa Si 


136 


eranted, and neglect on the part of provincial authorities in causing them to be 
respected; that a good system of administration, applying equally to all classes, 
would be found more practically remedial than fresh immunities conferred upon 
any separate class; that the initiative of any improvements to be adopted in 
future belongs to the Sultan; that by * initiative ” was meant the promulgation 
of them in the shape of a firman; and that finally, by anticipating in that 
respect the conclusive act of negotiation to the exclusion of Russia as a delibe- 
rative party, the Porte would raise itself in the estimation of foreign nations, 
and acquire a stronger hold on the affections of its own subjects. 

To these may be added the general belief, whether shown by assertion or 
by admission, that the present course of Ottoman administration is really so 
defective in action, and so liable to abusive influences in every department, as to 
require a searching and comprehensive reform. 

The points on which difficulties are principally to be foreseen, as appearing 
from the language employed in our deliberation, may be thus enumerated: On 
the part of the Turkish Ministers, we may expect procrastination, on the proud 
of respect for religious antipathies, popular prejudices, and unassociating habits, 
moral and physical; incompleteness in carrying out laws and regulations for the 
enforcement of necessary measures; deficiency of will for bringing the long- 
alienated classes into closer communion with each other; and a very natural 
inclination not to limit the process of reform to the Mussulman and dominant 
population; on the part of my colleagues, a certain reluctance to admit the 
distinction which, however, exists between protection by Russia and admonition 
by Europe; and, consequently, an eventual want of agreement as to the terms 
in which it will be desirable to consign the result of our conferences to the 
future Treaty of Pence. 

On this last point we only touched, agreeing to consider it as one not 
pressing for sr ueeate solution, and fit to be reserved for the decision of our 
respective Governments. 

The French Anibassador argued with energy in favour of the right to 
repair and erect places of worship in Turkey, without other check than that of 
an administrative character. He acknowledged the readiness with which his 
‘application for churches and church-bells had been recently granted by the 
Yurkish Government, He expressed a confident hope that the Sultan’s liberality 
would be carried still further; and (need not assure your Lordship that I joined 
with Baron Prokesch in supporting these views with cordiality, in the hope that, 
if so much had been conceded to a single ally, the Porte would not find it easy 
to resist the united importunities of all. 

With respect to a gradual and retarded process of reform, J reminded the 
Turkish Ministers that sixty years had already elapsed since the necessity of 
reform had been openly acted upon in Tarkey, and many more since the period 
when the Ottoman Empire first gave signs of meaning to associate itself with 
the Powers of Christendom. Admitting that the whole scheme of reform could 
not be accomplished in a week, | urged the rapid movement of human society in 
the present age, the favouring circumstances of the time, and the importance of 
anticipating the conclusion of peace, as so many stringent considerations for 
hastening the work in hand, On the question of religious difficulties, [ endea- 
voured to show the absolute necessity of not being stepped by them, and the 
impracticability of realizing a plan of efficient reform while the wheels of the 
State continued to be clogged, and the avenues of justice to be choked by obser- 
vances which were wholly irreconcileable with the known wants of the Empire 
and the established principles of equity, 

It remains for me to state that the meeting closed with an understanding 
that the Turkish Ministers would present a statement of existing privileges, 
spiritual and eivil, ancient and modern, together with an enumeration of such 
additions as it occurred to them to propose. In answer to an inquiry on my 
part, whether the intended state nent was to be left open for new suggestions 
und amendments, the Grand Vizier replied in the affirmative, and seemed not to 
have any definite limitations in view, 

We are to meet again ina week, and Prince Calliniachi is to assist at our 
deliberations. 1 proposed an earlier day, but the whole interval was thought 
indispensable for preparing the statement and talking over its contents. 

I have, &c. 7 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


(No. 96.) 


My Lord, Foreign Office, January 24, 1856. 

I HAVE learnt, with great satisfaction, by your Excellency’s despatch 
No. 29 of the Sth of January, the good disposition evinced by the Turkish 
Ministers in ‘the first meeting respecting the fourth point, which was held at 
Constantinople on the 9th instant; and I have to instruct you, in the event of 
the Turkish Ministers continuing to display the same laudable spirit, to inform 
them of the pleasure with which Her Majesty’s Government have learnt their 
mode of proceeding upon the great questions of toleration and reform respecting 
which, for the welfare and prosperity of the Ottoman Empire, Her Majesty's 
Government feel an anxious solicitude, and the settlement of which the Powers 
of Europe are entitled to expect, now that Turkey is to become an integral part 
of the European system. 

Your Excellency cannot too strongly impress upon the Turkish Ministers 
the importance of avoiding delay in coming to un arrangement with respect to 
the first and fourth points, in order that Russia may have no pretext for taking 
a in the discussions on those matters at Constantinople when the negotiations 
br peace commence. 

Iam, &c.

My Lord, Constantinople, Janwary 14, 1856. 

I RECEIVED yesterday from Fuad Pusha the enumeration of privileges 
in esse or in posse, which the Turkish Ministers promised to deliver, before the 
appointed meeting ten days hence, to the three Representatives, with whom they 
have agreed to confer on the subject of its contents, 

Unfortunately the paper is drawn up in Turkish, and of such length as to 
make the transmission of it to your Lordship by to-day's messenger an utter 
impossibility, notwithstanding the skill and aibeenes of the translators. 

A paper has also been composed, in anticipation of further proceedings, by 
each of my colleagues, and, in justice to Mer Majesty's Government, I have not 
thought it right to stand in the back ground. 

There will, probably, be an interchange of our respective views and 
suggestions at the next meeting, and f think it will be desirable for me and my 
two colleagues to ascertain those of each other with some degree of exactness 
before we cnter into discussion with the Sultan's Ministers. 

In the mean time I forward to your Lordship, herewith, the memorandum 
to which | have consigned my portion of the common labour, and most earnestly 
do I desire that the truths expressed in it may engage the concurrence of my 
fellow-lubourers, and obtain, however modified, the cordial acceptance of the 
Porte.

principles rather than measures, and in framing propositions to give them an

at best, a sketch, and may, perhaps, be subjected to amendment before pre.

Memorandum. 


L'EMPIRE et la souveraineté de la dynastie Ottomane dans leur juste 
extension se rattachent de nos jours au grand systéme Européen, et les 
Puissaneces amies et alliges de la Porte ont constamment ’ ceeur d'¢loigner tout 
danger qui pourrait les atteindre. Le désir de ces mémes Cours de fournir 4 Sa 
Majesté le Sultan les occasions, pour ne pas dire les moyens, d’asseoir son 
indépendance sur des bases solides et permanentes n'admet pas de question. 

Le progrés de la guerre fait prévoir que selon toute probabilité les opéra- 
tions combincées auront, tOt ou tard, pour résultat une pacification conforme aux 
veeux de Europe et aux besoins de la Turquie. Mais ce qui peut aider 
Mavance Vachévement d'une si belle ceuvre n'en est pas moins digne d’attention. 
Tl est urgent, au contraire, de fixer des & présent les principes et de concerter, 
autant que possible, les mesures néceseaires pour en obtenir tout Veffet au 
hénctice de VEmpire, objet de tant de soins et de sacrifices. Garantir le 
territoire Ottoman contre les dangers d'un envahissement, et l'abandonner en 
meme terms aux vices intérieurs qui le font dépérir 4 vue c’ocil, que serait-il, si 
non une ilusion pernicieuse, dont les penples, martyrs de la guerre, pourraient 
A juste titre demander raison ? 

la Porte est'en droit de dire que dés lorigine elle s'est montrée préte A 
tolérer les cultes autres que le sien propre, en ajoutant a la tolérance de ces 
cultes un nombre considerable de priviléges accordés aux peuples qui en 
frisaient profession. La confirmation de ces dons précieux, transmis de 
Souverain en Souverain, lui ont valu, 4 peu d'exceptions prés, l'obdissance 
et la fidélité de ceux parmi ses sujets qui, sans lui étre unis par les liens 
de la foi, sont néanmoins soumis au sceptre du Sultan. [lle ne eesse non 
plus de professer les principes de la reforme administrative, et d'aviser aux 
moyens de leur donner unc impulsion, ct surtout une application, telle que ses 
sujets de toute classe et les amis de |'Empire se rejouiraient de voir réalisée. 

En lui rendant cette justice, en appréciant aussi les motifs qui lui ont 
inspiré la résolution de repousser les prétentions de la Russie, les alligs de son 
gré veulent maintenant s‘associer aux travauxdestinés & en perpétuer l'exclusion. 
Par ladoption, la promulgation et la mise en vigueur i temps de mesures, d’autant 
plus efficaces qu'elles seraient équitables et généreuses, la Porte fera taire la 
reproche de ne pas avoir employé tous les moyens que l'occasion lui offre pour 
assurer son avenir, De ces mesures l'initiative et lexdéeution lui appartiennent, 
Les Puissances appelées au secours de la Turquie ne sauraicnt refuser leur 
coopération, L'Europe, qui reconnait la nécessité des réformes dans Vintérét de 
la paix et de la civilisation, se fera, sans doute, un devoir d’en consucrer l'accom- 
plissement, et de veiller, en ami, A leur maintien. 

La question des priviléges est tellement lie avee celle des réformes admi- 
nistratives que toutes les deux semblent devoir se ranger dans le méme eadre, 
Les rapprocher de maniére 4 faire disparaitre les différences qui séparent les 
Mussulmans des Ruyahs, serait un pas de géant dans la régencration progressive 
de | Empire. 7 

Il est 4 espérer que le temps viendra en aide i la législation pour effectuer 
cet immense bienfait. 

La Porte, en continuant i prendre le devant, se fera encore plus @honneur 
et consultera ses meilleurs intéréts, Tout concourt pour lui inspirer le courage

No. 156. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received February 1.) 


(No. 60.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, January 16, 1856. 

THE whole of this morning has been devoted by M. Thouvenel, Baron 
Prokesch, and myself to the question, the twofold question, of privileges and 
reforms. We met by agreement at the French Embassy. The memorandum, 
which [ had drawn up and transmitted to your Lordship with my despatch 
No. 51 of the 14th instant, was read over and approved by my colleagues, with 
the reserve, in which [ partook, of eventual amendments. I observed with 
pleasure, and drew a favourable inference from, the spontaneous accordance of 
our opinions. 

n talking over some of the points, language such as I should have held 
myself, was used by the Austrian as well as by the French Representative. At 
their suggestion, L introduced a clause for extending to foreigners the privilege 
of holding land in this country. I had contemplated the addition of this very 
essential auxiliary to an effective system of reform, but reserved the expression 
of it for a later and more promising occasion. Baron Prokesch stated that in 
conversing with the Grand Vizier, he had found that Minister well disposed 
on the subject. J] cannot say that any such intimation has ever reached me from 
a Turkish quarter; and on the subject of holding land, I believe that in 
England we still lie open to the charge of a strong remnant of exclusiveness. 

From the French lmbassy we went to the Grand Vizier’s house in Constan- 
tinople. Prince Callimachi was there with the two Turkish Ministers. Our 
proceedings were necessarily limited to an open perusal of the ‘Turkish enumera- 
tion of privileges, which had been previously communicated by the Grand Vizier, 
and to a free exchange of remarks and explanations relative to certain passages. 
A translation of this paper is inclosed herewith. Jt was understood to bea 
mere basis for discussion. It must be allowed to exhibit in its full extent a 
greater amount of liberality towards the Sultan's Christian subjects than is 
generally supposed. ‘Two things are particularly striking, namely, the degree 
of power attributed to the patriarchs and other dignitaries of the Christian 
churches, particularly of the Greek, over their respective dependent and the 
little provision made for protecting the latter from any abusive exercise of 
authority. Upon the whole, it may be affirmed with contidence that when the 
ancient and modern immunities are taken together, reality of application and 
practical enforcement are more palpably wanted than any considerable number 
of additional measures; and it must be added in fairness that the best work of 
amendment will be imperfect so long as the Greek and other Christian commu- 
nities are not brought decidedly and practically within the pale of reform. 

It was suggested, I think with propriety, that a Commission composed so 
as to command a sufficient degree of respect and confidence, should he charged 
with the duty of inquiring and reporting for that purpose. 

It was agreed that another meeting should be held on the 19th instant, 
and as the three Representatives intend to consult together beforehand, with the 
view of going into Conference with opinions completely identical, | venture to 
hope that essential progress will be made in our joint task before the end of the 
week, 

I have, &c. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

Inclasure in No, 156. 


Memorandum as to Privileges granted ab antiquo to the Christian Subjects of the 
Porte with reference to Spiritual Affairs. 
(Translation.) 

WHEN the Greek Patriarchate falls vacant, the Archbishops whose sees 
are in the neighbourhood of the capital, being those of Heraclea, Cygicus, 
Nicomedia, and Chalcedon, together with the Synod at Constantinople, proceed 
to elect a prelate in accordance with their religious rites and their opinion, alter 
which they address a report announcing their choice to the Porte, who confirms 
the person chosen in the dignity of Patriarch. The elections of the Armenian 
and (Armeno) Catholic Patriarchs are effected by the spiritual heads and notables 
of those nations and their investitures, as in the previous case, 

‘The Patriarchs, as long as they do not oppress their flocks or act contrary 
to their religion, are not dismissed, or unless they voluntarily resign, but hold 
office for life. If, as said before, they are dismissed, upen the unanimous 
consent of the Synod, after an accurate investigation of the facts. 

The direction and arrangement of their religious affairs, their income and 
expenditure, and other business of the Patriarchate does not depend, according 
to their religious customs, upon the actual Patriarchs exclusively; as these 
affairs are under the control of the Archbishops, the Patriarchate seals are 
consigned to those of the latter, chosen among themselves for the purpose, 
In the same manner the seals of all the convents in the Empire are in the hands 
of the Synod. é; 

From a very ancient period the Archbishops are unable to proceed to 
Constantinople occasionally without the express sanction of the Patriarch and 

rod, 
gl The subjects of all classes must have recourse to the Patriarchs upon 
all matters within the competency of the latter, whom they must obey in 
erything. 
see ihe Patriarch names and deposes, without hindrance, the Archbishops, 
Vicars, Bishops, and priests, in all the places under his jurisdiction. : 

When it becomes necessary to change a Greek Archbishop, the seal of the 
Synod must be used for the purpose; and when a Bishop or Vicar of another 
community is to be changed, a memorial, bearing the seal of the Patriarch and 
Medjlis of that nation, is to be presented, upon which the person elected will 
receive a Berat, enabling him to take possession of his post, and with it a firman 
inclosing the conditions imposed upon him by the Porte. No one can be 
named Archbishop or Vicar upon representations coming from other quarters, 

If the Pashas or Cadis denounces the misconduct of an Archbishop, Vicar, 
or Bishop, and demand his dismissal or exile, the complaints are not to be 
entertained without reference being made to the Patriarch for information - and 
iby chance a firman should be issued in consequence of the complaints of a 
Pasha or Cadi, the necessary steps are taken, | : 

Ifan Archbishop, Viear, or Bishop comes to Constantinople on business, 

leaving in his place aporiies priest to represent him, no obstacle is met with by 
him in such a proceeding. 
He No Fitoriaeeisse Tap be offered by any one to the Patriarchs, Archbishops, 
Bishops, Vicars, and Priests reading the Scriptures in their dwellings, lighting 
lamps and candles, hanging curtains and images, fixing stools, swinging censers, 
or holding a crozier in the exercise of their ntes. fie 

No objection can be made to the members of any community whatever 
burying their dead in accordance with their rites, hor any molestation olfered 
to their churches in their possession from old times, or their holy places, 
whether in Constantinople or in the Provinces, 

Respect is paid to the memorials bearing the patriarchal or synodal seal, 
or that of the National Assembly. very request having reference to their rites 
is accorded. wens 

No one can search the churches or convents of any community without a 
firman. : ; 

No one can hinder the repair, upon the ancient plan and under the 
recognised principles, of the churches and convents of any community; and 


144 


these ancient churches cannot be transferred to others upon (mere) petitions 
and written representations,* 

No claim can be raised upon the properties ofa church or convent on 
wecount of another person's debts, nor can they be interfered with or held as 
security ; and if by any means any one has taken them, he will be forced, by 
law, to restore them, and consigned to the quarter whence they were taken. 

All individuals of whatever communitity who are desirous of being married 
or divorced under the rites of their nation, must do so by means of the Patriarch 
or his representative, without the interference of any one else. When the 
Patriarch wishes to punish a priest for having married a couple uncanonically, 
and without a patriarchal, episcopal, or vicarial license, no interference can be 
made, When a dispute arises between two persons with reference to marriage 
or other subjects, and the Patriarch wishes to administer oaths in the church, or 
punish any one with excommunication, no opposition should be made by the Cadis 
or their deputies, tis contrary to the Christian religion for a person to divorce 


his wife, to commit bigamy, orto marry more than three times after the death of 


his wife; and the violation of this canonical law must be punished, Entrance 
to the church must also be forbidden to those who violate the ecclesiastical law 
of marriage; and neither the Cadis, Naibs, nor other authorities can force the 
priests to bury the body of one deceased under the ban of excommunication. 

Archbishops, Bishops, Vicars, Priests, Monks, and other Christians can 
bequeath property to their Patriarehs or churches, or to the poor, consisting of 
same article, or of one-third of their possessions. On their death, their wills 
are in force and valid, after the rules of their religion; and, after the reception 
of evidence, the property is recovered from the heirs of the deceased by the 
agency of the law. When Archbishops, Bishops, Priests, Monks, and Nuns die 
without heirs, the Patriarch or his deputy receive the money, effects, horses, and 
all other property of the deceased, in order to hand it over to the public chest; 
and the Kassam, or distributor of dead men’s effects, the Mutevellis, and the 
Muhassils cannot offer any interference. With reference to those who die 
leaving heirs, neither the Patriareh nor his deputy can seize any of the property 
belonging to them, with the exception of their ecclesiastical robes, which are to 
le deposited in the vestry. 

The Greek Metropolitans, the Armenian and Catholic Delegates, Priests, 
und Monks, who may conduct themselves ina manner contrary to the prescriptions 
of their respective sects, are punished by their Patriarchs, each according to his 
rite; their hair is shaved, themselves deprived of the priestly office, and the 
churches in their charge transferred to others; the necessary priests are elected 
in tie room of the clergymen thus dismissed from their charge in accordance 
with their miles, and without any impediment or obstacle from other quarters. 
And those priests or monks who are not attached to any church or monastery, 
but roam about from place to place and corrupt the real Greek and Armenian 
subjects of the Porte, contrary to their rules, are apprehended by means of the 
Patriarehs under a firman, and the necessary punishment is inflicted upon them 
wwecording to law. 

So interference on the part of any one is permitted with reference to the 
property and live stock attached to the ehurehes. and monasteries, and all their 
appurtenances situate in places dependent upon the patriarchate of each Patriarch, 
as Well as to other churches; but such property is held and enjoyed as practised 
from olden times, 

The money paid by the Greek, Armenian, and Catholie subjects of the Porte 
formerly as taxes to the treasury, oras voluntary contributions, the revenues and 
expenditure of the Patriarchate, and the (personal) patriarchal income, are paid now 
i# before, and no hesitation or opposition isallowed. iftheagents of the churches 
or monasteries appropriate or use any of the treasury or patriarchal dues, their 
accounts may be examined, and themselves forced to pay compensation without 
any interference. If the Patriarch have claims, in whatever place it may occur, 
upon the episcopal delegates or clergy out of these dues, the persons appointed 
by them to collect the said sums, having received un Imperial order for the same, 
und also a letter from the appointing Patriarch, receive legal assistance from the 
Cadis and Naibs, and the money is realised; while the persons thus appointed 
to secure patriarchal claims, or their deputies, receive assistance in every way

when on their journeys, and attention is paid to their safety in travelling ; nor

No interference or contravention of ancient practice is carried on by the 
Customs authorities at the various ports and gates »with reference to the con-

produce” and “charity,” and destined for the Patriarch’s own consumption,

churches, are not subject to any demand of customs or “octroi” duty at the 
Various ports and gates, / 

Those of the Greek, Armenian, or Catholic persuasion, who have sincerely 
and resolvedly embraced Islam are naturally, it is clear, exempt from inter- 
ference on the point of religion; those, however, who under their own free will 
have not embraced Islam cannot be subjected to violence or compulsion from 
any quarter. 

é imprisonment of any Greck, Armenian, or Catholic priest becoming 
necessary, under legal permission, the offender is kept in custody at the 
Patriarchate, and cannot be imprisoned elsewhere. 

No interference can be made regarding the patriarchal staff or crosier, the 
horses he rides, his men, his or their dresses and robes; or regarding the 
provincial delegates’ or bishop’s horses and animals; nor can the delegates and 
bishops be obliged to afford the use of their residences as “konack” (or 
traveller's lodging). 

Should any law-suit arise between Metropolitans, Delegates, or Bishops, and 
other individuals, it is referred to the eapital, and examined and decided 
according to the principles of equity, and with a reference to its special nature 
and conditions. 

The Patriarchs and Capu Kiayas, together with fifteen men of the suites, are 
exempted from all taxes, Goyernment imposts, or customary levies or demands. 


Conduct of Clerical Functionaries as practised by them under the name of the 
above "' Spiritual Privileges.” 


If the Metropolitans, their Delegutes, or the Bishops, levy dues at their 
pleasure from the Christian subjects, neither as regards the amount, nor the 
method of levying, do the State authorities exercise any interference. 

Each sect may open a school of any kind and in any place, nor has the 
State any interference with or knowledge for cognizance of the method of 
instruction therein followed. On a Patriarch, Metropolitan, Delegate, or Bishop, 
demanding the exile of an individual, whoever he may be, among the Christians 
on the grounds of his having acted contrary to the rites of his sect, such 
demand is complied with and executed without a trial. ) 

Each Patriarch prohibits the publication and perusal of booka among his 
nation and punishes those who act in a contrary manner. 


Concessions recently granted in Spiritual Matters. 


The permission formerly accorded to the reparation of churches is now 
applied to their extension or renewed construction. 

In the cnse of a Christian subject wishing to embrace Mahometanism, 
inquiries must be made, with a view to ascertaining that no kind of compulsion 
or violence has been employed, before the Porte in the eapital and betine the 
Medjlises in the provinces, in the presence of the person’s relations and connections: 
and if he has voluntarily abandoned his faith, he is to be accepted. Permission 
is not accorded to children whe have not reached the age of puberty or diserimi- 
nation to change their religion. 


Ancient Condition of non-Mussulman Subjects, 


_ Atal times non-Mussulman subjects have held roperty of every descrip. 
tion similarly with the Mussulmans, and under precisely the same title-deeds,

While the Mussulman subjects used to pay the State an “‘ushur,” or tenth, 
upon the produce of the soil, the mon-Mussulman subjects used to pay the State 
a “ sumn,” or eighth. | 

Mussulman subjects paying 4 per cent. upon their merchandises as customs 
duty, non-Mussulman subjects seen to pay a duty of 5 per cent. 

Non-Mussulman subjects were exempt from military service, and in its 
place paid compensation tax, or ‘tizyeh; " besides this, they paid under various 
names and titles different taxes, from which the Mussulmans were exempt, and 
rendered certain services under the names of “amja” ( ) and“ angaria ” 
(corvéc). 

They had no permission to travel out of the Ottoman dominions, except. 
such as were at liberty to trade with Europe. 

The whole of the public employments and ranks were universally bestowed 
upon Mussulmans, and the non-Mussulman subjects were inelig ble to all of 
them, 

Distinctions existed between the Mussulman and non-Mussulman subjects 
in the form and colour of their dress and houses, in all transactions and in 
employment of terms. 


Concessions accorded to non-Mussulman Subjects since the Tunzimat. 


All the Sultan’s subjects are in the enjoyment of equal rights, and security 
of life and property and preservation of honour and character. 

The difference in taxation which existed between the Mussulman and non- 
Mussuliman subject is entirely abolished, aud the whole of the Ottoman subjects 
are now upon an equal footing as regards the payment. of taxes, whether of 
general taxation, of tithes, of custom or other dues, 

Forced services of the nature of‘ amja,” or corvcée, are completely abolished 
for all classes of Ottoman subjects. 

All classes of Ottoman subjects are at liberty to travel wherever they may 
please, whether for commerce, for information, or for pleasure. 

Ottoman subjects are admitted to military service, and the commutation- 
tax 1s nbolished; it has, however, been decided that a tax be levied, called 
“Military Aid Pund” (Tydne-i-askeriyé) as a substitute for their rendering 
personal service. 

Non-Mussulman subjects are employed in the service of the Porte accord- 
ing ta their merits and capacity, and participate in the ranks conferred by the 
tate, 

In the provincial assemblies of each Eyalet, Liva, and Kaza, each nation or 
sect 1s naturally represented by its spiritual chiefs; but in addition to them they 
have other members. respectively, who all equally ‘have a voice in civil and 
municipal affairs: and in, all causes tried before these assemblies the opinions 
and decisions of the Mussulman and mon-Mussulman members are upon an 
equal footing. 

Tn the Tidjaret Courts, and Courts-of Inquiry, established in the capital and 
the provinces, the members, likewise, consist of Mussulmans and non-Mussul- 
mans, Whose opinions and decisions are on a footing of perfect equality. 

Each nation may hold an assembly in its Patriarchate for the transaction of 
its own special national affairs, such as the control of hospitals, schools, and the 
like ; in the provinees, likewise, each nation chooses and appoints its own chiefs 
in the cities and the villages. 

In the provinces, the larger and the smaller districts (Eyalet, Liva, and 
Kaza), the general taxation is levied by the Porte, but cach nation effects its 
own assessment for purposes of raising its own national taxation, and the amount 
being obtained by means of its chiefs is deposited in the national chest. All 
Ottoman subjects being equal before the law, the legal tribunals aceept the 
evidence and information of subjects of all classes. 

All distinctions affecting non-Mussulinan subjcets in the matter of dress, 
residence, and other particulars, are completely done away with, and everybody 
is at liberty to dress himself, to build his house, and to move about precisely as he 
may like, while as regards forms, titles, and the use of terms, a treatment of 
perfect equality is practised towards all subjects of the Ottoman Empire.

No. 157. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon,—(Received February 1.) 
(No. 73.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, January 19, 1856. 


IN concert with my French and Austrian colleagues, I have devoted nearly 
the whole of to-day and yesterday to the pending ques of privileges and 
reforms. The progress has been considerable in so fur as concerns our under- 
standing with the Turkish Ministers. ‘The result of two separate mectings on 
our part was a paper founded principally on my memorandum, and expressive of 
our joint opinion after due inquiry and discussion. This series of articles, which 
is inclosed herewith, became the subject-matter of a third interview between the 
three Representatives and the two Turkish Ministers, assisted by Prince 
Callimachi. We met, as before, at the Grand Vizier’s house. We agreed to 
discuss the paper, alter a general perusal of its contents, article by article, noting 
down at the close of each whether, and with what alterations or reserves, 1b ‘coul 
be accepted unanimously. 1 observed with pleasure that the Grand Visier and 
Fuad Pasha were to all appearance favourably disposed to the acceptance of our 
proposals. ‘Their assents, however, were varied with different degrees | of 
admission; they intimated, with respect to some leading points, an apprehension 
of difficulties elsewhere, and they reserved for the whole, in distinet terms, the 
approval of their colleagues and the sanction of their Government, — It is further 
to be observed, that on two or three points they drew back at first from the 
statement which had been previously given in by themselves. They seemed to 
have much to surmount with respect to the abolition of all restraints on 
conscience in religious matters, and to the adimissibility of all classes under rules 
of general application to public employment. ee 

It was agreed that the question of administrative improvement did not 
belong to the fourth point in the same sense as that of Christian privileges 
belonged to it. But no one denied that the two questions were virtually bound 
up together as the essential elements of an effective reform in this country, The 
Grand Vizier avowed with laudable frankness that a thorough administrative 
reform was indispensable to the existence of the Empire. - a 

It can hardly escape your Lordship’s notice, on comparing the series of 
articles with the propositions contained in my memorandum, that although much 
of what might seem at first sight to have been lopped off, has been only con- 
densed or moulded into a better form, yet that in several instances not only 
prominent details but’ even important securities have been dropped. The change 
is attributable to a very simple cause. [It was impossible to avoid some 
differences of opinion in so extensive and complicated a work, and it was felt by 
all that whatever discussions might be found unavoidable at a later period, there 
would be great advantage in placing an unaniinous opinion in the outset under 
the consideration of our Turkish allies. It was evident that their acceptance of 
the plan, as now presented to them, would be an earnest of further success both 
in urging the adoption of corresponding measures, and in obtaining whatever else 
might be necessary for the completion and enforeement of a new administrative 
system. . 

I have, &e. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

No. 159. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No. 126.) 


My Lord, Foreign Office, February 4, 1856. 

WITH reference to your Excellency’s despatch No. 60 of the 16th of 
January, I have to inform you that Her Majesty’s Government entirely approve 
the course of proceeding which you have followed relative to the questions of 
privileges and reforms in Turkey. 

_ Her Majesty's Government have learnt with satisfaction the identity of 
views which exists between your Excellency and your colleagues upon the most 
important of the points which have come under discussion. 

You will express to the Porte to the thanks of Her Majesty's Government 
and their cordial satisfaction at the liberal and enlightened spirit in which they 
have met the proposals that it was the duty of the allies of the Sultan to submit 
to the Porte; and you will further express the conviction of Her Majesty's 
Government that by giving real and practical effect to the principles which have 
been laid down, the best interests of the Sultan will be promoted, and His 
Majesty will carn for himself the sympathy and gratitude of the Christian 
Powers, which will prove the firmest support of the position which Turkey is 
now about to occupy in Europe. 

Tam, ke. 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

Inclosure 1 in No. 162, 
Acting Vice-Consul Barker to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Aleppo January 7, 1856. 


| HAVE the honour to acknowledge the receipt of your despatch dated 
Sth December, sent by steam-transport ‘‘ Armenian,” which reached my hands 
on the Ist of this month, wherein accurate information is required respectin 
the alleged execution of a subject of the Porte at Aleppo, for blasphemy or 
conversion, 

After a careful investigation, and going through all the late Mr. Derry’s reports, 
from 1849 to 1853, about the date when I remember such an execution to have 
taken place, I have not been able to find any trace of such an event. 

But that such executions have taken place, from time to time, ure well- 
established facts, 

The last took place in 1852, and the following are the leading facts :— 
Latouf, or Abd-el-Latouf, a Moslem and cavass of the Russian Consul in this city 
M. Raphael de Picciotto, about forty-five years of age, had’ become nearly blind, 
and on that account had become a supernumerary cavass. Having had some 
differences with the inhabitants of his quarter, he blasphemed the religion of 
Mahomet, upon which his adversaries accused him before the Medjlis, and he 
was imprisoned, and a maysatta or certificate was signed and sent to Constanti- 
pople. As the reply from the capital was long in coming, he was set at liberty, 
but on an order arriving for his execution, he was taken to the bazaar before 
the gate of the Khan Fassibieh, and there decapitated, 7 

His body, however, was buried with the usual Moslem rites in the burying- 
ground opposite the door of Bistan-eleKhalé, The tombstone bears the date; 


162 


it is broken in two pieces and fastened with iron. I have the honour to inclose 
herewith copy of the inscription with a translation. 
A similar execution took place some years previous in the quarter called 
Barkoosa, I| have not had time to find out the particulars. 
(Signed) E. B. B. BARKER,

No, 163. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 
(No. 160.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, February 9, 1856. 


| HAVE received your Excellency’s despatch No. 90, inclosing a report 
from Mr, Consul Barker of the circumstances under which Abd-el-Lateef was 
executed at Aleppo, in the year 1852, for blasphemy; and | have to state to 
your Excellency in reply, that it would be desirable to know what the exact 
nature of the offence alleged against this man was; why, because he quarrelled 
with his neighbours, he blasphemed Mahomet; in what terms he blasphemed ; 
and why, having been decapitated and buried as a Moslem, a tombstone, 
recording that he was a martyr, was allowed by the Turkish authorities. 
I have consequently to instruct your Excellency to call upon Her Majesty's 
Consul at Aleppo to furnish any information he can obtain upon these points. 
I am, &e. 
(Signed) CLARENDON.
