No, 164. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—( Received February 11.) 


(No. 100.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, January 28, 1856. 

HAVING received information from a sure, though private, source that 
the Turkish Ministers were startled by difficulties proceeding from their 
colleagues in the Council, and hesitating as to the acceptance of the propesitions 
given in by the three Representatives, | thought it my duty to make an 
additional effort for their encouragement and support. The points from which 
they were most inclined to recede, are, those of religious persecution; equal 
admissibility to office; and the possession of landed property by foreigners. To 
these important questions 1 therefore confined miyself, as your Lordship will 
borate on reading the copy, inclosed herewith, of my instruction to Mr, Pisani. 
t was no longer a time for recurring to arguments which had been previously 
exhausted. Respecting the first of the three questions, I sent in the official note 
which, as your Lordship is aware, had been long since prepared. On the 
uestion of landed property | made a statement calculated to remove every 
doubt of any difference of opinion between me and my colleagues, With 
regard to public offices, | repeated in decided terms the opinion jointly expressed 
at our preceding conference. 

My note on religious persecutions and the rights of conscience in matters 
of religion, has received an addition at the end, and a few amendments in the 


15d 


receding paragraphs. Your Lordship will be able to appreciate their extent 
ond steatieatiqn OF comparing the tee versions, the first of which was forwarded 
with one of my preceding reports last year. I stated at the time that some 
changes might be found necessary, and [ hope that in the additional sentences I 
have offered a facility or two for attaining the desired conclusion. Sedition 
under religious pretences ought to be repressed in Turkey, as elsewhere ; and 
blasphemy, when kept within its natural limits, and not visited with any extreme 
infliction, may fairly be left on tlie list of punishable offences, 

The French Ambassador had often expressed a wish that I should postpone 
the complete execution of your Lordship's instructions on this subject until the 
general question of privileges and reform should be treated with reference to the 
fourth point of negotiation. 3 

Having complied with his Excellency’s wish I felt a greater responsibility 
as to leaving open the door for an unfavourable decision without the advantage 
of bringing to bear upon it the full weight of opinion and requisition whieh had 
been placed in my hands by Her Majesty's Government. 

Mr. Pisani has recorded in what manner the note and the instruction were 
received by Fuad Pasha and the Grand Vizier. A copy of his report ts inclosed 
herewith. ; 

I have since learnt from him that the joint propositions were taken ito 
consideration at a mecting of the Council the night before last, and generally 
sanctioned by that Assembly, with the exception of what concerns the possession 
of landed property by foreigners, and the procedure in Civil Courts. The subject 
matter of my official note Is also reserved for some ulterior communication, und 
Lam under an impression that it will not improbably be made a ground of 
resistance, which nothing short of a very decided perseverance in support of it 
will overcome. 

Another and I trust a final interview is to take place to-morrow on the 
whole question of privileges. 

I have, &c. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


Inclosure | in No. 164, 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Mr. Pisani. 


Monsieur, Palais & Angleterre, le 25 Janvier, 1856. 


SOUS le méme pli avec cette instruction vous recevrez un memorandum 
en Francais relatif aux priviléges et reformes administratives dont la Porte 
s'occupe de concert avec ses alliés, et une note officielle en Anglais, accompagnée 
d'une traduction Turque, relative aux persécutions religieuses. Vous vous 
empresserez de présenter ces pidces 4 son Excellence Fuad Pacha apres en avoir 
donné lecture A son Altesse le Grand Vizier. Celle qui concerne les priviléges 
est confidentielle, mais les Ministres Ottomans peuvent en faire tel usage qu’ils 
jugent convenable, en la soumettant meme & Sa Majesté le Sultan et a leurs 
collézues. C'est le méme memorandum dont il a été question dans notre 
seconde réunion chez Je Grand Vizier, 

I] mest revenu que je suis supposé d’attacher moins d’importance que les 
Représéntants de France et d’Autriche i ce que la permission de posstder des 
biens fonds en Turquie soit accordée aux étrangers. J'ai A coeur de faire savoir 
que cette supposition est tout-A-fait erronce. Je suis convaincu, au contraire, 
que le rapprochement cordial de la Turquie & I'Europe est essentiel au bien-ctre 
et au maintien de Empire Ottoman, et que la mesure la plus efficace dans ce 
gens ne serait autre que la faculté accordée aux étrangers d'y posséder des 
immeubles de toute espéce.

expres de mon Gouvernement, soit de bouche soit par cert. Les heésitations

jfavuis déja prépare il y a deux mois environ, et j'espére que la Sublime Porte

Il ne me reste qu’d ajouter un mot en confirmation de mes opinions, déja

Inclosure 2 in No. 164. 
Note addressed to the Porte by Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


SEVERAL years have passed away since the execution of a young 
Armenian at Constantinople, who had renounced the Mahometan faith and 
returned to that of Christ, followed shortly afterwards by another similar 
infliction at Broussa, excited the indignation of Europe, and induced tne Govern- 
ments of England and France to unite in preventing the repetition of so great a 
scandal. The Sultan and his Ministers were not deaf to their persevering 
remonstrances. The promise given personally by His Imperial Majesty to the 
British, and also to the French Ambassador, in addition to the official engage- 
ments of the Porte, had every appearance of sufficing to secure the Christian 
subjects of the Empire from religious persecution, and, in particular, to abolish 
the barbarous practice of putting seceders to death. 

It cannot, however, be conceded that in practice these diplomatic agree- 
ments between the Porte and its allies, though crowned with the Sultan's 
promise, have not been attended with results so steady or effectual as the French 
and British Governments were entitled to expect. In two known instances, 
individuals have lately suffered death by the hand of the executioner on account 
of religious considerations. In other cases, imprisonment, ill-treatment, and 
forced removal to-a distance have been employed, either to obtain an unwilling 
recantation, or to appease the malice of a fanatical populace. Whole families of 
Catholic converts were on one occasion driven from their homes and sent across 
the sea into Asia, under circumstances revolting to humanity, and operating on 
their lives with futal effect. Protestants, though recognized by Imperial firmans, 
and recommended to the local authorities, have been exposed to unmerited 
vexations, while Turkish functionaries, far from protecting them against the 
violence of their Christian persecutors, have themselves but too often connived 
at the work of persecution. 

Surely the time is come when these remnants of an ignorant age should be 
completely and permanently effaced, when the Porte’s enlightened declarations 
should be carried into full effect. Acts which, to speak plainly, are absurdities 
in the view of reason, and horrors in that of humanity, can no longer with 
prudence defy the common sense and good feeling of nations united by the 
closest ties. ‘True policy condemns, and civilization rejects them. A legislative 
system which retains the anomaly is dangerous to those who uphold it, A 
nation, incapable of throwing off the taint, forfeits at length the sympathy of 
others, and finally becomes the victim of its insulated weakness, 

Deeply convinced of these important truths, and anxious to rescue the 
Ottoman Empire from evils generated by neglect of them, a friendly Govern- 
iment, in concert with a powerlul und generous ally, appeals once more to that 
wisdom and magoanimity which on critical oceasions have rarely been wanting 
to the counsels of the Porte. 

England and France, as every one knows, are making gigantic efforts, and 
submitting to enormous sacrifices, in support of the Sultan's Empire, ‘Though 
many glorious successes have marked the progress of their arms, they have still 
a wide field of exertion before them, and Providence alone can determine the 
final issue, 

Such are the circumstances under which the Undersigned is instructed by 
his Government to press this subject most earnestly on the attention of the 
Ottoman Porte. He is ordered to observe that the Great European Powers can 
never consent to perpetuate, by the triumphs of their fleets pnd wimies, the 


155 


enforcement in Turkey of a law which is not only a standing insult to them, but 
4 source of crucl persecution to their fellow-Christians. They are entitled to 
demand, and the British Government distinctly demands, that the Mahometan 
who turns Christian shall be as free from every kind of punishment on that 
account as the Christian who embraces the Mahometan faith, It stands to 
reason that, in all such cases, the human conscience being left free, the temporal 
arm must abstain from interiering to coerce its spiritual convictions. 

The Undersigned entertains a firm hope that the Sultan’s Ministers, 
enlightened by experience, and duly attentive to the exigencies of time and 
circumstance, will not only do justice to the motives of this demand, but recog- 
nize the necessity of redressing a grievance which stands in such painful 
contrast with the services rendered to Turkey by Christian Powers. 

It rests with the Porte to determine in what manner this just and indis- 
pensable reform may be most satisfactorily completed. ‘The Undersigned cannot 
for a moment doubé that the Turkish Government, in doing away with the last 
remnant of sanguinary fanaticism, and clearing its antecedent pledges from every 
shade of uncertainty, will maintain an inviolable protection over all its subjects, 
of whatever creed, who, following the dictates of conscience in religious matters, 
may exercise its rights without seeking to disturb the peace of society. 

Hitherto the British Government has treated with similar liberality the 
millions of Mussulman population brought under its rule in India, and it would 
Cail lament the necessity of adopting a less generous policy towards them. 

fhe Porte, unwilling to incur so deep a responsibility, will, doubtless, bear 
in mind that society may be secured against sedition working under religious 
forms, and that religion itself may be secured from reproach, by special enact- 
ments applied with judgment and moderation to cases of open turbulence and 
undisguised blasphemy. Her present and first obligation is to meet the demands 
of her ally with gratitude equal to the circumstances which inspire that senti- 
ment, and with wisdom adapted to the political prospects of her Menge and to 
the welfare of those who profess the Mussulman creed beyond as well as within 
ils limits. 

The Undersigned, &c. 

(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

(No, 174.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, February 13, 1856. 


WITH reference to your Excellency’s despatch No. 111 of the 30th of 
January, I have to state to you that Her Majesty's Government approve of your 
having accepted the Porte’s version of the clause relating to religious liberty 
with the reserve explained in your despatch. 

As regards the privilege of holding land, Her Majesty’s Government 
consider it of vast importance to Turkey, as there is reason to believe that'a 
large amount of capital is ready for investment in land, upon proper guarantees 
as to tenure, whenever peace is restored.

No. 176. 
The Earl of Clarendon to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


(No, 193.) 
My Lord, Foreign Office, February 18, 1856. 

WITH reference to the question of religious persecutions in Turkey, and 
the efforts which your Excellency, as stated in your despatch No. 100 of the 
28th of January, very properly continues to make to effect the abolition of the 
punishment of death as applied to apostates from Islamism, I have to state to 
your Excellency that Her Majesty’s Government are of opinion that it might be 
strongly represented to the Porte that, as the Turkish Empire is by Treaty 
stipulations to be declared part and parcel of the European system, it is quite 
impossible for the Powers of Europe to acquiesce in the continuance in Turkey 
of a law, and a practice, which is a standing insult to every other nation in 
Europe. If Turkey is to gain the great and important advantage of being 
deemed part of the European family of nations, she must by necessity adapt her 
laws and practices so as to make them compatible with her association with the 
community of States into which she desires to be aden 

itn, 


Ac, 
(Signed) CLARENDON,

No. 177. 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received February 20.) 


(No, 158.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, February 11, 1856. 

LAM unable to write as fully to your Lordship to-day as I had wished, 
being obliged to attend at a conference appointed to take place this morning at 
the Grand Vizier's house, previous to his Highness’ departure for Paris to-morrow. 

I have, however, the honour to inclose, for your Lordship’s ‘information, 
copy of a paper containing certain Articles agreed upon yesterday by M, Thouvenel, 
Baron Prokesch, and myself, with respect to the first point. This paper was 
communicated yesterday to the Turkish Ministers, and we shall, I hope, be able 
to obtain their acceptance of it to-day, though probably not without some 
modifications. ithe 

I reserve all necessary remarks and explanations for the next opportunity, 
which I hope will occur to-morrow, 

The firman respecting the rights and privileges of the non-Mussulman 
anbjects of the Porte, will be forwarded to your Lordship by the next opportu- 
nity. It has received the Sultan’s sanction, and there is every reason fo expect 
that it will be promulgated with due solemnity in two or three days. It iso 
mere translation of the Articles agreed upon, with an addition suggested by me, 
as to the encouragement of agriculture and trade, and the employment of foreign 
capital and industry, 

1 have, Xe. 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

Inclosure in Na. 177. 
Memorandum relative to the First Point. 


1. LES Traités conclus entre la Russie ct la Porte relativement a ln Mol-

No. 178. 


Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received February 24.) 


(No. 160.) 
My Lord, Constantinople, February 11, 1856. 

_ EVERY practicable exertion has been made by me and my colleagues to 
bring the preparatory arrangement of the first and fourth points, as they are 
called, of the impending negotiation to a close before the departure of Aali Pasha 
for Paris. We have succeeded with respect to the latter, and I entertain a 
sanguine hope of being able to complete the former also in time, though we have 
now only twenty-four hours at our disposal for the purpose. Our difficulties 
have been greatly increased by the want of instructions bearing on the particulars, 
or even on the principles, to which it is desirable that our proceedings should be 
adapted in providing for the future condition of Wallachia and Moldavia. 
The field is wide, and offers many difficult and entangled questions for 
consideration. 

_ Your Lordship is aware that there are, chiefly, two principles, either of 
which presents a clear point to set out from, though in different directions One 
is that of a material, the other of a moral, barrier against foreign encroachment. 
Could a strong frontier line be obtained against Russia, such, for instance, as the 
eourse of the Dniester, with its long-established fortresses, it might be well 
worth while to unite the two Provinces under a single Government ; to give to 
its Administration a vigorous and decisive character; to place a foreign Prince 
at its head ; and to entrust him with the command of sufficient forces for the 
defence of the frontier, More than one Power might object to so determined an 
arrangement, but the real European interest attached to it would probably 
overrule the objections, as well at Constantinople as elsewhere. 

The idea of a moral barrier, without excluding the employment of military 
means of defence, addresses itself more particularly to the social and adminis- 
trative condition of the Principalities, an to the welfare of the inhabitants at 
large as resulting from that condition. In the supposed case the institutions of 
the country should be such as to content all classes of the population, and their 
contentment would naturally operate as a bond of union, confidence and goodwill 
between the Porte and her grateful dependents. 

If | may be allowed, in the absence of instructions, to express a personal 
opinion, I confess that the adoption of the former principle would have been 
preferable in my judgment ; but, under the circumstances, it would have been 
useless for me to take my stand on that ground, and the only result of such an 
experiment would have been the failure of our endeavours to send a basis of 
areneenne to Paris before the commencement of the negotiations. 

felt myself, therefore, compelled to abandon the course which J should 
have preferred, and to join simply with my colleagues and the Sultan’s Ministers 
In putting forward such principles and institutional forms for each Principality 
as might hereafter secure the welfare of its inhabitants, and bind them both more 
closely to the Sultan's Empire. 

The result of our discussions, as between the three Representatives, has 
been already transmitted to your Lordship. It remains for us to obtain the 
assent of the Turkish Ministers, and to combine our ideus with theirs on the 
subject of Servia. 

Your Lordship will observe that the proposition relating to Moldavia and 
Wallachia sets aside the Organic Statutes, and grounds the future institutions 
of the two countries on a modified confirmation of their ancient privileges. It 
has the salutary effect of substituting the Sultan's charter for Russian dictation, 
and of being on that account more acceptable to the Principalities, In the 
remaining Articles we have endeavoured to combine a proper de ndence of the 
Principalities on the Porte, with securities for the maintenance their privileges, 
for the administration of justice, for the abolition of abuses, for placing all classes 
of the population on an equal footing, and, finally, for investing the Prince with 
a suitable amount of authority, 

With respect to the defences of the country, and a Peatbatare founded on 
national representation, I had not the good fortune to take the same view as my 
colleagues of France and Austria, On the former point it was my wish to 


166 


define the Sultan’s right of sending an army into the Principalities for the 
defence of the frontier, or erecting fortresses on their territory, in such manner 
as to guard, in their interest, against abuse without crippling the action of the 
Sovercien in cases of real necessity. On the second, I was desirous of obtaining 
a Representative body, capable of exercising a wholesome control over the 
Executive without opening too much room for popular excitement. 

Inclosed herewith are the conflicting paragraphs which appeared respee- 
tively in my draft of the prep siaons and in that presented by M. Thouvenel, 
and supported by Baron Prokesch. A mutual wish to agree, and want of time 
for piclonsed discussion, induced us to adopt the version as it now stands in the 
series of numbered Articles. 

On the whole, my Lord, I trust that, although my position was in almost 
every case of disagreement that of a minority, our work, however imperfect, will 
be found sufficient for the main object immediately in view. 

L have, &e. 


(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE.

M. Thouvenel’s Project, Lord Stratford's Project.

Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received February 24.) 


(No. 176.) 


My Lord, Constantinople, February 13, 1656. 


I HAVE ascertained that the Turkish version, as prepared for the Sultan's 
firman, of the concerted Articles relating to privileges and reforms, agrees 
completely with the original, in French, transmitted to your Lordship, Although 
the Sultan has given his consent, the promulgation of the firman, translation of 
which is herewith inclosed, may still be delayed for three or four days. The 
instrument itself is to be invested with the Sultan’s hatti-sherif,; and read 
solemnly at the Porte in presence of the Ministers, the grand functionaries of 
the Empire, and the heads of religious communities. It is also to be communi- 
eated to the whole Diplomatic body, as well as to those Representatives who took 
a direct part in preparing the groundwork of it. Finally, it is to be sent into 
the provinces for publication according to custom. 

I have urged the Turkish Ministers not to shrink from giving the utmost 
solemnity and publicity to this final act of reform. It has been deliberately 
adopted as indispensable for the existence of the Empire, and the ‘Turkish 
Government cannot therefore neglect its communication to the people and its 
effective enforcement without exposing themselves to the severest reproaches, 
Much will depend, however, on the zeal and spirit with which the measures in 
preparation, and, generally, the means of execution, are stimulated and directed. 
he change in prospect is as great as it is beneficial, and proportionate efforts 
are required to keep it alive, and to carry it into effect, so as to produce decisive 
and durable results. 

T have, &c, 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE,

MON présent firman Imperial est adreasé & toi, mon illustre Grand Vizir 
et mon alter ego, distingué par les belles qualites, et décoré du Noble Nishan de

Depuis le jour de mon avénement au Trone, je n'ai eu qu'une pensée, celle 
de faire, par toutes sortes de moyens, et d’une maniére compléte, le bonheur de 
toutes les classes de mes sujets, qui sont un dépot confié par le Ciel entre nos 
mains, Dieu soit loue! mes efforts Acet égard ont produit des fruits trés-utiles, 
comme ona vu, et mon Empire et ma nation font de plus en plus des progrés

plus dextension aux reformes que j'at réussi de faire jusquiici, et ecla afin de 
ne laisser rien & désirer par rapport & la dignité de ma Sublime Porte, et 
ala haute et importante position qu'elle occupe parmi les nations du monde 
social, |

lexige, toutes égales; ef les mesures: caloulées 4 faire prospérer mes Etats,

Cela étunt, fordonne, et mes ordres sont dictés par la justice méme, que lon

que les indigénes, et aprés que des arrangements auront eu lieu avec le 
Puissances étrangéres. 

Les impots sont exigibles au méme titre de tous les sujets de mon Empire, 
sans distinction de classe ni de culte. On avisera aux moyens les plus prompts 
et les plus énergiques de corriger les abus dans la perception des impdts et 
notamment des dimes, Le systéme de la perception directe sera, successivement 
et aussitdt que faire se pourra, substitué au régime des fermes dans toutes les 
branches des revenus de |’Etat. Tant que ce systéme demeurera en vigueur, 
il sera interdit, sous les peines les plus sévéres, 4 tous les agents de Vautorité et 
a tous les membres des Medjlis de se rendre adjudicataires des fermes qui 
seront annoneées avec publicité et concurrence, ou d’avoir une part quelconque 
Wintérét dans leur exploitation. Les impositions locales seront, autant que 
possible, caleulées de facon 4 ne pas affecter les sources de la production, ni 
entraver le mouvement du commerce intérieur. 

Les travaux d'utilité publique recevront une dotation convenable, 4 laquelle 
concourront les impositions particuliéres et spéciales des Provinces appelées a 
jour de l’établissement des voies de communication par terre et par mer. 

Une loi spéciale ayant déja été renduc, qui ordonne que le budget des 
reeettes et des dépenses de I’Mtat sera fixe et communiqué chaque année, cette 
loi sera ohservée de la maniére la plus scrupuleuse. On procedera a la révision 
(les traitements affectés 4 chaque emploi. 

Les chefs et un délegué de chaque communauté, désigné par ma Sublime 
Porte, seront appelés 4 prendre part aux délibérations du Conseil Supréme de 
Justice dans toutes les circonstances qui intéresseraient Ju geénéralité des sujets 
de mon Empire. Ils seront spécialement convoqués a cet effet par mon Grand 
Vizir. Le mandat des delégués sera annuel; ils préteront serment en entrant 
en charge. Tous les membres du Conseil, dans les réunions ordinaires et extra- 
ordinaires, émettront librement leurs avis et leurs votes sans qu'on puisse jamais 
les inquiéter i ce sujet, 

Les lois contre Ja corruption, la concussion ou Ja malversation seront 
appliquees, d’aprés les formes légales, 4 tous les sujets de mon Empire, quelle 
que soit leur classe et la nature de leurs fonctions. 

On s‘oceupera de la création de banques et d'autres institutions semblables 
pour arriver 4 la réforme du systéme monétaire et financier, ainsi que de la 
eréation de fonds destinés 4 augmenter les sources de la richesse matérielle de 
mon Empire. 

On s’occupera également de la création de routes ct de canaux qui rendront 
les communications i faciles et augmenteront les sources de la richesse du 
pays. On abolira tout ce que peut entraver le commerce et Vagriculture, Pour 
urriver & ces buts, on recherchera les moyens de mettre 4 profit les sciences, les 
arts et les capitaux de l'Europe et de les mettre ainsi successivement en 
execution. 

Tels étant mes volontés et mes ordres, vous qui étes mon Grand Vizir, 
vous ferez, suivant l'usage, publier, soit dans ma capitale, soit dans toutes les 
parties de mon Empire, ce firman Impérial, et vous yeillerez avec attention et 
prendrez toutes les mesures nécessaires afin que tous les ordres qu'il contient 
soient dorénavant exécutés avec lu plus rigoureuse ponctualite. 


Inclosure 2 in No. 181. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to Mr. Pisani, 


Sir, Pera, Mebruary 19, 1856, 

1 AM glad to learn from you that you remarked to Fuad Pasha, after the 
solemnity of yesterday, the detective choracter of the Sultan's hatti-shent, 
notwithstanding my previous exhortations aud his assurances, 1 have glad also 
to learn that you lost no time in reminding him of several pending questions, 
which had been reserved till after the promulgation of the firman. 1 have now 
to instruct you on both subjects. You will inform the Secretary of State that | 
was much dieappointed by the ordinary character of the huatt, whisk: as I under- 


stood from him, was to be more comprehensive, and calculated to show a deeper 
interest than usual in the enforcement of the firman. I have no pretension to 
prescribe the forms of what is an internal act of the Government, but I regret, 


177 


on so an important and vital an occasion, that anything capable of giving full 
weight and effect to the Sultan's most gracious intentions should have een 
omitted or abridged ; the more so as I had reason to believe that his Excellency 
entered into my sentiments, and felt the importance of investing the Sultan’s 
charter with all that might impress respect upon the people and confidence on 
foreign nations. What is done, is done. 1 now look to the execution of the 
measures proclaimed, and to the fulfilment of all the reforms and improvements 
so solemnly promised, It would be a great satisfaction to know in what manner 
it is proposed to carry out the firman, and for that purpose what regulations are 
to be framed, and what auxiliary steps to be taken. iatiaa th, 

With respect to the pending questions alluded to above, I earnestly solicit 
attention to the representations which you addressed to Fuad Pasha yesterday 
with much propriety. There is the Greek Convention on brigandage; the 
transfer of the Tidjaret; the improvement of the custom-house, the settlement 
of the harbour dues; the recruitment of the Contingent. There may be others, 
which do not oceur to me at this moment, of less importance indeed, but calling, 
nevertheless, for early attention. ; 

I have to desire that you will talk these several matters over with the 
Pasha, so as to ascertain his presen state of mind respecting them, and to 
enable me to put them more completely in train, oa 

I wish Ako that you would sound him as to the expediency of establishing 
a Crédit-Mobilier Bank. In the opinion of mercantile people, there are sufficient 
elements for the creation and progress of such an institution, and T am myself 
acquainted with a respectable and intelligent individual having eommand of or 
access to the necessary amount of capital who might be induced to embark in 
the enterprise and even to conduct it. 

Nothing further remains to be suggested at this moment, 


I have, &c. | 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE. 


P.S,—I wish you to read this instruction to Mehemed Pasha as well as to 
Fuad.

Inclosure 3 in No, 181, 
Mr. Pisani to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Pera, February 20, 1855, 
HAVING read and explained to Fuad Pasha and Mehemed Pasha your 
Excellency’s instruction to me of yesterday's date, respecting the firman 
containing the Articles of the fourth point, the Convention about brigandage, 
the transfer of the Tidjaret, and the improvement of the custom-house, &e., | 
have now the honour to report that both his Excellency and his Highness told 
me, that you may rest assured that the Sultan’s charter, contaming the powits 
agreed upon in the Conferences, bears the most authentic and solemn charaeter, 
invested as it is with His Imperial Majesty's hatti-sherif.. It is true, added Fuad 
Pasha, that he said to your Excelleney that the hatt was to be more 
comprehensive ; but when he said so, there was no question of a preamble 
which subsequently was thought indispensable to give the frman more weight, 
and calculated to show a deeper interest than usual. He repeated again that he 
perfectly enters into your sentiments, and because he and his colleagues felt all 
the importance of investing the Sultan’s charter with all that might impress 
respect upon the people and confidence on foreign nations as to the sincerity 
of the Porte’s Ministers, they gave such publicity and solemnity to its 
romulgation. 
bite With reference to the execution of the mensures prochiimed, both the 
Kaimakam Pasha and Fuad Pasha requested me to tell. your Excellency that all 
the measures which can be carried into effect at once will suffer no delay, ‘There 
are others which require time; for instance, the formation of tribunals ol 
commerce, police, &c. The new Penal Code has already been translated and will 
soon he printed; that of commerce is belore a Mixed Commission, as you 
Excellency is aware, and immediately the report of that Commission ts presente 
to the Porte, the necessary ineasures will be adopted. 


178 


As regards the pending questions alluded to above, his Excellency said that 
the Greek Convention on Brigandage will be communicated to his colleagues in 
the first meeting of the Ministerial Council, most likely on Saturday, or at the 
latest on Sunday. He gave orders, in my presence, to the Amedgi Effendi to 
collect all the papers connected with that matter and have them ready for 
perusal, in the Couneil, by Saturday. For the transfer of the Tidjaret he is in 
communication, since yesterday, with the Minister of Commerce. As to the 
improvement of the custom-house, he spoke already to the Minister of Finance, 
under whose control that establishment is placed, and expects his answer. 

I have sounded his Excellency as to the expedience of establishing a Crédit- 
Mobilier Bank. I found him very well disposed, and requests your 
Excellency to tell the person whom you recommend to present his plan in 
writing to the Porte; and should what he proposes be acceptable to them, they 
will talk over the matter with him in order to come to an understanding. 

I have, &e. 
(Signed) ET, PISANL.

No. 185, 
Lord Stratford de Redeliffe to the Earl of Clarendon.—(Received a 
(Telegraphic. ) Constantinople, February 28, 1896. 


THE new Imperial firman, great as are the prospects which it opens, will 
require much steady attention and vigilant energy to carty it into effect and 
ensure its full results. Its enforeements, though indispensable for the regenera- 
Hon of this Empire cannot be left with any degree of confidence to the Turkish 
Government. Responsibility towards Rurope must stimulate their sense of duty 
‘n that respect. ‘The fourth point eannot therefore with safety or prudence be 
omitted from the Treaty. The right of superintendence, which was refused to 
Russia, may be given without inconsistency to Europe, if care be taken that no 
single Power acquire & pretension to interfere alone, or on light grounds. The 
fear of a joint interference from all can hardly fail to give weight to the counsels 
of those who have fought for the Turkish Empire, and conquered its best 
securitics.

My Lord, — Constantinople, March 6, 1856. 

HAVING been told that some doubts were entertained as to the exactness 
of the Sultan's recent hatti-sherif with respect to the articles in French, originally 
agreed upon in conference, and that the printed translations in Greek and French 
were also thought to be imperfectly executed, | requested Mr. Smythe, in concert 
with Mr. Frederick Pisani, to look the whole over again with minute attention, 
and to make known to me officially the real state of the case. 

1 have now the honour to inclose Mr. Smythe’s report, and | think your 
Lordship will find reason to be satisied with the correctness in all important 
respects of the |mperial hatti-sherif.

Inclosure in No. 186. 
Mr. P. Smythe to Lord Stratford de Redeliffe. 


My Lord, Pera, March 1, 1856. 


I HAVE to report, for your Excellency’s information, that I have carefully 
compared the Turkish text of the new firman of rights and privileges with the 
original French memorandum drawn up by the three Representatives, and 
embodied in that firman, as well by myself as with the concert of Messrs. 
Frederick and Etienne Pisani. I have the satisfaction to announce to your 
Excellency that we have been unable to detect any difference amounting to 
discrepancy or material variation between these documents. Such differences 
as exist are few in number and almost entirely verbal in their character, arising 
out of the respective peculiarities of the Turkish and Irench idioms, and not 1m 
any way mainly affecting the sense. Difference in form has been to a certain 
extent unavoidable with these documents, in consequence of their not being 
precisely identical in nature. The one is a project, the other a decree, and the 
measures and resolutions which appear as simple statements in the former are 
enforced with authority in the latter, by the Sultan taking occasion to speak im 
the first person, . 

[ have, &e. 


(Signed) PERCY SMYTHE.

Inclosure in No, 187. 
Adress. 


To the Right Hon. Viscount Stratford de Redcliffe, Peer of England, Envoy 
Extraordinary and Minister Plenipotentiary of Her Britannic Majesty at 
the Sublime Porte, &e. 


My Cord, 

THE Undersigned, Protestant missionaries belonging to various Christian 
Churches and Societies of England and America, consider it their duty at the 
present important and auspicious period of this Empire, signalized by the 
publication of the Imperial hatti-sheriff of the reigning Sultan, to give utterance 
to their feelings of gratitude to. God, the Supreme Gver of every goal gift, to 
express to your Lordship their entire satisfaction with the extent and the spirit 
of that document, relative to religious freedom and the rights of conscience ; and 
to congratulate you on the honour providentially and deservedly conferred Ban 
your Lordship, of having become instramental in accomplishing so great and so 
ood a work for the millions of Turkey. While we would gratefully recognize 


181 


the valuable services rendered by the Representatives of several other countries, 
to forward this praiseworthy end, we cannot but realize that the accomplish- 
ment of this work is pre-eminently due, under God, to the influence of the 
Representative of Great Britain. 

From the beginning of the disastrous war still pending between the great 
Western Powers and Turkey on one side, and Russia on the other, we have 
looked upon each passing event with painful and prayerful interest. We have 
prayed for the maintenance and the triumph of right, and for the speedy return 
of peace ;—n peace reestablishing justice among neighbouring nations, and 
promoting truth and righteousness, aud the temporal and spiritual prosperity of 
the various classes of society and the different nationalities resident in the 
Turkish Empire. We have always believed that such would be the result, and 
this has been our comfort amid the scenes of horror which surrounded us. 

Nor has our hope been disappointed. The Imperial hatti-sheriff lately 
published, has convinced us that our fond expectations are likely to be realized. 
Turkey, snatched from the border of imminent destruction, will see a better day. 
The light will shine upon those who have long sat in darkness; and blest by 
social prosperity and religious freedom, the millions of Turkey will, we trust, be 
seen ere long sitting peacefully under their own vine and fig-tree. Your Lordship 
will allow us to say that we consider the hatti-sherif entirely satisfactory, not 
only in its social enactments, but also relative to freedom of conscience. To 
give that bolder utterance to this great principle which some seem to have 
expected, would, in our opinion, have been imprudent, and would have retarded 
the cause of truth instead of advancing it. It would have imperilled the 
reorganisation and reformation of Turkey. As it is, the prospects of this country 
appear to us bright. The Imperial document will only need a consistent and 
disereet application when called for, and the world will soon perceive the 
importance of the Imperial act, We would Braretlly acknowledge the kindness 
of the Sovereign of this country, and the wise and liberal moderation of his 
Government. We see no reason to entertain any doubt of their sincerity and 
loyalty in the promulgation of the Imperial edict, or of their intention to give it 
effect throughout the land, But we cannot help doing justice to the fmendly 
agencies from more enlightened countries, which have led them to take so 
elevated and enlightened a stand in the future government of this country; and 
among these agencies your Lordship will permit us to consider you foremost, 
as the Representative of the most liberal Protestant country of Europe. It is 
highly gratifying to us to give utterance to this just sentiment. 

é cannot, however, close this inadequate expression of our views and 
feelings on the subject, without alluding to the necessity of the continued 
experienced counsel, and the friendly encouragement and assistance too, which 
the enlightened Western Powers, and especially England, will have to afford to 
the Government of Turkey in introducing and supporting these principles, which 
are so far beyond the conceptions of an ignorant and fanatical population. The 
temptation of yielding to circumstances, and of sacrificing the principles of 
justice and truth to popular prejudice, will be great and constant, The very 
novelty of the moral principles now to be introduced into the administration of 
the spiritual interests of society, as well as the depth and extent of their bearings, 
will, for some time to come, render experienced counsel and cooperation from 
abroad a weleome service, even to the most vigorous Government, in carrying 
out the intentions of the benevolent Sovereign, and in meting out equal justice 
to the various religious denominations and to individuals, without respect of 
persons or of traditionary fanaticism. 

But though we consider the problem which is now to be solved a ver 
delicate one, we would look with cheerfulness to the future, trusting in God, 
who has done already so much for Turkey, and who will doubtless carry furward 
to completeness the great work of His regeneration. 

Our devout wish and prayer in closing is, that it may please God to spare 
your Lordship yet for many years to come to this country, whose wisest 
measures have been matured for bairty yas past under your personal influence 


182 


and advice. And may the God of all grace accept and bless the labour of your 


hands, and prepare you for rest in a better world when the contest and the trials - 


of this present life shall be over, 
We have, &c. 

(Signed) W. G. SCHAUIFLER, D.D. 
W. GODELL, D.D. 
H. G. 0. DWIGHT, D.D. 
JAMES STEVENSON BLACKWOOD, LL.D. 
ELIAS RIGGS, D.D. 
HORACE M. BLAKISTON. 
R. KOENIG, 
Cc. HAMLIN, D.D. 
DUNCAN TURNER. 
HENRY A. STERN. 
JOHN BOWEN. 
J. FAYETTE PETTIBONE. 
EDWIN E. BLISS. 
GEORGE A. POLLARD, 
FILLMAN C. TROWBRIDGE. 
JOEL §. EVERETT. 
ORSON P. ALLEN. 

Pera, Constantinople, March 4, 1856.

No. 188. 
Lord Stratford de Redcliffe to the Earl of Clarendon —(Recetved March 18.) 


(No, 265.). 
My Lord, Constantinople, March 6, 1856, 


HAVING communicated, as it was clearly incumbent on me to commu- 
nicate, your Lordship’s instruction No. 190 to General Mansfield, that officer 
has written to me in explanation of the opinions from which your Lordship 
expressed therein a decided dissent. 

A copy of General Mansfield’s letter is inclosed herewith, and in forwarding 
it to your Lordship | think it my duty to state that the objections to mixing up 
‘Turks and Christians in the same companies appear in my judgment to be well 
founded, partly for the reasons stated by your Lordship, and partly in virtue of 
moral considerations. ‘The time, I conceive, will come when these objections 
may be expected to give way ; but for the present I hold them to be conclusive. 
The military view of the question 1s beyond my sphere, and | can only state 
that more than one officer with whom I have conversed on the subject has 
expressed himself as seeing no objection to putting the Christians into separate 
companies, though in the same regiment or battalion with the Ottoman 
soldiers. 

I have, &e. | 
(Signed) STRATFORD DE REDCLIFFE, 


———— 


Inclosure in No. 188. 
Brigadier-General Mansfield to Lord Stratford de Redcliffe. 


My Lord, Pera, March 2, 1856. 

IN Lord Clarendon’s despatch No. 194 of the 18th ultimo, his Lordship is 
pleased to express his opinion that the views entertained by me regarding the 
mode of forming the Christian recruits in the Turkish army are unsound, 
Perhaps it may not appear irrelevant to your Excellency that | should state 
more fully than I have as yet had an opportunity of doing the grounds of my 
ideas on this subject, and how they may be worked out. 

I venture to submit to your Excellency the following remarks. Her 
Majesty's Government are of opinion that the Christian recruits should be 
formed in separate regiments, Yo Excellency would prefer, 1 believe, that 


aha 


183 


they should constitute distinct eompanies in otherwise Mussulman regiments, 
and J, in the third place, would rather advocate a thorough fusion of the various 
religious sects, both in companies and battalions. 

The present object of legislation in Turkey, and of the counsel of the allied 
Powers, is to produce an absolute fusion of the various religious sects and races 
composing the population of this Empire. We shall doubtless soon see Stamboul 
colonized with Christians, and in all parts of the country the action of commerce 
and the new laws for the tenure of land will bring the rivals into close contact. 

In time, the prejudices and differences which have hitherto been at the 
bottom of the hostility between them will be removed, according to all human 
probability. This change is already largely in course in places where there is 
much development of commerce, and if we carefully consider the social results 
of the numerous projects, commercial and agricultural, which await but the 
termination of the war to be carried into execution, we cannot but expect to see 
the same change in the remotest districts. 

Turkish Ministers with whom I have come into contact dwell complacently 
on the evidence given of this change in the places which have been more particus 
larly affected by the presence of the Ruropean armies. Such I take to be the 
aim of your Excellency’s policy, which has been so steadily pursued during a 
long career, and has been, as far as legislation can ensure it, finally crowned with 
success in the late sweeping hatti-sherif. 

I would submit with the greatest respect that if the new formation of the 
army is to be based on a system embodying separate regiments of the different 
sects, we shall be liable to the charge of proceeding on a different principle in 
that quarter from the one to which we are endeavouring to give form and 
vitality among the population at large. 

My experience of Turkey is but short, but I think that I know enough of 
it to say that in no country in the world is the science and practice of intrigue 
carried further. Iam therefore led to the irresistible conclusion that the head- 
quarters of every Christian regiment would be the centre of a Russo-Greek 
conspiracy against the authority of the Sultan; a conspitacy not to bear imme- 
diate fruit, but one which, in the course of a quarter of a century, will robably 
undo what has been effected by the present war, or, at all events, infallibly 
produce the most extraordinary and dangerous convulsions. The powerful lever 
of “esprit de corps” will operate, in common with spiritual conviction, with 
the memories of former oppression, and with Russian diplomacy, to build up an 
‘Gmperium in imperio” more fatal than that of the Janissaries, because the 
latter, though tyrannical as the Preetorian Guard, were at all events stedfast in 
hatred to an enemy beyond the frontier. 


At the present moment it may appear difficult to call a Christian army into 
life; but we must carefully guard against what the creature may do hereafter, 
when it shall have become adult and as strong or stronger than its parent. 
Your Excellency’s idea tht the religious sects should exist in the same battalion, 
but in separate companies, would appear to be founded on the same grounds ag 
those which have occurred tome; your Excellency being averse to 4 more 
complete fusion on moral considerations. 

On that point | do not presume to give an opinion, my knowledge of the 
people being so limited and superticial. But T will venture to state the military 
objection to the idea, although I admit 1 to be by no means insuperable. 

All regimental Commanders are aware that, independent of the feeling 
called ‘‘ esprit de corps,” which animates a whole regiment, there is another, 
which uy be called ‘esprit de compagnie,’ which is almost as strong as the 
former, In battalions of men owning a like creed, and sprung from the same 
race, a judicious Commander makes great use of the “esprit de compagnie,” 
by causing a wholesome emulation between the various companies to operate 
fayourably towards the good conduct of the whole battalion, 

But no British Commander would dare to have in the same regiment 
Roman Catholic companies, Protestant companies, Seotch companies, [rish 
companies, English companies. “T'o preserve the peace of the whole, he is 
compelled to fuse the dillerent elements in euch company, in order that the 
military ‘‘ esprit de corps” and “de compagnie” may overcome all the prejudices 
and affections in the soldier which exist in the recruit. 

[ would ask if what is necessary in one our regiments would not be equally 
so in one composed of Turks, Grecks, Armenians, Bosnians, and Albanians. Tho 


184 


point taken by Her Majesty’s Government against the absolute fusion in one 


attalion is, that great oppression would be practised towards the Christian i 
soldiery ; that there is no analogy between the Turkish service here and our tie 
Indian services, as no Turkish Commander could be brought to moderate justly k 


between Turks on the one hand and Christians on the other. 

In answer to this, | would submit that these are the difficulties of organisa- 
tion which may be overcome by careful system, and strict attention to, and 
supervision of, the working of individuals under it; that if the system succeed, 
the Government will contribute in no inconsiderable manner to that gradual 
reciprocal education of the rival races which is being initiated by commerce, and 
that whatever ‘difficulty there may be will occur at the outset of the experiment 
while men are excited by the prospect of change; whereas in the other case, the 3 
danger and consequent difficulties may be trifling at first, but with every succeed- | | 
d ing year will become larger and larger. ib? 
‘t I cannot but think that what has been done in the matter of army ba 
organisation in this country since the commencement of the reforms of the f 
late Sultan, is sufficient earnest that in this respect we have nothing to fear 
eventually. 

It is a fact that until the establishment fainted, owing to the inanition of 
the Treasury, there was no army in Europe better cared for in all essential ie 
matters than the ‘Turkish army. Let us hear in mind how short a time was t 
y necessary, not only to put the system in train, but to produce such results as 
BS those we have seen exhibited in the most trying circumstances during the last 
ql three years. Let us then consider the consequence with which the Christian

ma races now demand and enforce justice to themselves, how their rights will be 
ind more zealously guarded than ever by the other Powers. Let us reflect on the 
ea: circumstances of the half of one thousand armed soldiers being Christians, and 
ait we shall hardly conceive that these latter will submit to unfair treatment, or that | 
a a Turk in command will dare to inflict it. | 


But, as said before, careful system and careful supervision of the system 
will be necessary to curb popular passion and to check the crimes and vices of 
individuals, whether among the commanders or the commanded, But is not 
this the task of military discipline ? ; 

It appears to me, but I write with real diffidence, that a board of inspection 
should be formed consisting of a ‘'urkish Mushir and an European officer, whose 
business should be to inspect together, or to direct the inspection of all mixed 
regiments. ‘This inspection should include not only the ordinary details of 
military life, but all the nice matters which would arise under the new formation, 
After a time, a Christian officer, who had risen in the force to the rank of 
General would naturally be associated with the Turk, but until that could 
happen, no better plan could be followed than to invest some British officer 
with the necessary Turkish rank, and place him in the position indicated. ‘This 
would, perhaps, when it came to be known, have a better effect in producing 
confidence than any other which could be devised; while it would not be 
inconducive to British influence for the general benetit of Turkey, I believe 
from the information I have been able to gather, which is naturally most 
imperfect, that the idea of military service is by no means welcome to the 
majority of the Christian population; and that the latter would almost be 
content rather to submit to the payment of the haratch than to undergo military 
duty. 

Perhaps it might appear advisable to your Excellency to consider the French 
plan, by which the payment by the conscript of a certain sum to Government is 
allowed to stand in lieu of personal service. 

The adoption of such a plan would, jdoubtless, be very agreeable to the 
population of the towns, and more particularly to the Greeks, while the real 
conscription, for military purposes, would still fall, as it now does, on the rural 
districts, 

The additional item of revenue would, in such case, not be contemptible. 

I must apologize for having troubled your Excellency at such length, but 
as Lord Clarendon did me the honour to notice what was advanced in my 
reports to you, [ have thought it my duty to lay before your Excellency what 
has been the result of much consideration on these matters. 

T have, &e. 
(Signed) W. R. MANSFIELD.
